Phase 1 · Days 6–55 · Lessons 6–55

Java Language Core

Phase 1 teaches the Java language itself, from how a source file becomes bytecode to types, operators, strings, control flow, arrays, methods, classes, interfaces, records, enums, sealed types and exceptions, with the reasons behind each rule rather than just the syntax. It builds on the tools set up in Phase 0 and ends with a milestone project, a console Library Management System written in plain Java, which is the foundation that collections and generics in Phase 2, and later Spring itself, assume.

By the end of this phase you can

  • Compile and run multi-package programs from the command line, build a runnable JAR, and diagnose classpath errors and stack traces without an IDE.
  • Predict the result of any expression involving primitive types, overflow, floating point, casts, promotion, boxing and operator precedence, and use BigDecimal correctly for money.
  • Work with strings correctly: explain the string pool and == versus equals, handle Unicode code points, and build text efficiently with StringBuilder, formatting and text blocks.
  • Write control flow with switch expressions and pattern matching, process arrays, and design methods with clear parameters, overloading, varargs and recursion.
  • Design classes with constructors, encapsulation and immutability; explain initialization order, inheritance, dynamic dispatch, abstract classes, interfaces and composition over inheritance.
  • Implement equals, hashCode, toString and Comparable correctly, and choose between enums, records, sealed hierarchies and nested classes.
  • Design an exception strategy with checked and unchecked exceptions and try-with-resources, and deliver the phase milestone, a console Library Management System built with core Java only.
  • 50 lessons
  • 33h 50m video
  • 36h 15m practice
  • Days 6–55
L6 · DAY 6

Your First Java Program & The Compilation Pipeline

35m video40m practice

Java runs in two stages: javac checks your source and translates it into portable bytecode once, and the java launcher starts a JVM that loads, verifies and executes that bytecode every time the program runs. Most beginner confusion, from "why static?" to reading a stack trace, clears up once you know which stage you are in.

HelloWorld, line by line

/**
 * Prints a greeting. A Javadoc comment: the javadoc tool turns it into HTML.
 */
public class HelloWorld {                      // a public class must live in HelloWorld.java
    public static void main(String[] args) {  // the entry point the launcher looks for
        /* A block comment
           can span several lines. */
        System.out.println("Hello, World!");   // a statement; ends with a semicolon
    }
}
  • public class HelloWorld: all Java code lives inside a type. A file may hold one top-level public class, and the file must be named after it, case included.
  • Braces delimit the class body and the method body. Whitespace and line breaks are insignificant to the compiler.
  • System.out.println(...): a method call; the statement ends in ;.

Anatomy of main, and why each keyword is there

PartWhy it is required
publicThe launcher calls the method from outside your class, so it must be accessible.
staticNo object of your class exists when the program starts; a static method belongs to the class and can be called without one.
voidThe launcher ignores a return value. A process exit code is set with System.exit(int); returning normally means exit code 0.
mainThe name the launcher looks up. Other methods called main (overloads) are legal but are not entry points.
String[] argsThe command-line arguments: java HelloWorld a b gives args = {"a", "b"}. String... args is equivalent.
Java 25 note

JEP 512 (final in Java 25, after previews from Java 21) relaxes these rules: main may be an instance method with no parameters, and a "compact source file" may omit the class declaration entirely, so void main() { IO.println("Hello"); } is a complete program. The launcher then creates an instance and calls it. This course uses the classic form, because every existing code base and framework does, and because it compiles on Java 21 without preview flags.

Source to bytecode to execution

javac (itself a Java program, the jdk.compiler module) parses the source into a syntax tree, resolves every name, type-checks every expression, runs flow analysis (is every variable assigned before use? is every statement reachable?), removes syntactic sugar, and writes one .class file per class. A class file starts with the bytes CA FE BA BE, then the class-file version (65 for Java 21), a constant pool of names and literals, and the bytecode of each method. javap -c HelloWorld shows it:

public static void main(java.lang.String[]);
  Code:
     0: getstatic     #7   // Field java/lang/System.out:Ljava/io/PrintStream;
     3: ldc           #13  // String Hello, World!
     5: invokevirtual #15  // Method java/io/PrintStream.println:(Ljava/lang/String;)V
     8: return

(The # numbers are constant-pool indexes and vary between compilers.) java, the launcher, creates a JVM, asks the application class loader to find HelloWorld.class on the classpath (L7), then links it (the verifier proves the bytecode cannot corrupt memory), initializes it (runs static initializers), and invokes main on a thread named main. The JVM starts by interpreting bytecode; methods that run often are compiled to native code by the JIT compilers (C1, then C2). The process ends when every non-daemon thread has finished, or on System.exit.

What System.out.println actually is

Definition

System is a final class in java.lang, which every file imports implicitly. out is a public static final field of type java.io.PrintStream, connected to the process's standard output when the JVM starts. println is an instance method on that PrintStream, overloaded for every primitive type, char[], String and Object (which calls String.valueOf, hence toString()); it writes the text followed by the platform line separator. System.err is a second stream for errors, which is why redirection can split them (L5). In server code you will replace both with a logging framework (Phase 13).

Comments, naming and style

Three comment forms: // line, /* block */ (cannot be nested), and /** Javadoc */ placed before a class, method or field and processed by the javadoc tool, with tags such as @param, @return and @throws. (Java 23 added Markdown doc comments written with ///.) Comment why, not what; the code already says what.

KindConventionExample
Class, interface, record, enumUpperCamelCase nounLibraryCatalog
MethodlowerCamelCase verbcheckOut, isOverdue
Variable, parameterlowerCamelCasedueDate
ConstantUPPER_SNAKE_CASEMAX_LOANS
Packagelowercase, reversed domaincom.example.library

For layout, adopt a published style rather than inventing one: the Google Java Style Guide (2-space indent, 100 columns) or IntelliJ's defaults (4 spaces). What matters is that a formatter enforces it; in Phase 7 you will wire one into the build.

Your first compile error and your first stack trace

Compile errors come from javac and name a file, a line, a message, and a caret under the problem:

HelloWorld.java:8: error: ';' expected
        System.out.println("Hello, World!")
                                           ^
1 error

HelloWorld.java:8: error: package system does not exist
        system.out.println("Hello, World!");
              ^

The second message surprises everyone: with a lowercase system, the compiler reads system.out as a class out in a package system. Fix the first error first; later ones are often consequences of it. Runtime errors come from the JVM as an exception with a stack trace:

public class Divide {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int total = Integer.parseInt(args[0]);
        System.out.println(average(total, 0));
    }
    static int average(int total, int count) {
        return total / count;
    }
}
$ javac Divide.java && java Divide 10
Exception in thread "main" java.lang.ArithmeticException: / by zero
	at Divide.average(Divide.java:7)
	at Divide.main(Divide.java:4)

Read it top down: the thread, the exception type, the message, then frames from the innermost call outward. The first frame is where the exception was thrown; the first frame in your code is usually where the bug is. Run java Divide with no argument and you get ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException: Index 0 out of bounds for length 0 at line 3 instead. Longer traces have Caused by: sections; the last one is the root cause.

Interview trap
  • "Can main be overloaded?" Yes, but only main(String[]) (in Java 21 form) is the entry point.
  • "What if main is not static or not public?" It compiles, but on Java 21 the launcher refuses to start it and asks for public static void main(String[] args). Java 25 accepts an instance main.
  • "Is Java compiled or interpreted?" Both: compiled to bytecode ahead of time, then interpreted and JIT-compiled to native code at run time.
  • "Why can't the file be named hello.java?" A public top-level class must be in a file with exactly its name; on case-insensitive file systems the mismatch can even work locally and fail in CI on Linux.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Type HelloWorld, compile it with javac and run it with java HelloWorld from the terminal, not the IDE. Make it print args.length and each argument.
  2. Run javap -c HelloWorld, and look at the first 8 bytes of the class file (xxd HelloWorld.class | head -1 or IntelliJ's hex view). Find cafe babe and the version byte 0x41.
  3. Introduce five different compile errors one at a time (missing semicolon, lowercase system, wrong file name, missing brace, println misspelt) and record each message in your journal.
  4. Run Divide with no argument, with abc, and with 10. Name the exception and the throwing line each time.
  5. Add Javadoc to average with @param and @return, then run javadoc -d docs Divide.java and open the HTML.

Read JLS (Java SE 21) §3.7 "Comments" and ch. 12 "Execution" (§12.1 on start-up) · JVMS ch. 4 "The class File Format" · JEP 512, "Compact Source Files and Instance Main Methods" · Google Java Style Guide · Javadoc: java.io.PrintStream

L7 · DAY 7

javac, java, Classpath, JAR Basics & JShell

40m video40m practice

The JVM finds a class by turning its fully qualified name into a file path and looking for that path under each classpath entry in turn. Every "could not find or load main class", every NoClassDefFoundError and most "works in the IDE, fails on the server" bugs are that lookup failing; Maven and Gradle only automate it.

Packages on disk and in source

A package declaration puts a class in a namespace, and by convention the source tree mirrors it. The compiled tree must mirror it: the class com.example.library.model.Book has to be at com/example/library/model/Book.class below some classpath root.

library/
  src/com/example/library/App.java
  src/com/example/library/model/Book.java
// src/com/example/library/model/Book.java
package com.example.library.model;

public record Book(String isbn, String title) {}
// src/com/example/library/App.java
package com.example.library;

import com.example.library.model.Book;

public class App {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var book = new Book("978-0134685991", "Effective Java");
        System.out.println(book);   // Book[isbn=978-0134685991, title=Effective Java]
    }
}

Compiling many files, -d, and running with -cp

javac -d out src/com/example/library/App.java src/com/example/library/model/Book.java
javac -d out --source-path src src/com/example/library/App.java   # javac finds Book.java itself
find src -name "*.java" > sources.txt && javac -d out @sources.txt   # argument file

out/com/example/library/App.class          # -d creates the package folders
out/com/example/library/model/Book.class

java -cp out com.example.library.App       # the fully qualified name, not a path, no ".class"

Without -d, javac writes each .class next to its source, mixing source and output; always use it. With --source-path, javac compiles any referenced class it can find as source, so naming the entry file is enough.

Definition · classpath

The classpath is an ordered list of directories and JAR files that javac and the JVM's application class loader search for classes. Set it with -cp (also -classpath, --class-path); entries are separated by : on macOS/Linux and ; on Windows; lib/* means every JAR in lib (quote it so the shell does not expand it). If you give no -cp and no CLASSPATH environment variable, the classpath is the current directory. Giving -cp replaces that default; . is no longer included unless you list it.

The usual ways it breaks: running from the wrong directory; pointing -cp at out/com instead of the root out; typing a path (out/com/example/library/App) instead of a class name; or a dependency JAR present at compile time and missing at run time. Since JDK 11 the launcher reports the first case as Error: Could not find or load main class com.example.library.App followed by Caused by: java.lang.ClassNotFoundException.

Runnable JARs and the manifest

A JAR is a zip file with an optional META-INF/MANIFEST.MF. The manifest's Main-Class attribute is what lets java -jar start it.

jar cfe library.jar com.example.library.App -C out .   # c=create f=file e=entry point; -C = cd into out
jar tf library.jar                                     # list contents
java -jar library.jar

# META-INF/MANIFEST.MF generated by jar:
Manifest-Version: 1.0
Main-Class: com.example.library.App
Created-By: 21.0.x (Eclipse Adoptium)

With -jar, the JAR is the classpath: any -cp option and the CLASSPATH variable are ignored. Dependencies must either be listed in a Class-Path: manifest attribute (space-separated paths relative to the JAR) or packed inside it, which is what Spring Boot's "fat JAR" does with its own launcher (Phase 13). If you write a manifest by hand, end it with a newline; the last line is silently dropped otherwise.

Single-file source programs and JShell

Since JDK 11 (JEP 330), java Hello.java compiles the file in memory and runs it, writing no .class files; the first top-level class must contain main. JDK 22 (JEP 458) extended this to programs whose classes are spread across several source files in the same directory tree. It is ideal for scripts and experiments, and is how many snippets in this course are meant to be run. On Unix a file without the .java suffix can even start with #!/usr/bin/env -S java --source 21 and be executed directly.

JShell (JDK 9, JEP 222) is a read-eval-print loop: type an expression or statement and see the result immediately, with no class or main.

$ jshell --class-path out
jshell> int x = 5 * 7
x ==> 35
jshell> Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1
$2 ==> -2147483648
jshell> import com.example.library.model.Book
jshell> new Book("1", "Dune").title()
$4 ==> "Dune"
jshell> /vars           (also /methods, /imports, /list, /save file, /open file, /reset, /exit)

Semicolons are optional for single expressions, Tab completes names and shows method signatures, and common packages such as java.util and java.util.stream are pre-imported. Use it to check an API or an operator's behaviour in seconds rather than wondering.

javap: a first look inside a class file

$ javap -cp out com.example.library.App
Compiled from "App.java"
public class com.example.library.App {
  public com.example.library.App();
  public static void main(java.lang.String[]);
}

Notice the constructor you never wrote: the compiler added a default constructor (L31). Run javap on Book and you will see the canonical constructor, the accessors isbn() and title(), and the equals, hashCode and toString that the compiler generated for the record. Useful flags: -c (bytecode), -p (include private members), -v (everything, including the constant pool and major version: 65). javap settles arguments about what the compiler really does, from string concatenation (L15) to autoboxing (L14).

What breaks in production
  • NoClassDefFoundError after a clean compile. The class was on the compile classpath but not the run-time one: an unpackaged dependency, or a thin JAR run with java -jar. The variant NoClassDefFoundError: Could not initialize class X means something else: X's static initializer threw earlier.
  • JAR hell. Two versions of one library on the classpath; the first wins, and code compiled against the other fails with NoSuchMethodError. Build tools' dependency trees (Phase 7) exist to find this.
  • Separator mix-ups. A script with : separators run on Windows sees one long, nonexistent path.
  • A global CLASSPATH variable set years ago by an installer changes behaviour on one machine only. Leave it unset.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Create the two-file project above outside the IDE. Compile with -d out, run with -cp out, then reproduce three classpath failures on purpose (wrong directory, -cp out/com, a path instead of a class name) and record the messages.
  2. Package library.jar with jar cfe, unzip it with any zip tool, and read the manifest. Run it from a different directory.
  3. On JDK 22+, run java src/com/example/library/App.java and let the multi-file launcher find Book.java by itself; on JDK 21, put both types in one file and run that.
  4. In JShell, evaluate 10 / 3, 10 / 3.0, 'a' + 1 and "a" + 1, and predict each result before pressing Enter.
  5. Run javap -p -cp out com.example.library.model.Book and list every member the compiler generated.

Read JDK 21 Tool Specifications: javac, java, jar, jshell, javap · JLS §7.2 "Host Support for Packages" · JEP 330, "Launch Single-File Source-Code Programs" · JEP 458, "Launch Multi-File Source-Code Programs" · JEP 222, "jshell" · The Java Tutorials, "Packaging Programs in JAR Files"

L8 · DAY 8

Variables, Identifiers & Primitive Types

35m video40m practice

A variable is a named, typed slot whose type is fixed at compile time. Java has exactly eight primitive types with sizes fixed by the language on every platform; everything else is a reference to an object. Where a slot lives and whether it has a default value depends on whether it is a local variable or a field.

Declaration, initialization and the eight primitives

A declaration introduces a name and a type (int count;); an initialization gives it its first value (count = 0;); usually you do both at once (int count = 0;). Several variables of one type can share a declaration (int x = 1, y = 2;), though one per line reads better.

TypeSizeRangeField default
byte8 bits, signed−128 to 1270
short16 bits, signed−32,768 to 32,7670
int32 bits, signed−2,147,483,648 to 2,147,483,6470
long64 bits, signed−9,223,372,036,854,775,808 to 9,223,372,036,854,775,8070L
float32 bits, IEEE 754up to about ±3.4 × 1038, roughly 7 significant decimal digits0.0f
double64 bits, IEEE 754up to about ±1.8 × 10308, roughly 15–16 significant digits0.0
char16 bits, unsigned'\u0000' to '￿' (0 to 65,535)'\u0000'
booleannot specifiedtrue, falsefalse

The sizes are part of the language, not the platform: an int is 32 bits on every JVM, unlike C. The JVM Specification leaves boolean's storage open; HotSpot uses a byte per element in boolean[] arrays and an int slot for a local. Integer details are in L9, floating point in L10, char in L11.

public class Primitives {
    static int loans;          // field: default 0
    static boolean overdue;    // field: default false

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        byte level = 127;
        short year = 2_026;
        int population = 2_147_483_647;
        long worldPopulation = 8_100_000_000L;   // too big for int: needs the L suffix
        double price = 9.5;
        char grade = 'A';

        System.out.println(Byte.MIN_VALUE + ".." + Byte.MAX_VALUE);       // -128..127
        System.out.println(Integer.SIZE + " bits = " + Integer.BYTES + " bytes");
        System.out.println(Long.MAX_VALUE);                                // 9223372036854775807
        System.out.println((int) Character.MAX_VALUE);                     // 65535
        System.out.println(loans + " " + overdue);                         // 0 false
        System.out.println(level + year + population + worldPopulation + price + grade);
    }
}

The last line previews L9 and L14: + evaluates left to right, so level + year + population is computed in int and silently overflows before worldPopulation widens the sum to long; then price makes it a double and 'A' is added as the number 65.

Where primitives live: the stack, and the myth

Definition · where values live

Each method call gets a stack frame on its thread's stack holding its local variables and parameters (JVMS §2.6; long and double take two slots). A local of primitive type stores the value itself in the frame; a local of reference type stores a reference, while the object it refers to lives on the heap. A field lives wherever its object lives, so a primitive field is on the heap. "Primitives go on the stack" is true only of primitive locals, and even then the JIT may keep them purely in CPU registers.

Identifiers and reserved words

An identifier is a sequence of Unicode letters, digits, _ and $ that does not start with a digit, is not a keyword, and is not one of the literals true, false, null. Identifiers are case-sensitive and have no length limit. total2, _cache and größe are legal; 2total, class and my-var are not. A lone _ has been a keyword since Java 9 and, since Java 22, marks an unnamed variable. Reserved keywords include the type names (int, boolean…), modifiers (public, static, final…), control flow (if, for, switch…), and two that do nothing at all: const and goto. Words such as var, record, yield, sealed and permits are contextual: special only in certain positions, so old code using record as a variable name still compiles. $ is legal but reserved by convention for generated code.

Naming conventions from L6 apply: camelCase for variables and methods (dueDate, maxLoans), UPPER_SNAKE_CASE for constants (MAX_LOANS). Prefer full words; daysOverdue beats d everywhere except a three-line loop.

final variables and constants

A final variable can be assigned exactly once. For a local, the assignment can come later (final int fee; if (late) fee = 5; else fee = 0;, a "blank final"), and the compiler checks that it happens exactly once on every path. A constant is a static final field of primitive or String type initialized with a compile-time constant expression: static final int MAX_LOANS = 5;. final on a reference fixes the reference, not the object: final List<Book> books can still have books added (L32 covers immutability).

Defaults, definite assignment and var

Fields and array elements start at a default (0, false, '\u0000', null) because the JVM zeroes memory when it allocates an object. Local variables get no default; instead the compiler proves you assign one before every read. This is definite assignment (JLS ch. 16), and it is a flow analysis over constant conditions only:

static int discount(boolean member, int amount) {
    int percent;
    if (member) {
        percent = 10;
    }
    // return amount * percent / 100;   // error: variable percent might not have been initialized
    if (!member) {
        percent = 0;                    // the compiler does not reason that member and !member cover every case
    }
    // reading percent here would still be an error; the fix is one if/else or a switch expression
    return member ? amount * 10 / 100 : 0;
}

var (Java 10) lets the compiler infer a local variable's type from its initializer: var total = 0L; is a long, var books = new ArrayList<Book>(); is an ArrayList<Book>. The type is still fixed at compile time; Java does not become dynamically typed. It works only for locals with an initializer (and loop variables), never for fields, parameters or return types, and not with null or a bare array initializer like {1, 2}. You will see when it helps and when it hurts in L29.

Interview trap
  • "Primitives are stored on the stack." Only primitive locals. Primitive fields are inside their objects on the heap, and a static field lives with its class.
  • "What is the size of a boolean?" The language does not say. Answering "1 bit" is wrong for any real JVM.
  • "What is the default value of a local int?" There is none: reading it before assignment is a compile error, not 0.
  • Changing a public constant. Compile-time constants are copied into every class that uses them. Change MAX_LOANS in a library JAR without recompiling its callers and they keep the old value.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Write a program that prints MIN_VALUE, MAX_VALUE and SIZE for every wrapper class (Byte … Double, Character) and check the table above.
  2. Declare a local int without assigning it and read it; then a field. Explain both outcomes. Try final with an assignment in only one branch of an if.
  3. In JShell, test whether int $x, int _x, int _, int var = 1 and int record = 1 compile, and explain each result.
  4. Rewrite Primitives using var wherever legal, then hover each variable in IntelliJ to see the inferred type. Which one changes type and why (hint: level and year)?

Read JLS (Java SE 21) §3.8 "Identifiers", §3.9 "Keywords", §4.2 "Primitive Types and Values", §4.12.4 "final Variables", §4.12.5 "Initial Values of Variables", ch. 16 "Definite Assignment" · JVMS §2.6 "Frames" · JEP 286, "Local-Variable Type Inference" · Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Item 57

L9 · DAY 9

Integer Types, Literals, Overflow & Bitwise Operations

45m video50m practice

Java integers are fixed-width two's-complement numbers, so arithmetic on them is arithmetic modulo 232 or 264: exceeding the range wraps around silently instead of failing. Knowing the bit pattern explains overflow, negative hex literals, the three shift operators and every bitmask trick in the JDK.

Two's complement

Definition · two's complement

In an n-bit two's-complement integer, bit n−1 has weight −2n−1 and every other bit i has weight +2i. So the range is −2n−1 to 2n−1−1, zero has a single representation, and negating a number means invert every bit, then add 1. Addition and subtraction use the same circuit for signed and unsigned values; the result is simply kept modulo 2n.

The four integer types and their literals

byte (8 bits, −128 to 127), short (16 bits, −32,768 to 32,767), int (32 bits, about ±2.1 billion) and long (64 bits, about ±9.2 × 1018). Use int by default, long for anything that can pass 2 billion (IDs, epoch milliseconds, byte counts, money in cents at scale), and byte mainly for binary data.

  • Bases: decimal 42, hexadecimal 0x2A, octal 052 (any leading zero), binary 0b101010 (Java 7). All four are the same value.
  • Underscores group digits: 1_000_000, 0xFF_EC_DE_5E, 0b1010_0001. Only between digits: not first, last, next to the L, or right after 0x.
  • The L suffix: an integer literal is an int unless it ends in L. long big = 3_000_000_000; fails with "integer number too large". Always use uppercase L; l looks like 1.
  • Hex and binary literals can be negative: 0xFFFFFFFF is a legal int equal to −1, because the literal names a bit pattern.

Silent overflow, and how to make it loud

public class Ints {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int max = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
        System.out.println(max + 1);                      // -2147483648: wraps silently
        System.out.println(Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE));  // -2147483648: has no positive twin
        long microsPerDay = 24 * 60 * 60 * 1000 * 1000;   // computed in int, THEN widened
        System.out.println(microsPerDay);                 // 500654080
        long fixed = 24L * 60 * 60 * 1000 * 1000;         // long from the first operand on
        System.out.println(fixed);                        // 86400000000
        System.out.println(0x2A + " " + 052 + " " + 0b10_1010);   // 42 42 42
        System.out.println(Integer.toBinaryString(-5));   // 11111111111111111111111111111011
        try {
            int sum = Math.addExact(max, 1);
            System.out.println(sum);
        } catch (ArithmeticException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());           // integer overflow
        }
    }
}

The JLS defines integer overflow as wrap-around, not an error (§15.18.2), so no exception is thrown and nothing warns you. microsPerDay is the classic: every operand is an int, so the multiplication happens in 32 bits and wraps to 500,654,080 before the assignment widens it. The only exceptions integer arithmetic can throw are for division and remainder by zero (ArithmeticException: / by zero). When overflow would be a bug, use Math.addExact, subtractExact, multiplyExact, incrementExact, negateExact and toIntExact, which throw ArithmeticException instead of wrapping.

Bitwise operators and shifts

OperatorMeaningExample on int
&AND: 1 where both are 10b1100 & 0b1010 = 0b1000
|OR: 1 where either is 10b1100 | 0b1010 = 0b1110
^XOR: 1 where they differ0b1100 ^ 0b1010 = 0b0110
~NOT: invert all bits~5 = −6 (since ~x == -x - 1)
<<shift left, fill with 03 << 4 = 48
>>arithmetic right shift: copies the sign bit-17 >> 1 = −9
>>>logical right shift: fills with 0-17 >>> 28 = 15

Two rules catch people. First, byte, short and char operands are promoted to int before any bitwise or shift operation, so (byte) -1 >>> 4 is 268,435,455, not 15. Second, only the low 5 bits of the shift distance are used for int (6 for long): 1 << 32 is 1 and 1 << 33 is 2.

Practical uses

static final int READ = 1, WRITE = 1 << 1, EXEC = 1 << 2;   // one bit per flag

static void flags() {
    int perms = READ | WRITE;                 // set two flags: 0b011
    boolean canWrite = (perms & WRITE) != 0;  // test
    perms &= ~WRITE;                          // clear
    perms ^= EXEC;                            // toggle
    System.out.println(canWrite + " " + Integer.toBinaryString(perms)
            + " " + Integer.bitCount(perms));  // true 101 2
}
  • Flags and masks: pack booleans into one int, as Unix permissions and network protocols do. In ordinary Java code prefer EnumSet, which is a bit vector inside but type-safe (Effective Java Item 36).
  • Unsigned bytes: bytes read from a network or file are signed; b & 0xFF gives the 0–255 value. Integer.toUnsignedString, compareUnsigned and divideUnsigned cover unsigned ints.
  • Hashing: HashMap mixes a key's hash with h ^ (h >>> 16) so high bits influence the bucket, then picks the bucket with (n - 1) & hash, which equals Math.floorMod(hash, n) when n is a power of two (Phase 2).
  • Fast multiply and divide: x << 3 equals x * 8 (overflow included), and x >> 1 is floor division by 2, which differs from x / 2 for negative odd x (−9 versus −8). The JIT already makes these substitutions, so write * 8 for readability. One shift is worth knowing: (low + high) >>> 1 is a midpoint that cannot overflow, unlike (low + high) / 2.
  • Bit tricks: (x & 1) == 0 tests even; x > 0 && (x & (x - 1)) == 0 tests power of two; Integer.numberOfTrailingZeros, highestOneBit and bitCount map to single CPU instructions on most hardware.
What breaks in production
  • Negative array index from a hash. Math.abs(key.hashCode()) % n is negative when the hash is Integer.MIN_VALUE. Use Math.floorMod(hash, n).
  • Overflowing midpoint. (low + high) / 2 in a binary search went unnoticed in the JDK's own Arrays.binarySearch for about nine years, until arrays got big enough (Bloch, 2006).
  • Totals in int. Summing order amounts in cents or file sizes in bytes into an int works in testing and wraps in production. Use long and Math.addExact where wrapping would be wrong.
  • Leading zeros. int branch = 0110; is 72. Parsing user input with Integer.parseInt("0110") gives 110, so literals and parsed values disagree.
  • Epoch seconds in an int overflow in January 2038. Keep timestamps in long or java.time.Instant.

Practice · 50 min

  1. By hand, write −12 and −100 as 8-bit two's complement, then check with Integer.toBinaryString(-12 & 0xFF).
  2. Find the smallest positive n for which n * n overflows an int by loop, then compute it directly from Math.sqrt(Integer.MAX_VALUE). Repeat with Math.multiplyExact and catch the exception.
  3. Implement a Permissions class with grant, revoke, has and toString using an int bitmask; then rewrite it with EnumSet and compare the code.
  4. Predict, then verify in JShell: -1 >> 31, -1 >>> 31, (byte) 200, (byte) 200 & 0xFF, 1L << 63, ~0, -7 / 2, -7 >> 1, Math.floorMod(-7, 3).

Read JLS (Java SE 21) §3.10.1 "Integer Literals", §4.2.1–4.2.2, §15.18.2, §15.19 "Shift Operators", §15.22.1 · Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Item 36 · Bloch & Gafter, Java Puzzlers, Puzzle 3 "Long Division" · Joshua Bloch, "Nearly All Binary Searches and Mergesorts are Broken" (Google Research blog, 2006) · Henry S. Warren, Hacker's Delight, ch. 2 · Javadoc: java.lang.Math, java.lang.Integer

L10 · DAY 10

Floating Point, IEEE 754, BigDecimal & BigInteger

50m video50m practice

float and double are binary fractions with a fixed number of significant bits, so most decimal values, including 0.1, cannot be stored exactly and every operation rounds. That is fine for physics and statistics and wrong for money; for exact decimal arithmetic Java has BigDecimal, and for integers of any size, BigInteger.

IEEE 754 in one picture

Use double by default; float saves memory only in very large arrays (graphics, ML weights) and loses precision fast. Literals are double unless suffixed with f: float f = 3.14; does not compile. Gaps between neighbouring doubles grow with magnitude: Math.ulp(1.0) is 2−52 ≈ 2.2 × 10−16, but above 253 (about 9 × 1015) consecutive doubles are 2 apart, so 1e16 + 1 == 1e16 is true.

Why 0.1 + 0.2 != 0.3, and other precision losses

0.1 in binary is 0.000110011001100…, repeating forever, just as 1/3 repeats in decimal. The stored double is the nearest representable value, 0.1000000000000000055511151231257827021181583404541015625. 0.2 and 0.3 are also approximations, and the rounded sum of the first two lands on a different double from the third:

jshell> 0.1 + 0.2                    →  0.30000000000000004
jshell> 0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3             →  false
jshell> 1.03 - 0.42                  →  0.6100000000000001
jshell> 2.0 - 1.1                    →  0.8999999999999999
jshell> (float) 16_777_217           →  1.6777216E7     (2^24 + 1 needs 25 bits)
jshell> (double) 9_007_199_254_740_993L  →  9.007199254740992E15   (2^53 + 1)

Adding 0.1 ten times gives 0.9999999999999999: errors accumulate in loops. Compare doubles with a tolerance (Math.abs(a - b) < 1e-9, scaled to the magnitudes involved), never with ==. Double.toString prints the shortest decimal that identifies the double uniquely, which is why 0.1 prints as 0.1 even though it is not 0.1.

NaN, Infinity and −0.0

Definition · special values

Floating-point division by zero does not throw: 1.0 / 0 is Infinity, -1.0 / 0 is -Infinity, and 0.0 / 0, Math.sqrt(-1) and Infinity - Infinity are NaN (not a number). Only integer division by zero throws. NaN is unordered: every comparison with it (<, >, ==) is false and != is true, even NaN == NaN. There are two zeros: -0.0 == 0.0 is true, yet 1 / -0.0 is -Infinity.

Test with Double.isNaN(x) and Double.isFinite(x). Double.compare and Double.equals use a different, total order in which NaN equals itself and is greater than everything, and -0.0 is less than 0.0; that is what Arrays.sort(double[]) uses. The two orders disagree, so Double.valueOf(0.0).equals(-0.0) is false while 0.0 == -0.0 is true.

Money: never double

The rule (Effective Java Item 60): if a result must be exact, as with money, don't use float or double. Use BigDecimal, or a long count of the smallest currency unit (cents) when the arithmetic is only addition and subtraction.

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.math.RoundingMode;

public class Money {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(new BigDecimal(0.1));     // 0.1000000000000000055511151231257827...
        System.out.println(new BigDecimal("0.1"));   // 0.1
        System.out.println(BigDecimal.valueOf(0.1)); // 0.1 (goes through Double.toString)

        BigDecimal price = new BigDecimal("19.99");
        BigDecimal net = price.multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(3));         // 59.97, scale 2
        BigDecimal tax = net.multiply(new BigDecimal("0.20"));          // 11.9940, scale 4
        BigDecimal gross = net.add(tax).setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_EVEN);
        System.out.println(net + " " + tax + " " + gross);              // 59.97 11.9940 71.96

        BigDecimal a = new BigDecimal("2.0"), b = new BigDecimal("2.00");
        System.out.println(a.equals(b) + " " + (a.compareTo(b) == 0));  // false true

        BigDecimal third = BigDecimal.ONE.divide(BigDecimal.valueOf(3), 4, RoundingMode.HALF_EVEN);
        System.out.println(third);                                      // 0.3333
        // BigDecimal.ONE.divide(BigDecimal.valueOf(3)) throws ArithmeticException:
        // "Non-terminating decimal expansion; no exact representable decimal result."
    }
}

BigDecimal: construction, scale, rounding, comparison

  • Construction. A BigDecimal is an arbitrary-precision unscaled integer and a scale: 19.99 is 1999 × 10−2. new BigDecimal(double) captures the double's exact binary value, error included; construct from a String (or BigDecimal.valueOf(double) if you already hold a double), or from long cents with BigDecimal.valueOf(1999, 2).
  • Scale follows the operation. Addition takes the larger scale; multiplication adds scales (2 + 2 = 4 above). Fix the scale explicitly at business boundaries with setScale(2, mode).
  • Rounding modes (java.math.RoundingMode): HALF_UP (school rounding), HALF_EVEN (banker's rounding: ties go to the even digit, so rounding errors do not drift in one direction over many operations), HALF_DOWN, UP/DOWN (away from or toward zero), CEILING/FLOOR, and UNNECESSARY, which throws if any rounding would be needed. Which one is right is a business or regulatory decision; ask.
  • Division must say how to round unless the result terminates: pass a scale and mode, or a MathContext such as MathContext.DECIMAL128.
  • equals versus compareTo. equals compares value and scale, so 2.0 and 2.00 are not equal; compareTo compares numeric value only. A HashSet therefore keeps both, a TreeSet keeps one. Compare amounts with compareTo.
  • Immutable. total.add(x); on its own line does nothing; write total = total.add(x);.

BigInteger, and Math.round, floor and ceil

BigInteger is an immutable integer of any size. long holds 20! but not 21!; BigInteger holds 1000! without complaint. BigInteger.TWO.pow(100) is 1267650600228229401496703205376. It also provides the number theory behind public-key cryptography: modPow (413 mod 497 = 445 via BigInteger.valueOf(4).modPow(BigInteger.valueOf(13), BigInteger.valueOf(497))), modInverse, gcd, isProbablePrime and probablePrime. The JDK's RSA implementation is built on it, but in application code use java.security and javax.crypto rather than hand-rolled RSA.

Call2.5−2.5−2.6Returns
Math.round3−2−3long (int for a float); ties round toward +∞
Math.floor2.0−3.0−3.0double, toward −∞
Math.ceil3.0−2.0−2.0double, toward +∞
Math.rint2.0−2.0−3.0double, ties to even
cast to int2−2−2truncates toward zero
What breaks in production
  • Money in double. Sums are off by a cent after enough operations, and reconciliation with the bank fails. Store DECIMAL/NUMERIC columns, map them to BigDecimal, and declare JSON money fields as BigDecimal too (read into an untyped Map, Jackson turns JSON decimals into double by default; Phase 14).
  • Tests comparing BigDecimal with equals. assertEquals(new BigDecimal("10.00"), result) fails when result is 10.0. Compare with compareTo or normalise the scale first.
  • Unbounded division throws ArithmeticException the first time a customer splits a bill three ways.
  • NaN poisoning. One NaN from 0.0 / 0 propagates through every later calculation and makes comparisons silently false; a comparator built from < and > breaks sorting. Validate inputs with Double.isFinite.

Practice · 50 min

  1. Sum 0.1 ten times in a double loop and in a BigDecimal loop and print both. Print Long.toHexString(Double.doubleToLongBits(0.1)) and decode the sign, exponent and fraction by hand.
  2. Write split(BigDecimal total, int ways) that returns a list of shares that add up exactly to total (e.g. 100.00 / 3 → 33.34, 33.33, 33.33). Test it with compareTo.
  3. Round 0.125, 0.135, −0.125 and 2.5 to the scales 2 and 0 under HALF_UP, HALF_EVEN and FLOOR; tabulate the results.
  4. Compute 30! with long (see where it goes wrong) and with BigInteger. Verify 413 mod 497 = 445 with modPow.
  5. Put 0.0, -0.0 and Double.NaN in a HashSet<Double> and a double[] passed to Arrays.sort; explain what you see.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Item 60 · JLS (Java SE 21) §4.2.3 "Floating-Point Types, Formats, and Values", §4.2.4 · David Goldberg, "What Every Computer Scientist Should Know About Floating-Point Arithmetic", ACM Computing Surveys, 1991 · IEEE 754-2019 · Javadoc: java.math.BigDecimal, java.math.RoundingMode, java.math.BigInteger, java.lang.Math

L11 · DAY 11

char, Unicode, Escapes & the Character API

35m video35m practice

A Java char is not a character; it is one 16-bit UTF-16 code unit. That covered every assigned Unicode character when Java was designed, but not since Unicode 3.1 (2001), so any text outside the Basic Multilingual Plane, emoji included, takes two chars. Count, slice and validate text by code point, and always name the encoding when bytes become text.

ASCII, Unicode, UTF-8 and UTF-16

Definition

ASCII is a 7-bit character set: 128 codes, 0–127 ('0' = 48, 'A' = 65, 'a' = 97). Unicode assigns a number, a code point, to every character in every script: U+0000 to U+10FFFF, about 1.1 million slots in 17 planes; plane 0 (U+0000–U+FFFF) is the Basic Multilingual Plane (BMP). An encoding turns code points into bytes. UTF-8 uses 1 to 4 bytes per code point and is identical to ASCII for ASCII text, which is why it dominates files, the web and databases. UTF-16 uses one 16-bit code unit for BMP code points and two (a surrogate pair) for the rest.

Java's char is an unsigned 16-bit integer from 0 to 65,535 ('\u0000' to '￿'), holding one UTF-16 code unit, and a String is logically a sequence of them. Internally, strings may be stored more compactly (L15), but the API still speaks UTF-16.

Code points, code units and surrogate pairs

A code point above U+FFFF is split into a high surrogate (U+D800–U+DBFF) followed by a low surrogate (U+DC00–U+DFFF): subtract 0x10000, put the top 10 bits in the first, the bottom 10 in the second. Those ranges are reserved for this purpose, so a lone surrogate is always a sign of broken text. Even code points are not what a user calls a character: "é" may be one code point (U+00E9) or two (e plus the combining accent U+0301), and a thumbs-up with a skin tone is two code points. User-perceived characters are grapheme clusters; java.text.BreakIterator finds their boundaries, and java.text.Normalizer converts between the composed and decomposed forms.

public class Chars {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String s = "Hi 😀";                     // U+1F600 as a surrogate pair
        System.out.println(s.length());                    // 5: code units
        System.out.println(s.codePointCount(0, s.length())); // 4: code points
        System.out.println(Integer.toHexString(s.charAt(3)));      // d83d: half a pair
        System.out.println(Integer.toHexString(s.codePointAt(3))); // 1f600
        s.codePoints().forEach(cp -> System.out.println(Character.getName(cp)));
        // LATIN CAPITAL LETTER H, LATIN SMALL LETTER I, SPACE, GRINNING FACE
        System.out.println(s.substring(0, 4).endsWith("\uD83D")); // true: a broken string
        System.out.println(new StringBuilder(s).reverse().codePointAt(0) == 0x1F600); // true
        System.out.println(Character.charCount(0x1F600));  // 2
        System.out.println(Character.toString(0x1F600).equals(s.substring(3))); // true
    }
}

StringBuilder.reverse is documented to keep surrogate pairs together; a hand-written loop that reverses chars is not. Iterate with codePoints(), not chars() or charAt, whenever the text can come from users.

Escape sequences and Unicode escapes

Inside character and string literals: \n (line feed), \r (carriage return), \t (tab), \b (backspace), \f (form feed), \s (space, Java 15, mainly for text blocks), \", \', \\, and octal escapes \0–\377. A looks like one more but is different: Unicode escapes are translated before the compiler even splits the source into tokens (JLS §3.3), anywhere in the file. So char c = 'A'; and char c = 'A'; are the same program, and char nl = '\u000a'; does not compile, because the escape becomes a real line break inside the literal. Write '\n'.

Character methods, char arithmetic and conversions

  • Classification: Character.isDigit, isLetter, isLetterOrDigit, isWhitespace, isUpperCase, isLowerCase, isAlphabetic. Each has an int code-point overload; prefer it, because the char versions cannot see supplementary characters.
  • Case: Character.toUpperCase('a') is 'A'. Character-level case mapping is limited: Character.toUpperCase('ß') stays 'ß', while "ß".toUpperCase() is "SS". String case mapping uses the default locale, so "TITLE".toLowerCase() on a Turkish machine yields a dotless ı; use toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT) for identifiers and keys.
  • Digits: Character.digit('f', 16) is 15, Character.forDigit(7, 10) is '7', Character.getNumericValue('7') is 7.
  • Arithmetic: a char is a number, and arithmetic promotes it to int: 'A' + 2 is 67, (char) ('A' + 2) is 'C', '7' - '0' is 7, and 'a' + 'b' is 195 whereas "" + 'a' + 'b' is "ab". c++ and c += 2 keep the char type because of the hidden cast in compound assignment (L13).
From → toCode
char → intint code = 'A'; (implicit widening, 65)
int → charchar c = (char) 66; (explicit cast, 'B')
char → StringString.valueOf(c), Character.toString(c)
String → chars.charAt(0), s.toCharArray()
code point → StringCharacter.toString(0x1F600) (Java 11), new String(Character.toChars(cp))
String → code pointss.codePoints() (an IntStream), s.codePointAt(i)
bytes ↔ Stringnew String(bytes, StandardCharsets.UTF_8), s.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8)
What breaks in production
  • Truncating by char. name.substring(0, 20) can cut a surrogate pair in half; the lone surrogate becomes ? or an encoding error when written to a database or JSON. Truncate on a code-point boundary (s.offsetByCodePoints(0, n)).
  • MySQL's utf8 stores at most 3 bytes per character and rejects emoji; use utf8mb4. Column limits in bytes versus characters also differ by database.
  • Platform-default charsets. Before Java 18, new String(bytes), FileReader and getBytes() used the OS default, often windows-1252 on Windows, so the same code produced mojibake on one machine. Java 18 made UTF-8 the default (JEP 400); still pass StandardCharsets.UTF_8 explicitly.
  • isDigit is not "0–9". It is true for Arabic-Indic, Devanagari and other decimal digits, and Integer.parseInt accepts them too. For ASCII-only validation, check c >= '0' && c <= '9' or use a regex like [0-9]+.

Practice · 35 min

  1. Run Chars. Then compute the surrogate pair for U+1F44D by hand (subtract 0x10000, split 10/10 bits) and check with Character.highSurrogate and lowSurrogate.
  2. Write truncate(String s, int maxCodePoints) that never splits a pair, and test it on a string ending in an emoji.
  3. Write a Caesar cipher that shifts only ASCII letters, using char arithmetic and %, preserving case and leaving everything else unchanged.
  4. Print s.getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8).length and getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_16).length for "A", "é", "€" and the emoji. Explain the extra 2 bytes in UTF-16 (the byte-order mark).

Read JLS (Java SE 21) §3.1 "Unicode", §3.3 "Unicode Escapes", §3.10.4 "Character Literals", §3.10.7 "Escape Sequences" · Javadoc: java.lang.Character (section "Unicode Character Representations"), java.text.BreakIterator, java.text.Normalizer · JEP 400, "UTF-8 by Default" · Joel Spolsky, "The Absolute Minimum Every Software Developer Absolutely, Positively Must Know About Unicode and Character Sets" (2003)

L12 · DAY 12

Booleans, Relational & Logical Operators

30m video35m practice

Java's boolean is its own type with exactly two values and no relationship to numbers, and its && and || operators evaluate their right operand only when they need it. That short-circuit rule is what makes null guards work, and == on objects comparing identity rather than content is the bug every Java developer writes once.

The boolean type

A boolean holds true or false and nothing else. Unlike C, there is no conversion between boolean and any number: if (1), boolean b = 0; and (int) true are all compile errors. Conditions in if, while, for and the ternary must be of type boolean (or Boolean, which is unboxed; a null Boolean then throws, see L14). Name boolean variables and methods as predicates: isOverdue, hasCopies, canBorrow.

Relational and equality operators

<, >, <=, >= work on numeric types, including char, after binary numeric promotion (L14): 'a' < 'b' is true, and 1 == 1.0 is true because the int is widened to double. == and != work on every type, with two very different meanings:

Definition · equality

For primitives, == compares values. For references, == compares identity: whether both refer to the same object in memory. Whether two different objects have the same content is decided by the equals method, which each class defines for itself (L43). Comparisons with NaN are always false except != (L10).

Relational operators do not chain: 0 < x < 10 does not compile, because 0 < x is a boolean and boolean < int is meaningless. Write 0 < x && x < 10.

Logical operators and short-circuit evaluation

LeftRightAND (&&)OR (||)XOR (^)NOT left (!)
falsefalsefalsefalsefalsetrue
falsetruefalsetruetruetrue
truefalsefalsetruetruefalse
truetruetruetruefalsefalse
Rule · short-circuit evaluation

a && b evaluates a first; if it is false the result is false and b is never evaluated. a || b evaluates b only if a is false. Operands are always evaluated left to right (JLS §15.23, §15.24). De Morgan's laws hold: !(a && b) == (!a || !b) and !(a || b) == (!a && !b), which is how you negate a compound condition correctly.

That gives two reasons to care about operand order. Safety: a guard must come before the operation it protects, so s != null && s.isEmpty() is safe and s.isEmpty() && s != null throws. Performance: put cheap tests and tests most likely to decide the result first, so expensive calls run only when they matter.

Non-short-circuit & and |, and XOR

On boolean operands, & and | compute the same truth table as && and || but always evaluate both sides. They are the bitwise operators of L9 applied to one-bit values. You rarely want them; the legitimate use is when the right operand has a side effect that must happen regardless, and even then a separate statement is clearer. ^ (XOR) is true when exactly one operand is true, which for booleans is the same as !=; it reads well for "one or the other but not both", such as a discount for members or coupon holders but not both.

import java.util.Objects;

public class Logic {
    static int calls = 0;

    static boolean expensiveCheck() {
        calls++;
        return true;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String name = null;
        if (name != null && !name.isEmpty()) {          // safe: isEmpty() never runs
            System.out.println("hello " + name);
        }
        boolean a = false;
        boolean r1 = a && expensiveCheck();              // right side skipped
        boolean r2 = a & expensiveCheck();               // right side evaluated anyway
        System.out.println(r1 + " " + r2 + " calls=" + calls);   // false false calls=1

        boolean member = true, coupon = true;
        System.out.println((member ^ coupon) + " " + (member != coupon)); // false false

        String s1 = "java", s2 = new String("java");
        System.out.println(s1 == s2);                     // false: two different objects
        System.out.println(s1.equals(s2));                // true: same characters
        System.out.println(Objects.equals(null, s2));     // false, and no NullPointerException

        boolean done = false;
        if (done = true) {                                // compiles: assigns true, then tests it
            System.out.println("always runs");
        }
    }
}

Common mistakes

  • = versus ==. if (x = 5) does not compile because an int is not a boolean, so Java catches most of these. The exception is a boolean variable: if (done = true) assigns and is always taken. Write if (done) and if (!done); never compare a boolean with true or false.
  • Comparing objects with ==. Two equal strings, two equal BigDecimals or two equal LocalDates are usually different objects. Use a.equals(b), or Objects.equals(a, b) when either may be null. String literals sometimes compare equal with == because of the string pool (L15), which makes the bug intermittent.
  • Boxed numbers. Integer x = 127, y = 127; x == y is true, but with 128 it is false: values from −128 to 127 come from a cache (L54).
  • Where == is right: primitives, enum constants (each is a singleton), and deliberate identity checks such as this == other at the top of an equals method.
What breaks in production
  • IDs compared with ==. order.getCustomerId() == customer.getId() on Long fields passes every test with small IDs and fails once real IDs exceed 127. Use equals, or compare primitives.
  • | in a null guard. if (s == null | s.isEmpty()) evaluates s.isEmpty() even when s is null, so it throws exactly in the case it was meant to handle.
  • Side effects that short-circuit skips. if (isValid(req) && audit.record(req)) records only valid requests, which may not be what the auditors expect. Keep side effects out of conditions.
  • A refactor that reorders a condition, for example extracting s.isEmpty() into a variable computed before the null check, turns a safe guard into a NullPointerException.

Practice · 35 min

  1. Write canBorrow(Member m, Book b) that returns true when the member is not null, is active, has fewer than 5 loans and the book has a free copy. Order the tests so it never throws, and count how often an expensive hasFines(m) call runs for 1,000 random members.
  2. Negate age >= 18 && (hasCard || isStaff) with De Morgan's laws and verify all 8 input combinations in a loop.
  3. Run Logic, then replace each && with & and explain every changed output or exception.
  4. Compare two Integers, two Strings built with new String, and two enum constants with both == and equals; tabulate when they agree.

Read JLS (Java SE 21) §4.2.5 "The boolean Type and boolean Values", §15.20 "Relational Operators", §15.21 "Equality Operators", §15.22.2, §15.23 "Conditional-And Operator", §15.24 "Conditional-Or Operator" · Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Item 10 · Javadoc: java.util.Objects

L13 · DAY 13

Operator Precedence, Ternary & Compound Assignment

30m video30m practice

Precedence and associativity decide how an expression is grouped into a tree; Java then evaluates the operands strictly left to right. Separate those two ideas and the classic puzzles ("Sum: " + a + b, i = i++, b += 5 compiling where b = b + 5 does not) all become predictable.

The precedence table

Level (high to low)OperatorsAssociativity
Postfixx++ x--, plus member access ., indexing [], calls ()left to right
Unary++x --x +x -x ~ ! and casts (type)right to left
Multiplicative* / %left to right
Additive+ - (and string +)left to right
Shift<< >> >>>left to right
Relational< > <= >= instanceofleft to right
Equality== !=left to right
Bitwise / logical AND, XOR, OR&, then ^, then |left to right
Conditional AND, OR&&, then ||left to right
Ternary? :right to left
Assignment= += -= *= /= %= &= ^= |= <<= >>= >>>=right to left

Associativity breaks ties at one level: 10 - 4 - 3 is (10 - 4) - 3 = 3, and 2 * 3 % 4 is (2 * 3) % 4 = 2. Assignment is right-associative and is itself an expression whose value is the assigned value, so x = y = z = 7 assigns 7 to all three, starting with z.

Grouping versus evaluation order

Definition · evaluation order

Precedence only decides grouping. Java guarantees that the operands of every binary operator are evaluated left operand first, that all operands are evaluated before the operation is performed (except for &&, || and ? :), and that method arguments are evaluated left to right (JLS §15.7). So in f() + g() * h() the calls happen in the order f, g, h, even though the multiplication is performed before the addition. C and C++ leave much of this unspecified; Java does not.

When parentheses are mandatory

  • String concatenation: "Sum: " + a + b with a = 1, b = 2 is "Sum: 12", because the left + produces a String first. Write "Sum: " + (a + b).
  • Masks: flags & WRITE != 0 groups as flags & (WRITE != 0) and fails to compile (int & boolean). Write (flags & WRITE) != 0.
  • Casts: a cast binds tighter than arithmetic, so (int) price * qty truncates price before multiplying. Write (int) (price * qty).
  • Shifts: 1 << n - 1 means 1 << (n - 1), which is rarely what a reader expects; spell it out.

Stylistic parentheses are worth adding wherever a reader would need the table: a || b && c is already a || (b && c), but write the parentheses. Do not add them around single operands or obvious arithmetic.

The ternary operator

condition ? valueIfTrue : valueIfFalse is an expression: it produces a value, so it can initialize a final variable or appear in an argument, which an if statement cannot. Only the chosen branch is evaluated. It is right-associative, so a chain reads like else if:

String grade = score >= 90 ? "A"
             : score >= 80 ? "B"
             : score >= 70 ? "C"
             : "F";

One level is idiomatic; a formatted chain like this is acceptable; a ternary nested inside a branch's condition, or mixing side effects, is not. Past two or three cases, use a switch expression (L21). The result type follows the branch types: with numeric branches it is the promoted type, so true ? 1 : 2.0 is 1.0; with one Integer and one int branch it is int, which unboxes (see the trap box).

Compound assignment and its hidden cast

Rule · compound assignment

E1 op= E2 is equivalent to E1 = (T) ((E1) op (E2)), where T is the type of E1, except that E1 is evaluated only once (JLS §15.26.2). The cast is inserted silently, even when it narrows.

public class Operators {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int a = 1, b = 2;
        System.out.println("Sum: " + a + b);       // Sum: 12
        System.out.println("Sum: " + (a + b));     // Sum: 3

        byte level = 10;
        level += 5;                                // OK: means level = (byte) (level + 5)
        // level = level + 5;                      // error: possible lossy conversion from int to byte
        int count = 0;
        count += 3.7;                              // count = (int) (0 + 3.7), so 3
        short s = 1;
        s *= 100_000;                              // (short) 100000 wraps to -31072
        char c = 'a';
        c += 2;                                    // 'c'
        System.out.println(level + " " + count + " " + s + " " + c);   // 15 3 -31072 c

        int i = 5;
        int post = i++;                            // post = 5, then i = 6
        int pre = ++i;                             // i = 7, then pre = 7
        i = i++;                                   // i++ yields 7, sets i to 8, then i = 7 again
        System.out.println(post + " " + pre + " " + i);                // 5 7 7
        int j = 1;
        j = j++ + ++j;                             // 1 (j becomes 2) + 3 (j becomes 3)
        System.out.println(j);                     // 4
    }
}

Prefix, postfix and evaluation-order gotchas

++i increments and then yields the new value; i++ yields the old value and then increments. On their own as statements they are identical; the difference only appears when the value is used. i = i++ is the famous trap: the right side is evaluated first (yielding the old value and incrementing i), then the assignment overwrites i with that old value, so a loop of count = count++ leaves count at 0 forever. Two more consequences of left-to-right evaluation: in arr[i] = i = 1 the index arr[i] is evaluated with the old i; and in arr[k++] += 10, k is incremented once, not twice, because E1 is evaluated only once. Readable code uses ++ only as a standalone statement or in a for header.

Interview trap
  • The ternary NullPointerException. Integer copies = known ? stock.get(isbn) : 0; has an Integer branch and an int branch, so the conditional's type is int: when get returns null, unboxing throws, even though the target is an Integer. Write stock.getOrDefault(isbn, 0) or make both branches Integer.
  • Silent narrowing. total += price * 1.2 on an int total truncates every time with no warning from javac; total = total + price * 1.2 would have refused to compile.
  • "What does x = x++ + ++x print?" Answer it by evaluating operands left to right, then say you would never write it.

Practice · 30 min

  1. Without running them, fully parenthesize and evaluate: 2 + 3 * 4 % 5, 1 << 2 + 1, 10 > 5 == true, "" + 1 + 2 * 3, -5 % 3, !true || false && false. Check in JShell.
  2. Run Operators, then predict and verify int k = 0; k = k++ + k++; and int[] arr = new int[3]; int m = 0; arr[m] = m = 2;.
  3. Rewrite a five-level nested if that maps a member's loan count to a fee tier as a formatted ternary chain, then as a switch expression; decide which you would commit.
  4. Reproduce the ternary NullPointerException with a HashMap, read the stack trace, and fix it two ways.

Read JLS (Java SE 21) §15.7 "Evaluation Order", §15.14.2, §15.15.1, §15.25 "Conditional Operator ? :", §15.26.2 "Compound Assignment Operators" · The Java Tutorials, "Operators" · Bloch & Gafter, Java Puzzlers, Puzzles 9 "Tweedledum" and 25 "Inclement Increment"

L14 · DAY 14

Type Casting, Widening, Narrowing & Autoboxing

40m video40m practice

Java converts between types in a small number of precisely defined ways: widening happens silently because it is (almost) safe, narrowing needs a cast because it can lose data, reference casts change only how the compiler sees an object, and boxing quietly creates objects. Each has one characteristic failure: lost precision, truncated values, ClassCastException and NullPointerException.

Widening conversions and the promotion chart

Widening applies in assignments (long big = someInt;), method arguments and arithmetic. It never throws and never changes the sign or magnitude, but int or long to float, and long to double, round to the nearest representable value: float f = 16_777_217; stores 1.6777216E7. Constants get one extra allowance (JLS §5.2): byte b = 10; and char c = 65; compile because the compile-time constant fits, whereas byte b = someInt; does not.

Numeric promotion and integer division

Definition · binary numeric promotion

Before an arithmetic, comparison or bitwise operation on two numbers (JLS §5.6): if either operand is double, both become double; otherwise if either is float, both become float; otherwise if either is long, both become long; otherwise both become int. Unary operators and shifts promote byte, short and char to int too.

So byte + byte is an int, and byte total = a + b; fails with "possible lossy conversion" (the compound total += a compiles thanks to its hidden cast, L13). And int / int is integer division, which truncates toward zero: 7 / 2 is 3, -7 / 2 is −3, and % takes the sign of the dividend (-7 % 2 is −1). Where the cast goes matters: (double) sum / count casts sum first and divides in double; (double) (sum / count) divides in int and then converts the truncated result. A completion percentage written as done / total * 100 is 0 until the job finishes; write done * 100 / total or use double.

Narrowing casts and data loss

  • Integer to smaller integer keeps the low-order bits and discards the rest, so the sign can flip: (byte) 200 is −56 and (int) 3_000_000_000L is −1,294,967,296.
  • Floating point to integer truncates toward zero ((int) -3.99 is −3), turns NaN into 0, and saturates out-of-range values: (int) 1e20 is Integer.MAX_VALUE. To byte, short or char it goes through int first, so (byte) 300.7 is 44.
  • double to float rounds, and overflows to infinity if too large.
  • Use Math.toIntExact(long) to fail loudly instead, and Math.round when you mean rounding (L10).

Reference casts: upcast, downcast and instanceof

A reference cast never changes the object; it changes the static type through which the compiler lets you use it. An upcast (to a supertype, such as String to Object or CharSequence) is implicit and always safe. A downcast (to a subtype) needs an explicit cast and is checked at run time; if the object is not actually of that type, the JVM throws ClassCastException. Casting between types that can never be related, such as (String) someInteger where the static type is Integer, is rejected at compile time. Guard downcasts with instanceof, ideally in its pattern form (Java 16), which tests, casts and binds a variable in one step; null instanceof T is always false.

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class Conversions {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        byte b = (byte) 200;            // -56
        int t = (int) -3.99;            // -3
        int sat = (int) 1e20;           // 2147483647
        int sum = 7, count = 2;
        System.out.println(b + " " + t + " " + sat + " " + sum / count + " "
                + (double) sum / count + " " + (double) (sum / count));   // -56 -3 2147483647 3 3.5 3.0

        Object o = "library";                              // upcast
        if (o instanceof String s && s.length() > 3) {   // test + cast + bind
            System.out.println(s.toUpperCase());
        }
        Object n = 42;                                     // boxed to Integer, then upcast
        try {
            String bad = (String) n;                       // compiles: Object might be a String
            System.out.println(bad);
        } catch (ClassCastException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
            // class java.lang.Integer cannot be cast to class java.lang.String (...)
        }

        List<Integer> ids = new ArrayList<>(List.of(10, 20, 30));
        ids.remove(1);                    // remove(int index): removes 20
        ids.remove(Integer.valueOf(10));  // remove(Object): removes 10
        System.out.println(ids);          // [30]

        Integer missing = null;
        try {
            int value = missing;          // compiled as missing.intValue()
            System.out.println(value);
        } catch (NullPointerException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
        }
    }
}

Autoboxing and unboxing

Since Java 5 the compiler converts automatically between each primitive and its wrapper class (int/Integer, long/Long, double/Double, boolean/Boolean, char/Character…): when you put an int into a List<Integer>, assign it to an Object, or do arithmetic on wrappers. javap -c shows what it really writes: boxing is a call to Integer.valueOf (which caches −128 to 127), unboxing is a call to intValue(). The rules are strict:

  • Boxing goes only to the matching wrapper: Long id = 5; does not compile (an int cannot box to Long); write 5L. Unboxing may be followed by widening: long x = Integer.valueOf(5); is fine.
  • Overload resolution tries widening before boxing (L27), which is why ids.remove(1) picks remove(int index).
  • Unboxing null throws NullPointerException, in assignments, arithmetic, comparisons (count > 0), if (flag) on a Boolean, and the ternary of L13. Since Java 14 the message says exactly which call failed: Cannot invoke "java.lang.Integer.intValue()" because "missing" is null (local names appear when the class was compiled with -g, as build tools do by default; otherwise you see <local4>-style names).

The cost of boxing in loops

static long slowSum() {
    Long sum = 0L;                       // boxed accumulator
    for (long i = 0; i < Integer.MAX_VALUE; i++) {
        sum += i;                        // unbox, add, box a new Long: about 2^31 allocations
    }
    return sum;
}

Changing Long to long removes every allocation. Bloch's measurement of exactly this loop (Effective Java Item 6) found the boxed version roughly ten times slower; the JIT's escape analysis can sometimes remove boxes, but don't rely on it. Memory adds up too: an int[] stores 4 bytes per element, while a List<Integer> stores a reference to a separate object of typically 16 bytes for most values. Prefer primitives in fields, locals and hot loops (Item 61); use wrappers where you need null or a collection, and primitive streams such as IntStream for bulk arithmetic (Phase 3).

What breaks in production
  • Unboxing a nullable column. A nullable INTEGER column mapped to an Integer field, then passed to a method taking int, throws for the first row that has no value. Decide deliberately whether a field may be null, and use the primitive type when it may not.
  • list.remove(id) on a List<Integer> removes by index, not by value, or throws IndexOutOfBoundsException for large IDs.
  • Casting a long to int. (int) userId or (int) System.currentTimeMillis() produces negative numbers once values pass 231. Use Math.toIntExact or keep long.
  • Integer percentages and averages that are always 0 or silently truncated in reports.
  • Boxed counters in maps. A Map<Long, Long> updated millions of times per minute allocates constantly and pressures the garbage collector; use primitive-specialised structures or LongAdder where it matters (Phase 5).

Practice · 40 min

  1. Predict, then check: (short) 40_000, (char) 65.9, (int) Double.NaN, (long) -1e30, (int) (char) -1, (byte) 128 + (byte) 128.
  2. Write average(int[] values) that returns a double; make it correct for {1, 2} and for two values near Integer.MAX_VALUE (watch the sum).
  3. Run Conversions, then compile it and inspect it with javap -c: find every Integer.valueOf, intValue and checkcast the compiler inserted.
  4. Time slowSum against the same loop with long sum using System.nanoTime(). Then write a method that takes Object and uses pattern-matching instanceof to print a different message for Integer, String and anything else.

Read JLS (Java SE 21) ch. 5 "Conversions and Contexts" (§5.1.2, §5.1.3, §5.1.7, §5.1.8, §5.2, §5.6) · §15.17.2 "Division Operator /" · §15.20.2 "The instanceof Operator" · Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 6 and 61 · JEP 394, "Pattern Matching for instanceof" · JEP 358, "Helpful NullPointerExceptions"

L15 · DAY 15

Strings: Immutability, the String Pool & Interning

45m video40m practice

A String can never change after it is created. That single design decision is what makes strings safe to share between threads, safe to use as security-sensitive arguments and hash keys, and safe to deduplicate in a JVM-wide pool, and it is also why building a string by repeated += in a loop is quadratic.

Why String is immutable

Definition · inside a String

Since Java 9, java.lang.String is a final class holding a private final byte[] value, a byte coder saying how those bytes encode the characters, and a cached int hash. No method writes to value after construction, and because the class is final, no subclass can add one. Every "modifying" method (toUpperCase, replace, concat, substring) returns a new String.

  • Security. Strings carry file paths, URLs, host names, SQL, class names passed to class loaders, and credentials. If a caller could change a string after a security check had validated it, the check would be worthless.
  • Caching and sharing. Because nobody can modify a string, the JVM can hand the same object to every piece of code that uses the literal "GET"; that is the string pool below.
  • Thread safety. Immutable objects can be shared across threads without locks; there is no write to race with (Effective Java Item 17, Phase 5).
  • Hash-code caching. hashCode() is computed on first use and stored in the hash field; it can never become stale. Strings are the most common HashMap key, and repeated lookups skip the O(n) hash computation.

The string pool, literals and new String()

The string pool (HotSpot's StringTable) is a JVM-wide hash table of references to canonical String objects. Since JDK 7 those objects live in the ordinary heap (earlier JVMs kept them in the fixed-size PermGen) and can be garbage-collected when unreferenced. Every string literal and every compile-time constant string expression is interned: all occurrences of "java" in all classes resolve to one pooled object. new String("java") always creates a separate object with the same content. intern() looks the content up in the pool and returns the canonical instance, adding it first if absent.

public class Pool {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        String a = "java";
        String b = "java";               // same literal: same pooled object
        String d = new String("java").intern();
        String c = new String("java");   // always a new object
        String e = "ja" + "va";          // constant expression, folded by javac
        String part = "ja";
        String f = part + "va";          // computed at run time: a new object
        final String fixed = "ja";
        String g = fixed + "va";         // fixed is a constant variable: folded

        System.out.println(a == b);      // true
        System.out.println(a == d);      // true
        System.out.println(a == c);      // false
        System.out.println(a == e);      // true
        System.out.println(a == f);      // false
        System.out.println(a == g);      // true
        System.out.println(a.equals(c) && a.equals(f));   // true: same characters
    }
}

The lesson of the diagram: == on strings is an identity test that happens to succeed for literals, so code that compares with == passes unit tests written with literals and fails on strings read from a request, a file or a database. Always compare content with equals (or equalsIgnoreCase, or Objects.equals when null is possible). Calling intern() yourself is rarely worthwhile; for heaps full of duplicate strings, G1 and some other collectors offer automatic deduplication of the backing arrays (-XX:+UseStringDeduplication, Phase 6).

Compact strings (Java 9+)

Until Java 8 a String wrapped a char[]: two bytes per character, even for plain ASCII. JEP 254 (Java 9) switched to a byte[] plus the coder flag. If every character of a string is in Latin-1 (U+0000 to U+00FF), it is stored with one byte per character (LATIN1); otherwise all of it uses two bytes per UTF-16 code unit (UTF16). A single "€" or Chinese character switches the whole string to two bytes per character. The API is unchanged, since it still speaks UTF-16 (L11), but typical server heaps, full of ASCII identifiers, JSON keys and SQL, shrink noticeably.

How + is compiled: StringConcatFactory and invokedynamic

Up to Java 8, javac translated "id=" + id + ", name=" + name into new StringBuilder().append("id=").append(id)…toString(). Since Java 9 (JEP 280) it emits a single invokedynamic instruction instead. At the first execution, the bootstrap method StringConcatFactory.makeConcatWithConstants builds an optimized concatenation routine for that exact shape, using a "recipe" in which \u0001 marks each argument; the JVM can improve the strategy without your code being recompiled. Abridged javap output for that expression (constant-pool numbers vary):

$ javap -c -cp out Greeting
  ...
  invokedynamic #13,  0   // InvokeDynamic #0:makeConcatWithConstants:(ILjava/lang/String;)Ljava/lang/String;
  ...
BootstrapMethods:
  0: ... java/lang/invoke/StringConcatFactory.makeConcatWithConstants ...
    Method arguments:
      #20 id=\u0001, name=\u0001

Concatenating in loops

static String joinSlow(java.util.List<String> names) {
    String result = "";
    for (String n : names) {
        result += n + ",";              // copies everything built so far, every time
    }
    return result;
}

static String joinFast(java.util.List<String> names) {
    var sb = new StringBuilder();
    for (String n : names) {
        sb.append(n).append(',');       // amortised O(1) per append
    }
    return sb.toString();               // or simply String.join(",", names) + ","
}

Because a string cannot grow, every result += … allocates a new string and copies all characters accumulated so far. For n appends of similar size the total copying is proportional to n², not n: appending one character 100,000 times copies about 5 × 109 characters and leaves 100,000 dead strings for the garbage collector. invokedynamic does not help, because each iteration is a separate concatenation. Use a StringBuilder (L16–L17), String.join, or Collectors.joining (Effective Java Item 63). A single expression with many +s outside a loop is fine.

What breaks in production
  • == on strings from outside. if (role == "ADMIN") works with a literal in a test and fails for the value parsed from a JWT or a database row. Static analysis (SonarLint, IntelliJ inspections) flags it; treat the warning as an error.
  • Passwords in Strings. An immutable string cannot be wiped; it stays in memory until collected and shows up in heap dumps. Security APIs such as KeyStore and PBEKeySpec take char[] so callers can zero the array after use.
  • Report and CSV builders with += that are fast for 100 rows and take minutes for 100,000, while churning the young generation.
  • Interning unbounded input such as request IDs grows the string table with values that are never reused, for no gain.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Run Pool. Then read a word with new java.util.Scanner(System.in).next(), compare it with the literal using ==, equals and after intern(), and draw the memory diagram for each case.
  2. Compile a class whose method returns "id=" + id + ", name=" + name and find the invokedynamic and its recipe with javap -c -v.
  3. Time joinSlow and joinFast for 1,000, 10,000 and 100,000 names with System.nanoTime(). Check that the slow time grows roughly 100× per 10× more input.
  4. Explain in your journal why String must be final for immutability to hold, by sketching a subclass that would break a HashSet<String>.

Read JLS (Java SE 21) §3.10.5 "String Literals", §15.18.1 "String Concatenation Operator +", §15.29 "Constant Expressions" · JEP 254, "Compact Strings" · JEP 280, "Indify String Concatenation" · Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 17 and 63 · Javadoc: java.lang.String (intern), java.lang.invoke.StringConcatFactory

L16 · DAY 16

The String API, Method by Method

50m video50m practice

A String is an immutable sequence of UTF-16 code units, and almost every method on it either reads positions in that sequence or returns a new string. Know which methods take a regex, which depend on the default locale, and which count code units instead of characters, and the API stops producing surprises.

What a String is underneath

Definition · compact strings

Since Java 9 (JEP 254) a String holds a byte[] plus a coder flag: LATIN1 (one byte per char) when every char fits in 8 bits, otherwise UTF16 (two bytes per char). The array is never modified after construction, so every "modifying" method returns a new object. length() and charAt(i) are O(1) and work in UTF-16 code units, not user-visible characters: "😀".length() is 2, while codePointCount(0, s.length()) is 1.

Reading positions: length, charAt, substring, indexOf
