Indices are zero-based and ranges are half-open: substring(begin, end) includes begin and excludes end, so its length is end - begin. substring(begin) runs to the end. Out-of-range indices throw StringIndexOutOfBoundsException. Since Java 7u6 substring copies the chars, so a tiny substring no longer pins a huge parent array in memory.

indexOf returns the first match position or -1; indexOf(str, from) starts searching at from; lastIndexOf searches backwards. contains, startsWith and endsWith are plain literal tests (no regex) and return boolean. startsWith(prefix, offset) tests at an offset without creating a substring.

Comparing: equals, equalsIgnoreCase, compareTo

== compares references; two equal strings built at runtime are usually different objects. equals compares content exactly. equalsIgnoreCase compares char by char after Character.toUpperCase/toLowerCase; it ignores locale, which makes it safe for protocol tokens. compareTo orders lexicographically by UTF-16 code unit: at the first differing index it returns the difference of the two chars, and if one string is a prefix of the other it returns the length difference.

"Hello".compareTo("Help");   // 'l' - 'p' = -4
"app".compareTo("apple");    // 3 - 5     = -2
"a".compareTo("B");          // 97 - 66   = 31, so "B" sorts before "a"
"a".compareToIgnoreCase("B");             // negative
List.of("b", "A", "a").stream()
    .sorted(String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER).toList();   // [A, a, b]

Code-unit order is not dictionary order for human text (accents, case). For user-facing sorting use java.text.Collator.getInstance(locale).

Case conversion and the Turkish i

toUpperCase() and toLowerCase() without arguments use Locale.getDefault(). In Turkish and Azeri, lowercase i uppercases to dotted İ (U+0130) and uppercase I lowercases to dotless ı (U+0131). Case mapping can also change length: "ß".toUpperCase() is "SS".

Locale tr = Locale.forLanguageTag("tr");
"title".toUpperCase(tr);          // "TİTLE"  (not equal to "TITLE")
"TITLE".toLowerCase(tr);          // "tıtle"
"title".toUpperCase(Locale.ROOT); // "TITLE"  always
// Rule: machine identifiers (enum names, headers, keys) use Locale.ROOT;
// text shown to a user uses the user's locale.

Whitespace: trim, strip, isBlank, isEmpty

MethodSinceTreats as whitespace
trim()1.0any char ≤ U+0020 (space and ASCII control chars), both ends
strip(), stripLeading(), stripTrailing()11Character.isWhitespace: Unicode spaces such as U+2003, but not U+00A0
isEmpty()6nothing: true only when length() == 0
isBlank()11true when empty or only isWhitespace chars

Neither trim nor strip removes the non-breaking space U+00A0, which arrives constantly in data pasted from web pages and spreadsheets. If you must handle it, replace('\u00A0', ' ') first.

split, join, replace, replaceAll, matches

split(regex) takes a regular expression and is shorthand for split(regex, 0). The limit controls the result: positive n gives at most n parts (the last holds the rest), zero drops trailing empty strings, negative keeps them. A leading empty string appears when the input starts with a delimiter.

"a,b,,".split(",");         // [a, b]          trailing empties dropped
"a,b,,".split(",", -1);     // [a, b, , ]      kept
"k=v=w".split("=", 2);      // [k, v=w]
",a".split(",");            // [, a]           leading empty kept
"1.2.3".split(".");         // []  "." matches every char; all parts empty
"1.2.3".split("\\.");       // [1, 2, 3]
"1.2.3".split(Pattern.quote(".")); // same, for arbitrary delimiters
String.join("|", List.of("x", "y"));  // "x|y"

replace(CharSequence, CharSequence) replaces every literal occurrence; despite the name, replaceAll is not "replace, but all": it takes a regex, and in its replacement string $1 means group 1 and \ escapes. Pass user data through Matcher.quoteReplacement. matches(regex) must match the whole string (implicitly anchored) and compiles the pattern on every call, so precompile a Pattern for hot paths.

"a.b.c".replace(".", "/");                  // "a/b/c"
"a.b.c".replaceAll(".", "/");               // "/////"
"2024-01-05".replaceAll("(\\d+)-(\\d+)-(\\d+)", "$3/$2/$1"); // "05/01/2024"
"price".replaceAll("price", "$5");          // IndexOutOfBoundsException: No group 5
"price".replaceAll("price", Matcher.quoteReplacement("$5")); // "$5"
"abc123".matches("[a-z]+");                 // false: whole string must match

repeat, lines, chars, toCharArray, valueOf, format

"-".repeat(20) builds a separator (negative count throws). lines() returns a Stream<String> split on \n, \r or \r\n, without a trailing empty line. chars() is an IntStream of code units; use codePoints() when emoji or other supplementary characters matter. toCharArray() returns a fresh copy you may mutate. String.valueOf(x) converts any primitive or object, returning "null" for a null object reference; String.format and the instance method formatted are covered in L18.

long vowels = "Backend".toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT).chars()
        .filter(c -> "aeiou".indexOf(c) >= 0).count();   // 2
long nonBlank = "a\n\n b \r\nc".lines().filter(l -> !l.isBlank()).count(); // 3
char[] cs = "hello".toCharArray(); cs[0] = 'j';          // "hello" untouched
What breaks in production
  • Default-locale case mapping. status.toUpperCase() on a server whose default locale is Turkish turns "active" into "ACTİVE", and Status.valueOf throws. Use Locale.ROOT.
  • Regex where you meant a literal. split("."), split("|") and replaceAll("$", …) all misbehave silently. Escape, or use Pattern.quote.
  • Dropped trailing fields. Parsing "id,name,," with split(",") gives 2 fields, not 4, and the next line of code reads index 3. Pass -1.
  • Comparing with ==. It works in tests with literals (interned) and fails with strings read from a request.
  • Truncating by length(). substring(0, 10) can cut an emoji in half and leave an unpaired surrogate.

Practice · 50 min

  1. Write static String[] parseCsvLine(String line) that keeps empty and trailing fields. Test it on "a,,b," (expect 4 parts) and "".
  2. Set -Duser.language=tr, call "title".toUpperCase() and compare with "TITLE". Then fix it with Locale.ROOT and explain why equalsIgnoreCase still returned true.
  3. Build a string containing U+00A0 and U+2003 at both ends. Print the length after trim(), strip() and isBlank() on each, and predict the results first.
  4. Write static boolean isPalindrome(String s) that ignores case and non-letters, using chars() or two indices. Then write static String initials(String fullName) with split("\\s+") and String.join.
  5. Time 1,000,000 calls of s.matches("\\d{4}") against a precompiled Pattern.compile("\\d{4}").matcher(s).matches().
Answer · exercise 3

trim() removes neither (both are above U+0020). strip() removes U+2003 but keeps U+00A0. A string of only those chars: isBlank() is false because Character.isWhitespace('\u00A0') is false.

Read Javadoc java.lang.String, java.util.regex.Pattern, java.util.Locale · JLS §3.10.5 (string literals) · JEP 254, “Compact Strings” · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 17 (minimize mutability)

L17 · DAY 17

StringBuilder, StringBuffer & Text Blocks

40m video40m practice

Because String is immutable, building text piece by piece with += copies everything built so far on every step; a StringBuilder keeps one growable array and appends in amortized constant time. For text that is fixed at compile time, a text block is the readable alternative to both.

Why mutable builders exist

Each s += x allocates a new string and copies all of s into it. In a loop of n appends that is 1 + 2 + … + n char copies, which is quadratic. Since Java 9 (JEP 280) javac compiles a single concatenation expression such as a + ":" + b into an invokedynamic call to StringConcatFactory, which sizes the result once and is fast. That optimisation applies per expression; it cannot remove the new string created on each loop iteration.

public class ConcatBench {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int n = 100_000;
        long t0 = System.nanoTime();
        String s = "";
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) s += 'x';        // new String each pass
        long t1 = System.nanoTime();
        var sb = new StringBuilder();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) sb.append('x');  // amortized O(1)
        String t = sb.toString();
        long t2 = System.nanoTime();
        System.out.printf("concat %d ms, builder %d ms, same=%b%n",
                (t1 - t0) / 1_000_000, (t2 - t1) / 1_000_000, s.equals(t));
    }
}

Expect the concatenation loop to take hundreds of milliseconds to seconds and the builder loop a few milliseconds; the exact numbers depend on the machine and JIT warm-up. For trustworthy measurements use JMH; a single nanoTime run is a demonstration, not a benchmark.

The StringBuilder API

Every mutator returns this, so calls chain. Positions follow the half-open convention from L16.

var sb = new StringBuilder("backend");   // length 7, capacity 7 + 16 = 23
sb.insert(0, "java-");                   // java-backend
sb.append('!').append(42);               // java-backend!42
sb.delete(sb.length() - 2, sb.length()); // java-backend!
sb.replace(0, 4, "JVM");                 // JVM-backend!
sb.deleteCharAt(3);                      // JVMbackend!
sb.reverse();                            // !dnekcabMVJ
sb.setLength(4);                         // !dne   (longer length pads with '\0')
sb.setLength(0);                         // empty, but the array is kept for reuse
String out = sb.toString();              // copies into a new immutable String

reverse keeps surrogate pairs in order, so emoji survive. StringBuilder does not override equals: two builders with the same content are not equal. Compare with a.compareTo(b) == 0 (Java 11+) or a.toString().contentEquals(b).

Capacity and array growth

Definition · capacity

A builder has a length (chars in use) and a capacity (size of the backing array). The no-arg constructor starts at capacity 16; new StringBuilder(str) starts at str.length() + 16. When an append needs more room, the array grows to roughly 2 × old + 2 (or to the required size if that is larger) and the contents are copied. Doubling makes the total copy cost linear, so each append is amortized O(1).

If you know the final size, presize: new StringBuilder(rows * 80) avoids every intermediate copy. ensureCapacity(n) does the same later, and trimToSize() shrinks the array to the length. Since Java 9 builders also use the compact LATIN1/UTF16 byte layout, inflating to UTF16 the first time a char above U+00FF is appended.

StringBuffer and synchronization

StringBuffer (Java 1.0) has the same API with every public method synchronized. StringBuilder (Java 5) is the unsynchronized drop-in replacement and is what you should use. The locking rarely buys anything: a builder is almost always a local variable confined to one thread, and per-method locks do not make a sequence of calls atomic. Two threads doing if (buf.length() > 0) buf.append(',') and then buf.append(item) can still interleave. When several threads produce text, give each its own builder and combine the results, or use Collectors.joining on a stream. You still meet StringBuffer in old APIs such as Matcher.appendReplacement(StringBuffer, …) (a StringBuilder overload was added in Java 9).

Text blocks

Definition · text block (Java 15)

A literal that opens with """ followed by a line terminator and closes with """. The compiler (1) normalizes line endings to \n; (2) removes incidental whitespace: the smallest leading indentation over all non-blank lines, counting the closing-delimiter line if it stands alone; (3) strips trailing spaces on each line; (4) then interprets escapes. The result is an ordinary String, interned like any literal.

String sql = """
        SELECT id, name
        FROM users
        WHERE status = ?
          AND created_at > ?
        """;
// "SELECT id, name\nFROM users\nWHERE status = ?\n  AND created_at > ?\n"

String oneLine = """
    Lorem ipsum \
    dolor sit""";            // "Lorem ipsum dolor sit": no newlines at all

String kept = """
    red  \s
    green\s
    """;                     // "red   \ngreen \n": \s is a space that survives step 3

String html = """
    <p class="greeting">Hello, %s</p>
    """.formatted("Ada");    // quotes need no escaping; \""" embeds three

The closing delimiter is the indentation dial: move it left of the content and the content keeps that much leading space; put it on the last content line and the string has no trailing newline. Text blocks beat concatenation whenever the text has many lines or many quotes: SQL, JSON test fixtures, HTML and log templates read exactly as they will be sent, and diffs stay clean.

What breaks in production
  • += in a loop over request data. Fine with 10 rows in a test, a CPU spike with 50,000 rows in production.
  • A builder shared between threads. Use StringBuffer and the output still interleaves; use StringBuilder and you can also corrupt its internal state. Keep builders local.
  • Reusing a huge builder with setLength(0). The capacity stays, so a one-off 50 MB report keeps 50 MB alive for as long as the builder is reachable.
  • Mixed tabs and spaces in a text block. Each whitespace char counts as one column, so a mixed margin strips the wrong amount. Configure the editor to use spaces.
  • Text block plus formatted for SQL or JSON with user input. That is an injection bug; use bind parameters and a JSON library.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Run ConcatBench for n = 10,000, 50,000 and 100,000. Does the concat time grow roughly 4× when n doubles? Explain why.
  2. Write a loop that appends 1,000 chars to a default builder and prints capacity() each time it changes. Check the sequence against the figure.
  3. Write static String table(List<String[]> rows) that renders rows as an aligned text table with a presized builder, then the same with Collectors.joining.
  4. Rewrite a five-line SQL query built with + and "\n" as a text block. Assert that both strings are equal, adjusting the closing delimiter until they are.
Answer · exercise 1

Yes, about 4×: the copy count is n(n+1)/2, so doubling n quadruples the work. The builder time roughly doubles.

Read Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 63 (beware the performance of string concatenation) · Javadoc StringBuilder, StringBuffer, StringConcatFactory · JEP 378, “Text Blocks” · JEP 280, “Indify String Concatenation” · Laskey & Marks, “Programmer's Guide to Text Blocks” (Oracle)

L18 · DAY 18

String Formatting: printf, String.format & MessageFormat

35m video35m practice

Every printf-style method in Java is a front end to one engine, java.util.Formatter, driven by a small specifier grammar and a locale. Learn the grammar once, always decide which locale you mean, and reach for MessageFormat and NumberFormat when the text is for humans in more than one language.

The specifier grammar

Definition · format specifier

A format specifier is %[argument_index$][flags][width][.precision]conversion. Everything except the conversion is optional. The formatter scans the string left to right, copies literal text, and for each specifier takes the next argument (or the indexed one), checks that its type fits the conversion, and appends the formatted result.

ConversionAcceptsExampleOutput
%sanything (String.valueOf)"%.3s", "backend"bac
%dbyte, short, int, long, BigInteger"%,d", 12345671,234,567
%ffloat, double, BigDecimal"%08.3f", Math.E0002.718
%efloat, double, BigDecimal"%e", 12345.6781.234568e+04
%x / %Xinteger types"%#x", 2550xff
%banything"%b", "false"true (!)
%nno argument"a%nb"platform line separator

Flags: - left-justify (needs a width), + always show the sign, 0 zero-pad (needs a width, not with -), , locale grouping separators, ( negatives in parentheses, # alternate form (0x prefix). %x prints negative ints in two's complement: "%x", -1 gives ffffffff. %f rounds HALF_UP on the decimal value, defaults to precision 6, and %% prints a literal percent sign.

Argument indices, and which front end to use

String.format("%2$s, %1$s", "world", "hello");     // "hello, world"
String.format("%s is %<S", "ok");                   // "ok is OK"  (%< reuses previous arg)
System.out.printf("%-8s|%6d|%8.2f|%n", "apples", 42, 3.14159);
//                 apples  |    42|    3.14|
String row = "%s has %,d rows".formatted("users", 1_234_567);   // Java 15+

String.format returns a string, PrintStream.printf (an alias for format) writes to a stream, and "…".formatted(args) is the instance form that reads well after a text block (L17). All three use the default format locale; only String.format(Locale, …) and printf(Locale, …) let you pass one, so prefer them for machine-readable output.

java.util.Formatter is the engine itself. Create one over any Appendable (a StringBuilder, a Writer, a file) to format many lines without creating intermediate strings:

record Line(String sku, int qty, double price) {}

static String invoice(List<Line> lines) {
    var sb = new StringBuilder();
    try (var f = new Formatter(sb, Locale.ROOT)) {
        for (Line l : lines)
            f.format("%-10s %5d %10.2f%n", l.sku(), l.qty(), l.qty() * l.price());
    }                                  // close() is harmless: a StringBuilder is not Closeable
    return sb.toString();
}

Locale-aware formatting

double big = 1234567.891;
String.format(Locale.US, "%,.2f", big);        // 1,234,567.89
String.format(Locale.GERMANY, "%,.2f", big);   // 1.234.567,89
String.format(Locale.ROOT, "%.2f", 3.14159);   // 3.14 on every server

The decimal separator is part of the locale. A service that writes String.format("%.2f", x) into CSV or JSON will emit 3,14 the day it is deployed on a host whose default locale is German or French, and the consumer will fail to parse it.

NumberFormat and currency

NumberFormat usd = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance(Locale.US);
usd.format(new BigDecimal("1234.5"));                     // $1,234.50
NumberFormat eur = NumberFormat.getCurrencyInstance(Locale.GERMANY);
eur.format(new BigDecimal("1234.5"));                     // 1.234,50 €
NumberFormat.getPercentInstance(Locale.US).format(0.256); // 26%
NumberFormat.getIntegerInstance(Locale.US).format(2.5);   // 2  (HALF_EVEN)

NumberFormat handles symbol placement, grouping and the currency's usual fraction digits. Keep money in BigDecimal (or a long count of minor units) and only format at the edge. Note the German output puts a no-break space before the euro sign, so tests that compare with a plain space fail. NumberFormat and MessageFormat instances are not thread-safe; create one per use or per thread.

MessageFormat for i18n templates

MessageFormat uses numbered placeholders {0}, {1,number,integer}, {2,date,long}, so a translator can reorder them in a resource bundle without touching code. A choice subformat handles simple plurals.

var mf = new MessageFormat(
    "{0} uploaded {1,choice,0#no files|1#one file|1<{1,number,integer} files}.", Locale.US);
mf.format(new Object[] {"Ada", 1});      // Ada uploaded one file.
mf.format(new Object[] {"Ada", 1500});   // Ada uploaded 1,500 files.
MessageFormat.format("Order {0} isn''t ready", 12345);  // Order 12,345 isn't ready

Two rules cause most bugs: a single quote starts a quoted section, so an apostrophe must be written ''; and a bare {0} formats numbers with grouping, so an ID becomes 12,345. Use {0,number,#} or pass the ID as a string.

What breaks in production
  • Wrong conversion type. "%d" with a double throws IllegalFormatConversionException at runtime, not compile time. Too few arguments throws MissingFormatArgumentException; too many are silently ignored.
  • %b on a non-boolean. Any non-null object prints true, including the string "false".
  • \n versus %n. %n is the platform separator. For network protocols and files with a fixed format, write the exact bytes you need instead.
  • Default locale in machine output. Commas as decimal points in CSV, grouped IDs in log keys, Turkish case rules (L16). Pass Locale.ROOT.
  • A static shared NumberFormat. Concurrent calls corrupt its internal buffers and produce wrong digits without throwing.

Practice · 35 min

  1. Print a receipt of five items with left-aligned names (width 20), right-aligned quantities (width 4) and prices with two decimals, using one format string and %n.
  2. Format 1234567.891 under Locale.US, Locale.GERMANY, Locale.FRANCE and Locale.ROOT with "%,.2f". Print each result's chars as hex to find the French grouping char.
  3. Write a MessageFormat pattern for "You have no / one / N new messages" and test it with 0, 1, 2 and 10,000.
  4. Find the bugs: log.info(MessageFormat.format("can't find user {0}", userId)) with a numeric userId. Fix both.
Answer · exercise 4

The lone apostrophe opens a quoted section that runs to the end of the pattern, so the output is cant find user {0}: the apostrophe vanishes and the placeholder is printed literally. Write can''t. Once fixed, a numeric ID is grouped (12,345); use {0,number,#}.

Read Javadoc java.util.Formatter (the "Format String Syntax" section), java.text.MessageFormat, java.text.NumberFormat, java.text.ChoiceFormat · The Java Tutorials, “Formatting Numeric Print Output” and “Internationalization” trail

L19 · DAY 19

Console I/O: Scanner, BufferedReader & System Streams

35m video40m practice

Console input is a stream of bytes that you decode into characters, buffer, and split into lines or tokens. Scanner does all of that with regexes and convenience methods; BufferedReader does only the buffering and lines, which is why it is faster and why it never leaves a stray newline behind.

The three standard streams

Definition · standard streams

System.in is an InputStream of raw bytes. System.out and System.err are PrintStreams that encode chars to bytes and never throw IOException (check checkError() instead). The OS gives every process these three file descriptors (0, 1, 2), so a shell can redirect them independently: java App < in.txt > out.txt 2> err.txt.

Send results to out and diagnostics to err, so that piping the program into another tool does not mix log noise into the data. The two streams are buffered separately, so when both go to the same terminal their lines can appear out of order. System.setIn/setOut/setErr replace them, which is how tests capture output.

Scanner: tokens, lookahead, delimiters

A Scanner splits its input into tokens separated by the delimiter pattern (whitespace by default). nextInt(), nextDouble() and next() skip delimiters, read one token and parse it; a token that does not parse throws InputMismatchException and is not consumed. hasNextInt() and friends look ahead without consuming. nextLine() is different: it returns everything up to the next line separator, even if that is nothing, and consumes the separator. useDelimiter(",") changes the token pattern; useLocale(Locale.ROOT) stops nextDouble() from expecting 3,14 on a German machine.

The nextInt then nextLine bug

Worked example

Input typed: 42⏎Ada Lovelace⏎. The code calls int age = sc.nextInt(); then String name = sc.nextLine();. nextInt consumes 42 and stops before the newline. nextLine then sees the newline immediately, returns "", and the name is empty without the user ever being asked.

// Fix 1: discard the rest of the line after a token read
int age = sc.nextInt();
sc.nextLine();
String name = sc.nextLine();

// Fix 2 (preferred): read every answer as a line, then parse it
int age2 = Integer.parseInt(sc.nextLine().strip());
String name2 = sc.nextLine();

// Fix 3: never mix styles; use token methods only (works when no value has spaces)
int age3 = sc.nextInt();
String first = sc.next();

BufferedReader, and why it is faster

BufferedReader reads a large block (8,192 chars by default) from the underlying reader and serves readLine() from memory. It returns null at end of input and does no regex matching. Scanner runs a regex for every delimiter and token and keeps a smaller buffer, so on large inputs (competitive programming, log replay) it is commonly several times slower. With a reader you parse yourself:

import java.io.*;
import java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets;
import java.util.StringTokenizer;

public class SumFast {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {
        var in = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(System.in, StandardCharsets.UTF_8));
        int n = Integer.parseInt(in.readLine().strip());
        var st = new StringTokenizer(in.readLine());
        long sum = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) sum += Long.parseLong(st.nextToken());
        System.out.println(sum);
    }
}

Console, validation loops and closing

System.console() gives a Console with readLine(fmt, args) and readPassword(fmt, args), which turns off echo and returns a char[] so you can wipe it with Arrays.fill(pw, '\0') after use; a String would linger in the heap until collected. Traditionally console() returns null when there is no interactive terminal (IDE run windows, piped input); since JDK 22 it may return a console anyway and Console.isTerminal() tells you which, so always handle the non-interactive case.

static int readInt(Scanner sc, String prompt, int min, int max) {
    while (true) {
        System.out.print(prompt);
        if (!sc.hasNextLine()) throw new IllegalStateException("input closed");
        String line = sc.nextLine().strip();
        try {
            int v = Integer.parseInt(line);
            if (v >= min && v <= max) return v;
        } catch (NumberFormatException e) {
            // fall through and ask again
        }
        System.out.printf("Enter a whole number from %d to %d.%n", min, max);
    }
}

Closing a Scanner or reader closes the stream it wraps. Close one wrapped around System.in and every later read of standard input fails with NoSuchElementException or IOException. Create one input object for the whole program and leave it open; use try-with-resources for files and sockets, which you do own.

System.exit and exit codes

System.exit(status) runs shutdown hooks and terminates the JVM; it never returns, so finally blocks of the calling thread do not run. By convention 0 means success and anything else failure; many Unix tools use 2 for bad usage, and shells report 128 + n for a process killed by signal n (130 after Ctrl+C). If main ends with an uncaught exception, the launcher exits with 1. Scripts and CI read the code with echo $? (bash) or $LASTEXITCODE (PowerShell).

public class LineCount {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        if (args.length != 1) {
            System.err.println("usage: java LineCount FILE");
            System.exit(2);                                  // usage error
        }
        try (var lines = java.nio.file.Files.lines(java.nio.file.Path.of(args[0]))) {
            System.out.println(lines.count());               // falls off main: status 0
        } catch (java.io.IOException | java.io.UncheckedIOException e) {
            System.err.println("cannot read " + args[0] + ": " + e.getMessage());
            System.exit(1);                                  // runtime failure
        }
    }
}
What breaks in production
  • Infinite validation loop. while (!sc.hasNextInt()) System.out.println("again"); never consumes the bad token, so it spins forever. Consume it with sc.next(), or read lines.
  • System.exit inside a server or library. It kills the whole JVM, every request in flight included. Throw an exception and let the top level decide.
  • Platform charset. new InputStreamReader(System.in) without a charset decodes with a platform default; name the charset explicitly.
  • Logs on stdout. Mixing logs with data on out breaks every pipe that consumes the data.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Reproduce the nextInt/nextLine bug, then apply each of the three fixes. Which one survives a user typing 42 years?
  2. Generate a file with 1,000,000 integers. Sum it through Scanner.nextInt and through BufferedReader + StringTokenizer with input redirection, and compare times.
  3. Write a login prompt with Console.readPassword that wipes the array in a finally block and exits with status 1 when there is no terminal.
  4. Run LineCount with no arguments, a missing file, and a real file; print the exit code each time.
Answer · exercise 1

Fix 1 leaves age = 42 and discards " years"; fix 2 throws NumberFormatException on "42 years", which the validation loop can catch and re-prompt; fix 3 reads 42, then next() returns "years" as the name. Fix 2 inside a validation loop is the only one that notices the input is wrong.

Read Javadoc java.util.Scanner, java.io.BufferedReader, java.io.Console, java.lang.System, java.lang.Runtime (exit, halt, shutdown hooks) · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 9 (prefer try-with-resources) · JEP 400, “UTF-8 by Default”

L20 · DAY 20

Conditionals: if / else if / else

30m video35m practice

An if in Java takes exactly one boolean and nothing else, and its branches are tested strictly top to bottom. Most conditional bugs come from ignoring one of those two facts; most unreadable conditionals are fixed by guard clauses and De Morgan's laws.

Syntax, braces, and the dangling else

The grammar is if (Expression) Statement [else Statement], where the statement may be a single statement or a block. Without braces only the next statement is guarded, and an else always attaches to the nearest unmatched if (JLS §14.5 handles this with its "no short if" productions):

if (user.isAdmin())
    if (featureOn) enable();
else                      // indentation lies: this else belongs to if (featureOn)
    deny();

if (amount > limit);      // stray semicolon: the if guards an empty statement
{
    reject();             // always runs
}

Always use braces, even for one line. The cost is a few characters; the benefit is that adding a second line later cannot silently escape the condition. Apple's 2014 "goto fail" TLS bug in C was exactly a duplicated unbraced line.

else if chains and evaluation order

else if is not a keyword; it is an else whose statement happens to be another if. Conditions are evaluated in order and the first true branch wins, so later conditions may assume the earlier ones were false. Put the narrowest condition first.

static String grade(int score) {
    if (score < 0 || score > 100) {
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("score " + score);
    } else if (score >= 90) {
        return "A";
    } else if (score >= 80) {
        return "B";
    } else {
        return "C";
    }
}

Guard clauses: flattening nested conditionals

Deep nesting ("arrow code") forces the reader to hold every enclosing condition in mind to understand the innermost line. A guard clause checks a precondition at the top and leaves immediately with return or throw, so the main logic sits at one indentation level and reads as the normal case.

// Before: four levels, and the rule that matters is buried at the bottom
static double discount(Customer c, Order o) {
    if (c != null) {
        if (o != null) {
            if (c.active()) {
                if (o.total() > 100) {
                    return 0.10;
                } else {
                    return 0.05;
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return 0.0;
}
record Customer(String name, boolean active) {}
record Order(double total) {}

// After: guards first, then the one real decision
static double discount(Customer c, Order o) {
    if (c == null || o == null) {
        return 0.0;
    }
    if (!c.active()) {
        return 0.0;
    }
    return o.total() > 100 ? 0.10 : 0.05;
}

Other ways to cut nesting: merge two nested ifs that have no else into one with &&; extract a deep branch into a well-named method; replace a long chain over a closed set of values with a switch (L21). Methods that need more than two or three levels are usually doing more than one job.

No truthiness, and null-safe ordering

Definition · boolean condition

The condition must have type boolean or Boolean (JLS §14.9). There is no truthiness: if (count), if (list) and if (name) do not compile. A Boolean is unboxed, so a null Boolean throws NullPointerException.

Because if (x = 5) has type int, the C typo of assigning instead of comparing is a compile error, except for booleans: if (done = true) compiles and is always true. Write if (done).

&& and || short-circuit: the right operand is evaluated only if the left one does not already decide the result. That makes left-to-right order a null-safety tool. The non-short-circuit & and | evaluate both sides.

if (s != null && !s.isBlank()) { … }    // safe: isBlank never runs on null
if (!s.isBlank() && s != null) { … }    // NPE first, check second: useless
if (s != null & !s.isBlank()) { … }     // single &: both sides run, NPE
if ("admin".equals(role)) { … }          // constant first: false for null role
if (Objects.equals(a, b)) { … }          // null-safe for both sides

Simplifying boolean expressions and De Morgan's laws

Theorem · De Morgan's laws

!(a && b) equals !a || !b, and !(a || b) equals !a && !b. Negating a compound condition flips every operand and swaps && with ||.

BeforeAfterWhy
if (ok) return true; else return false;return ok;the condition already is the answer
if (flag == true)if (flag)comparing a boolean to a literal adds nothing
!(x >= 0 && x < n)x < 0 || x >= nDe Morgan, then flip each comparison
!(weekend || holiday)!weekend && !holiday"neither weekend nor holiday"
a && b || a && ca && (b || c)factor the common operand

One exception: flipping a comparison is only safe for integers and objects. For double, every comparison with NaN is false, so !(d < limit) is true for NaN while d >= limit is false. Validation code written as if (d < 0 || d > max) throw … lets NaN through; add Double.isNaN(d).

What breaks in production
  • Misordered ranges. if (age >= 18) … else if (age >= 65) …: the senior branch is unreachable, and the compiler does not warn.
  • Null Boolean from a database or JSON field. if (user.getVerified()) throws NPE when the column is null. Use Boolean.TRUE.equals(...).
  • Side effects in the right operand. if (cache.isValid() || reload()) never reloads while the cache is valid. That may be intended; make it obvious.
  • Comparing strings with == inside a condition (L16).

Practice · 35 min

  1. Write static String bmiCategory(double bmi) with an else-if chain; reject negative, zero and NaN values with a guard clause.
  2. Refactor a method with four nested ifs (write one, or use discount above with two extra rules) into guard clauses. Keep the behaviour identical and test every path.
  3. Apply De Morgan to !(isOpen && (hasStock || allowsBackorder)) and check your answer against all 8 rows of a truth table, printed by a triple loop over {false, true}.
  4. Predict, then run: Boolean b = null; if (b) {}, if (done = false) {}, and if (Double.NaN > 1 || !(Double.NaN > 1)).
Answer · exercise 3

!isOpen || (!hasStock && !allowsBackorder): the outer && becomes ||, and the inner || becomes && with both operands negated.

Read JLS §14.9 (the if statement) and §15.23–15.24 (conditional-and, conditional-or) · Fowler, Refactoring 2e, “Replace Nested Conditional with Guard Clauses”, “Decompose Conditional”, “Consolidate Conditional Expression” · Javadoc java.util.Objects

L21 · DAY 21

switch: Statements, Expressions & Pattern Matching

45m video45m practice

The classic switch is a jump table with fall-through; the modern one (Java 14 to 21) is an expression that returns a value, never falls through, matches on types and record shapes, and is checked by the compiler for exhaustiveness. Use the modern form by default and the old one only when you mean fall-through.

The classic statement: case, break, default, fall-through

A switch statement evaluates its selector once and jumps to the matching case label, or to default if none matches (with no default, nothing runs). Execution then continues through every following label until a break, return, throw or the closing brace. That fall-through is the language's oldest trap and occasionally exactly what you want:

switch (day) {                       // java.time.DayOfWeek
    case SATURDAY:
    case SUNDAY:                     // stacked labels: harmless fall-through
        System.out.println("weekend");
        break;
    default:
        System.out.println("weekday");
}

int perms = 0;
switch (level) {                     // intentional: each level includes the lower ones
    case 3: perms |= ADMIN;          // fall through
    case 2: perms |= WRITE;          // fall through
    case 1: perms |= READ;
}

Mark every intentional fall-through with a comment and compile with -Xlint:fallthrough so that the unintentional ones produce warnings.

Selector types and what the bytecode does

Without patterns the selector may be char, byte, short, int, their wrappers, String or an enum; not long, float, double or boolean (primitive patterns have been in preview since Java 23). Case labels must be distinct compile-time constants. Dense int labels compile to a tableswitch (an O(1) jump table), sparse ones to a lookupswitch (a sorted key search). A String switch switches on hashCode() and then confirms with equals; an enum switch maps ordinal() through a synthetic array; a pattern switch calls an invokedynamic bootstrap (SwitchBootstraps.typeSwitch).

Switch expressions: arrows and yield

An arrow label case A, B -> runs exactly one expression, block or throw; there is no fall-through and no break. Used as an expression, the switch produces a value. In a block body, yield supplies it (a return there would try to leave the enclosing method and is a compile error).

static int daysIn(Month m, int year) {
    return switch (m) {
        case FEBRUARY -> {
            boolean leap = Year.isLeap(year);
            yield leap ? 29 : 28;
        }
        case APRIL, JUNE, SEPTEMBER, NOVEMBER -> 30;
        case JANUARY, MARCH, MAY, JULY, AUGUST, OCTOBER, DECEMBER -> 31;
    };                                   // no default needed: all 12 constants listed
}

Exhaustiveness

Definition · exhaustive switch

A switch expression, and any switch statement that uses patterns or case null, must cover every possible selector value: all constants of an enum, all permitted subtypes of a sealed type, or a default. When the compiler accepts a switch as exhaustive without a default, it still inserts a hidden one that throws MatchException at runtime (Java 21+), in case a new constant or subtype appears in a class compiled later.

That is why listing every enum constant is better than writing default: add CANCELLED to your own Status enum and every exhaustive switch without a default stops compiling, pointing at each place you must update. A default would silently absorb the new value.

Patterns: types, guards, records, null

Since Java 21 (JEP 441, JEP 440) the selector may be any reference type, and labels may be patterns. A type pattern case String s tests and binds in one step. A guard when adds a boolean condition. A record pattern deconstructs a record into its components, nested to any depth, with var allowed. Labels are tried top to bottom, and the compiler rejects a label that an earlier one dominates (for example case CharSequence cs before case String s).

sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Rect, Square {}
record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}
record Rect(double w, double h) implements Shape {}
record Square(double side) implements Shape {}

static double area(Shape s) {
    return switch (s) {
        case Circle c -> Math.PI * c.r() * c.r();
        case Rect(double w, double h) -> w * h;
        case Square(double side) when side < 0 ->
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("side " + side);
        case Square(var side) -> side * side;
    };                     // exhaustive: Shape is sealed, all three are covered
}

static String describe(Object o) {
    return switch (o) {
        case null -> "null";                      // without this: NullPointerException
        case Integer i when i < 0 -> "negative int " + i;
        case Integer i -> "int " + i;
        case String str when str.isBlank() -> "blank string";
        case String str -> "string of length " + str.length();
        case int[] arr -> "int[" + arr.length + "]";
        default -> o.getClass().getSimpleName();
    };
}

A switch without case null throws NullPointerException on a null selector, as it always has. case null, default -> handles both together.

Refactoring if-else chains into switch

A chain of instanceof tests or equals comparisons against one value is a switch in disguise. The switch version states the selector once, gets dominance and exhaustiveness checks, and can return a value:

// enum Status { NEW, PENDING, DONE, FAILED }
// before
String label;
if (status == Status.NEW || status == Status.PENDING) label = "open";
else if (status == Status.DONE) label = "closed";
else label = "unknown";

// after
String label2 = switch (status) {
    case NEW, PENDING -> "open";
    case DONE -> "closed";
    case FAILED -> "unknown";
};

Keep if when the branches test unrelated conditions (ranges, several variables); switch is for one selector against a set of values or shapes.

What breaks in production
  • A missing break in a colon-style switch: the next case's code runs too. Prefer arrows.
  • Null selectors. A String or enum from a request or database row is null, and the switch throws NPE before checking any label.
  • default on an enum switch expression hides newly added constants; list them all instead.
  • Separate compilation. A library adds a sealed subtype or enum constant, your jar is not recompiled, and the hidden default throws MatchException at runtime.
  • Mixing styles. One switch cannot mix : and -> labels; the compiler rejects it.

Practice · 45 min

  1. Write a classic switch that maps 1..7 to day names, forget one break, observe the output, then rewrite it as a switch expression.
  2. Model sealed interface Payment permits Card, Bank, Wallet with records, and write fee(Payment p) with record patterns and one when guard. Add a fourth record and read the compile error.
  3. Put case CharSequence cs above case String s and read the dominance error; then add case null and pass null.
  4. Take an eight-branch if (o instanceof …) chain and refactor it into a pattern switch.
Answer · exercise 3

javac reports that the String label is dominated by a preceding case label: every String is a CharSequence, so the second label could never match. Swap the order.

Read JLS §14.11 (the switch statement) and §15.28 (switch expressions) · JEP 361, “Switch Expressions” · JEP 441, “Pattern Matching for switch” · JEP 440, “Record Patterns” · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 34 (use enums instead of int constants) · Javadoc java.lang.MatchException

L22 · DAY 22

Loops: for, while & do-while

40m video50m practice

All three loops are the same machine: test a condition, run a body, repeat. They differ only in where the test sits and where the bookkeeping lives. Choose the one whose shape matches the problem, state the loop invariant, and off-by-one errors become something you can check rather than guess.

Anatomy of the classic for

for (init; condition; update) body runs init once, then repeats: test condition, run body, run update. All three parts are optional; an omitted condition means true, so for (;;) loops forever. It is exactly a while loop with the bookkeeping pulled into the header, except that continue in a for still runs the update.

A variable declared in init is scoped to the loop, which is a feature: it cannot leak into later code by accident (Effective Java Item 57). Declare the counter before the loop only when you need its final value afterwards. The header may declare several variables of one type and run several comma-separated update expressions:

static void reverse(int[] a) {
    for (int i = 0, j = a.length - 1; i < j; i++, j--) {
        int t = a[i];
        a[i] = a[j];
        a[j] = t;
    }
}

while, do-while and controlled infinite loops

Use while when the number of iterations is not known in advance and depends on what happens inside: reading until end of input, iterating until convergence, following a chain. Use do-while when the body must run at least once before the test makes sense: a prompt, a first attempt. Note the semicolon after its condition.

long x = 27;
int steps = 0;
while (x != 1) {                     // Collatz: no way to know the count up front
    x = (x % 2 == 0) ? x / 2 : 3 * x + 1;
    steps++;
}                                    // steps == 111

int attempt = 0;
boolean connected;
do {
    attempt++;
    connected = tryConnect();        // must try once before testing
} while (!connected && attempt < 5);

while (true) {                       // deliberate infinite loop
    String cmd = in.readLine();
    if (cmd == null || cmd.equals("quit")) {
        break;                       // EOF or explicit exit
    }
    handle(cmd);
}

A deliberate infinite loop needs a visible exit (break, return, a thrown exception, or a flag another thread sets) and, in servers, a bound: maximum attempts, a deadline, or a check of Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted() so the loop can be shut down.

Loop invariants

Theorem · loop invariant method

If a property P (1) holds before the first test, and (2) is preserved by every execution of the body when the condition is true, then (3) when the loop exits, P and the negated condition both hold. Choose P so that those two facts together are the result you want, and show that some quantity strictly decreases so the loop terminates.

/** Index of the largest element; requires a.length > 0. */
static int indexOfMax(int[] a) {
    int best = 0;
    // invariant: a[best] is a maximum of a[0..i)
    for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
        if (a[i] > a[best]) {
            best = i;
        }
    }
    // exit: i == a.length, so a[best] is a maximum of the whole array
    return best;
}

Initially i = 1 and a[0..1) holds only a[0], so the invariant is true. The body compares one new element and keeps the larger, preserving it. At exit, i == a.length. The decreasing quantity is a.length - i. Writing the invariant as a comment costs one line and documents what the loop is for.

Off-by-one errors, dissected

Most off-by-one bugs are a mix-up between half-open ranges [lo, hi), which contain hi - lo elements, and inclusive ranges [lo, hi], which contain hi - lo + 1. Java's indices, substring, Arrays.copyOfRange and List.subList are all half-open; stick to that convention in your own loops.

TaskCorrectTypical mistake
every index of an arrayi = 0; i < ni <= n: ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException
the numbers 1 to ni = 1; i <= ni < n: n is skipped
backwards over an arrayi = n - 1; i >= 0; i--i > 0: index 0 skipped
n items joined by commasn - 1 separatorsa trailing comma (the fencepost error)
pairs i < jj = i + 1j = i: pairs an element with itself

Two checks catch nearly all of them: run the loop by hand for n = 0 and n = 1, and ask what the counter's value means at exit.

Choosing a loop, and hoisting invariant work

Counted iteration with an index you need: for. Visiting every element without the index: enhanced for (L23). Unknown count, test before: while. Body must run once: do-while. Transforming a collection: often a stream. Whichever you choose, move work that gives the same result every iteration out of the loop. The JIT performs loop-invariant code motion for simple expressions it can prove pure (an array length, a field it knows is unchanged), but not for arbitrary method calls:

// slow: recompiles the regex and recomputes the bound on every pass
for (int d = 2; d <= Math.sqrt(n); d++) { … }
for (String s : lines) { if (s.matches("\\d+")) count++; }

// hoisted
static final Pattern DIGITS = Pattern.compile("\\d+");
for (String s : lines) { if (DIGITS.matcher(s).matches()) count++; }

static boolean isPrime(int n) {
    if (n < 2) {
        return false;
    }
    for (int d = 2; d <= n / d; d++) {   // same as d * d <= n, but cannot overflow
        if (n % d == 0) {
            return false;
        }
    }
    return true;
}
What breaks in production
  • Counter overflow. for (int i = 0; i <= Integer.MAX_VALUE; i++) never ends: i wraps to negative. The same happens with for (byte b = 0; b < 200; b++).
  • Floating-point counters. for (double x = 0; x != 1.0; x += 0.1) never hits exactly 1.0. Loop over an int and compute x = i / 10.0.
  • Changing the counter in the body of a for: the header no longer tells the truth.
  • Busy-waiting. while (!done) {} burns a core, and without volatile it may never see done change. Use a proper wait (later in the concurrency phase).
  • Unbounded retry loops that hammer a failing dependency. Cap attempts and back off.

Practice · 50 min

  1. Rewrite one for loop as a while loop, including a continue. Where must the update go so the behaviour stays identical?
  2. Write static int digitSum(long n) with a while loop and static String readNonEmpty(Scanner sc) with a do-while.
  3. Write static int binarySearch(int[] a, int key) on [lo, hi) with the invariant "if key is present, it is in a[lo..hi)" in a comment. Test empty, one-element and missing-key cases.
  4. Find the bug in for (int i = 1; i < s.length(); i++) out = s.charAt(i) + out; (intended to reverse a string), and fix it.
  5. Time isPrime for all n below 2,000,000 with the bound d <= Math.sqrt(n) in the header versus d <= n / d.
Answer · exercise 4

It starts at 1, so the first char is lost: "abc" becomes "cb". Start at i = 0. (It is also quadratic; use new StringBuilder(s).reverse().)

Read JLS §14.12 (while), §14.13 (do), §14.14.1 (basic for) · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 57 (minimize the scope of local variables) · Cormen et al., Introduction to Algorithms, §2.1 (loop invariants) · Gries, The Science of Programming (the chapters on loops and invariants)

L23 · DAY 23

Enhanced for, break, continue & Labeled Loops

35m video40m practice

The enhanced for loop is syntactic sugar for an index loop over an array or an Iterator loop over an Iterable; knowing that desugaring explains both why you cannot remove elements inside it and why it is still the default way to traverse. break, continue and labels give you exits; nested loops give you quadratic cost, so count iterations before you nest.

What for-each compiles to

Definition · enhanced for (JLS §14.14.2)

For an array expression, for (T x : arr) body means: evaluate arr once into a hidden variable, then for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) { T x = a[i]; body }. For an Iterable, it means for (Iterator<T> it = expr.iterator(); it.hasNext(); ) { T x = it.next(); body }. Anything else does not compile; a null expression throws NullPointerException.

So the loop variable is a fresh local copy on every iteration. Assigning to it changes nothing in the array or collection, and it may be declared final or var. You get no index; if you need one, use the classic loop. Over arrays the two forms compile to essentially the same bytecode, so there is no performance reason to avoid for-each (Effective Java Item 58).

int[] scores = {70, 85, 90};
for (int s : scores) {
    s += 5;                       // modifies the copy only
}
// scores is still [70, 85, 90]

Map<String, Integer> stock = Map.of("apple", 3, "pear", 0);
for (var e : stock.entrySet()) {  // Map is not Iterable; its views are
    System.out.println(e.getKey() + " = " + e.getValue());
}

Why you cannot remove inside for-each

Collections such as ArrayList and HashMap count structural changes in a modCount field. Their iterators record the count when created and check it in next(); if the collection was changed by anything other than the iterator itself, next() throws ConcurrentModificationException. This "fail-fast" check is best-effort, and the hole in it is a classic interview question:

var a = new ArrayList<>(List.of("al", "bob", "cy", "dee"));
for (String n : a) {
    if (n.length() < 3) a.remove(n);   // removes "al", next() throws CME
}

var b = new ArrayList<>(List.of("ann", "bob", "cy", "dee"));
for (String n : b) {
    if (n.length() < 3) b.remove(n);   // removes "cy": no exception, "dee" never visited
}

In the second loop, removing the second-to-last element makes the cursor equal the new size, so hasNext() returns false and the loop ends quietly. Correct ways to remove while traversing:

b.removeIf(n -> n.length() < 3);                    // simplest
for (Iterator<String> it = b.iterator(); it.hasNext(); ) {
    if (it.next().length() < 3) it.remove();         // the iterator's own remove is legal
}
for (int i = b.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--) {             // index loop, backwards
    if (b.get(i).length() < 3) b.remove(i);
}

Replacing an element with list.set(i, v) is not a structural change and does not trip the check, but for-each gives you no i; use replaceAll or an index loop.

break, continue and labels

break leaves the innermost enclosing loop or switch. continue abandons the current iteration of the innermost loop and goes to its next test (in a classic for, via the update). A label such as outer: names a statement; break outer leaves that statement, and continue outer starts the next iteration of that loop. Labels are not goto: you can only jump outward, to the end of an enclosing statement.

int[][] grid = { {4, 8, 1}, {6, 5, 9}, {7, 2, 3} };
int row = -1, col = -1;
search:
for (int r = 0; r < grid.length; r++) {
    for (int c = 0; c < grid[r].length; c++) {
        if (grid[r][c] == 5) {
            row = r;
            col = c;
            break search;              // leaves both loops at once
        }
    }
}

rows:
for (int[] line : grid) {
    for (int v : line) {
        if (v < 0) continue rows;      // skip the rest of a row with bad data
    }
    process(line);
}
When labels are acceptable

A label is fine when the loops are short, the label is a few lines above the jump, and there is one of them. It is a smell when the loop body spans a screen, when several labels interact, or when the same effect is a plain return from an extracted method. The method version is usually clearer:

record Cell(int row, int col) {}

static Optional<Cell> find(int[][] grid, int target) {
    for (int r = 0; r < grid.length; r++) {
        for (int c = 0; c < grid[r].length; c++) {
            if (grid[r][c] == target) {
                return Optional.of(new Cell(r, c));
            }
        }
    }
    return Optional.empty();
}

Nested loop patterns and their cost

Three shapes cover most nested loops. Matrix traversal: rows outside, columns inside, matching how Java stores 2D arrays (L25). Pair generation: for (i = 0; i < n; i++) for (j = i + 1; j < n; j++) visits every unordered pair exactly once. Search and exit: stop at the first hit with return or a labeled break.

ShapeIterationsGrowth
two loops in sequence, n each2nO(n)
inner 0..m inside outer 0..nn·mO(nm)
pairs, inner from i + 1n(n - 1)/2O(n²)
outer 0..n, inner j *= 2 up to nabout n·log₂ nO(n log n)
three nested loops over nn³O(n³)

As a rough guide a JVM does on the order of 10⁸ to 10⁹ simple operations per second, so an O(n²) loop is fine for n = 10³, slow for n = 10⁵ (10¹⁰ steps) and hopeless beyond.

What breaks in production
  • Hidden nested loops. for (Order o : orders) if (vipIds.contains(o.customerId())) is O(n·m) when vipIds is a List. Make it a HashSet and the loop is O(n).
  • Removing inside for-each, with or without the exception (see above). Use removeIf.
  • Assigning to the loop variable and expecting the array to change.
  • for-each over a nullable collection from a DTO: NPE. Return empty collections, never null (Effective Java Item 54).

Practice · 40 min

  1. Write both CME examples above, predict the outcome, run them, then fix each with removeIf and with an explicit Iterator.
  2. Write static List<int[]> pairsWithSum(int[] a, int target) with the j = i + 1 pattern, then an O(n) version with a HashSet. Time both for n = 20,000.
  3. Find the first negative number in a jagged 2D array with a labeled break, then refactor to a method returning Optional<Cell>.
  4. Count, without running it, how many times the body of for (i = 1; i < n; i *= 2) for (j = 0; j < i; j++) runs. Then check with a counter.
Answer · exercise 4

The inner loop runs 1 + 2 + 4 + … up to the largest power of two below n, a geometric sum of less than 2n. It is O(n), not O(n log n).

Read JLS §14.14.2 (enhanced for), §14.7 (labeled statements), §14.15 (break), §14.16 (continue) · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 58 (prefer for-each loops) and Item 54 (return empty collections, not null) · Javadoc ArrayList (fail-fast iterators), ConcurrentModificationException

L24 · DAY 24

Arrays: Declaration, Initialization & Traversal

40m video45m practice

A Java array is a fixed-length object on the heap, and an array variable holds only a reference to it. Every surprise with arrays (aliasing, shallow copies, methods that change your data) follows from that one fact.

Declaring, creating and default values

int[] a = new int[3];               // [0, 0, 0]: elements always start at a default
String[] names = new String[2];     // [null, null]
int[] primes = {2, 3, 5, 7};        // array initializer: only in a declaration
primes = new int[] {11, 13};        // anonymous array: anywhere an expression goes
int[] none = new int[0];            // legal and useful: an empty result
int c[] = {1};                      // C-style, legal, avoid
int[] p, q;                         // two arrays
int r[], s;                         // r is an array, s is a plain int: one reason to avoid C-style
Definition · array object

An array is an object whose class is created by the JVM (int[], String[]…). Its length is fixed when it is created and stored in the object; length is a public final field, not a method (compare s.length() for strings and list.size() for collections). Elements are default-initialized: 0 for numeric types, '\u0000' for char, false for boolean, null for references. A negative size throws NegativeArraySizeException.

On a 64-bit HotSpot JVM an array carries a header of typically 16 bytes (mark word, compressed class pointer, length) before the elements, so new int[1000] occupies about 4,016 bytes, laid out contiguously. That contiguity is why array traversal is fast. Every access is bounds-checked: a[3] on a length-3 array throws ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException: Index 3 out of bounds for length 3. The JIT removes checks it can prove redundant, for example in a loop bounded by a.length.

Reference semantics and copying

int[] b = a; copies the reference, not the array: both names now point at one object, and a write through either is visible through both. To get an independent array you must copy, and there are four ways:

int[] src = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5};

int[] c1 = new int[src.length];
for (int i = 0; i < src.length; i++) c1[i] = src[i];   // manual loop

int[] c2 = new int[src.length];
System.arraycopy(src, 0, c2, 0, src.length);          // native bulk copy into an existing array

int[] c3 = Arrays.copyOf(src, 7);                     // new array: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 0, 0]
int[] c4 = Arrays.copyOfRange(src, 1, 3);             // half-open: [2, 3]
int[] c5 = src.clone();                               // same type, no cast needed

System.arraycopy(src, 0, src, 1, 4);                  // overlap is safe: [1, 1, 2, 3, 4]

System.arraycopy needs a destination that already exists and behaves as if it copied through a temporary buffer, so overlapping ranges work. Arrays.copyOf allocates, and truncates or pads with defaults. clone() is the shortest way to copy a whole array. All four are shallow: for an array of references they copy the references, not the objects they point to.

StringBuilder[] orig = { new StringBuilder("a"), new StringBuilder("b") };
StringBuilder[] copy = orig.clone();
copy[0].append("!");                   // orig[0] is now "a!" as well: same object
copy[1] = new StringBuilder("z");      // orig[1] is still "b": the slots are separate

int[][] grid = { {1, 2}, {3, 4} };
int[][] g2 = grid.clone();
g2[0][0] = 9;                          // grid[0][0] is 9 too: the rows are shared
int[][] deep = Arrays.stream(grid).map(int[]::clone).toArray(int[][]::new);

Arrays as parameters and return values

Passing an array passes a copy of the reference (L26 proves Java is always pass-by-value). The method can therefore change the caller's elements, but assigning a new array to the parameter changes only its local copy:

static void fillWith(int[] arr, int v) { Arrays.fill(arr, v); }   // caller sees the change
static void replace(int[] arr) { arr = new int[] {42}; }          // caller does not

static int[] evens(int n) {                                       // build and return a new array
    int[] out = new int[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) out[i] = 2 * i;
    return out;
}

Return an empty array rather than null so callers can loop without a check (Effective Java Item 54). If a class keeps an array in a private field, copy it on the way in and on the way out; otherwise callers can mutate your internal state through the reference you handed them (Item 50):

public final class Polygon {
    private final int[] xs;
    public Polygon(int[] xs) { this.xs = xs.clone(); }   // copy in
    public int[] xs() { return xs.clone(); }             // copy out
}

Traversal, and a varargs preview

Traverse with for-each when you only read elements, and with an index loop when you need positions, write elements, or walk two arrays together (L22, L23). A varargs parameter int... xs is an int[] inside the method, and the compiler builds the array at the call site; L27 covers the rules.

static int sum(int... xs) {
    int total = 0;
    for (int x : xs) total += x;
    return total;
}
// sum() == 0, sum(1, 2, 3) == 6, sum(new int[] {4, 5}) == 9
What breaks in production
  • Leaking internal arrays. A getter that returns a private array lets any caller change the object's state, including a "constant" public static final String[]. Return a copy or List.of(...).
  • Shallow copies of 2D arrays. grid.clone() before "trying a move" in a game or simulation, and the original board changes.
  • Array covariance. Object[] objs = new String[1]; objs[0] = 42; compiles and throws ArrayStoreException.
  • Printing or comparing arrays directly. println(arr) prints something like [I@1b6d3586, and a.equals(b) is identity. Use Arrays.toString and Arrays.equals (L25).

Practice · 45 min

  1. Print the default element of arrays of all eight primitive types and of String.
  2. Write static int[] append(int[] a, int v) with Arrays.copyOf, and static void rotateRight(int[] a) with one System.arraycopy call on overlapping ranges.
  3. Write fillWith and replace above, call both, and draw the stack and heap before and after each call.
  4. Write a class holding a private int[] with a getter, show that a caller can corrupt it, then fix it with defensive copies.
  5. Copy a 3×3 int[][] with clone() and with the stream deep copy; change one cell in each copy and compare the originals.
Answer · exercise 2 (rotateRight)

Save int last = a[a.length - 1];, then System.arraycopy(a, 0, a, 1, a.length - 1);, then a[0] = last;. Guard against an empty array first.

Read JLS ch. 10 (arrays), especially §10.3 (creation), §10.6 (initializers) and §10.7 (members: length, clone) · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 50 (make defensive copies) and Item 54 (return empty arrays, not null) · Javadoc System.arraycopy, java.util.Arrays

L25 · DAY 25

Multi-Dimensional Arrays & java.util.Arrays

40m video45m practice

Java has no true matrices: int[][] is an array of references to separate int[] rows. That explains jagged arrays, why row-by-row traversal is faster, and why so many java.util.Arrays methods come in a plain and a deep version.

2D arrays are arrays of arrays

Definition · array of arrays

new int[3][4] creates one outer array of 3 references plus 3 separate int[4] row objects. m.length is the number of rows and m[i].length the length of row i. new int[3][] creates only the outer array; every row is null until you assign one. Rows may have different lengths: a jagged array. A rectangular array is just the special case where they are all equal.

static int[][] pascal(int n) {                 // a jagged array built row by row
    int[][] t = new int[n][];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        t[i] = new int[i + 1];
        t[i][0] = t[i][i] = 1;
        for (int j = 1; j < i; j++) {
            t[i][j] = t[i - 1][j - 1] + t[i - 1][j];
        }
    }
    return t;
}
// Arrays.deepToString(pascal(4)) -> [[1], [1, 1], [1, 2, 1], [1, 3, 3, 1]]

Row-major traversal and cache friendliness

Each row's elements are contiguous, so a loop with the column index innermost reads memory sequentially and every 64-byte cache line it loads is fully used. Swapping the loops makes each step jump to a different row object, which wastes most of each cache line and defeats the hardware prefetcher. For large matrices the column-major version is commonly several times slower, for the same arithmetic.

long sum = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < m.length; i++)        // row-major: fast
    for (int j = 0; j < m[i].length; j++)
        sum += m[i][j];

for (int j = 0; j < cols; j++)            // column-major: same result, poor locality
    for (int i = 0; i < m.length; i++)
        sum += m[i][j];

Higher dimensions nest the same way: new double[2][3][4] is 1 + 2 + 6 = 9 array objects. For large dense grids (images, simulations) a single flat array indexed as a[i * cols + j] avoids the extra objects and dereferences entirely.

Printing, comparing and hashing: plain versus deep

Plain methodDeep versionDifference
Arrays.toStringArrays.deepToStringplain prints rows as [I@1b6d3586; deep recurses into them
Arrays.equalsArrays.deepEqualsplain compares row references; deep compares contents
Arrays.hashCodeArrays.deepHashCodepair each with the matching equals

For one-dimensional arrays the plain versions compare and hash element by element, which is what you want; a.equals(b) on an array, by contrast, is inherited from Object and only tests identity.

Sorting and binary search

Two sorting algorithms

Arrays.sort on primitive arrays uses Dual-Pivot Quicksort (Yaroslavskiy, Bentley and Bloch, JDK 7); the Javadoc promises O(n log n) on all inputs. It is not stable, which cannot matter for primitives. On object arrays it uses TimSort, a stable, adaptive merge sort that runs in near-linear time on partially sorted data. Stability is what lets you sort by one key and then another, and it is why objects get the merge sort. Doubles sort by Double.compare: -0.0 before 0.0, NaN last.

record Emp(String name, String dept, int age) {}

Emp[] staff = {
    new Emp("Ada", "eng", 36), new Emp("Bo", "ops", 29), new Emp("Cy", "eng", 29) };
Arrays.sort(staff, Comparator.comparing(Emp::dept).thenComparingInt(Emp::age));
// [Cy eng 29, Ada eng 36, Bo ops 29]

int[] a = {5, 3, 9, 1};
Arrays.sort(a);                   // [1, 3, 5, 9]
Arrays.binarySearch(a, 5);        // 2
Arrays.binarySearch(a, 4);        // -3, i.e. -(insertion point 2) - 1

binarySearch requires the array to be sorted by the same order (the same comparator for objects); on unsorted data the result is undefined, not an exception. A miss returns -(insertionPoint) - 1, which is always negative, so -(r + 1) recovers where to insert. With duplicates, any matching index may be returned.

The rest of java.util.Arrays

int[] b = {1, 2, 3, 4};
Arrays.fill(b, 1, 3, 0);                            // [1, 0, 0, 4]
int[] sq = new int[5];
Arrays.setAll(sq, i -> i * i);                      // [0, 1, 4, 9, 16]
int total = Arrays.stream(sq).sum();                // 30 (IntStream)
Arrays.mismatch(new int[] {1, 2, 3}, new int[] {1, 2, 4});  // 2; -1 if equal
Arrays.compare(new int[] {1, 2}, new int[] {1, 3});        // negative: lexicographic

String[] arr = {"b", "a"};
List<String> view = Arrays.asList(arr);             // fixed-size view backed by arr
view.set(0, "z");                                   // arr[0] is now "z"
// view.add("c");                                   // UnsupportedOperationException
List<int[]> oops = Arrays.asList(new int[] {1, 2}); // size 1: the int[] is the element
List<Integer> ok = Arrays.stream(new int[] {1, 2}).boxed().toList();

mismatch (Java 9) returns the first differing index, or the shorter length when one array is a prefix of the other; compare (Java 9) orders arrays lexicographically using that index. setAll computes each element from its index, and parallelSort/parallelSetAll split large arrays across the common fork-join pool.

What breaks in production
  • Arrays.fill(grid, new int[cols]) puts the same row object in every slot; writing one cell changes a whole column. Allocate each row in a loop.
  • Subtraction comparators. (x, y) -> x - y overflows for large values, and TimSort may throw IllegalArgumentException: Comparison method violates its general contract!. Use Integer.compare or Comparator.comparingInt.
  • binarySearch on unsorted data returns plausible-looking garbage, silently.
  • Arrays.asList treated as a normal list: add throws, and writes go through to the array you thought was separate.
  • equals on 2D arrays in tests passes only when the rows are the same objects; use deepEquals or assertArrayEquals.

Practice · 45 min

  1. Write static int[][] transpose(int[][] m) for rectangular input, and print the result with deepToString.
  2. Sum a 4000×4000 int[][] row-major and column-major; time each three times and report the ratio on your machine.
  3. Sort an array of records by two keys using a stable sort, once with chained comparators and once with two successive sorts (secondary key first). Confirm both give the same order.
  4. Use Arrays.mismatch to write static int commonPrefixLength(char[] a, char[] b).
  5. Reproduce the Arrays.fill(grid, row) bug, then fix it.
Answer · exercise 4

int i = Arrays.mismatch(a, b); return i == -1 ? a.length : i; When one array is a prefix of the other, mismatch already returns the shorter length.

Read Javadoc java.util.Arrays · JLS §10.2 and §15.10.1 (array variables and creation) · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 14 (consider implementing Comparable; avoid subtraction comparators) · Yaroslavskiy 2009, “Dual-Pivot Quicksort” · Peters, listsort.txt (the TimSort design notes in CPython)

L26 · DAY 26

Methods: Signatures, Parameters & Pass-by-Value

40m video40m practice

Every call creates a new stack frame and copies each argument's value into a fresh parameter variable. For an object, the value copied is a reference, so a method can change the object you passed but can never make your variable point somewhere else. That is pass-by-value, and Java has nothing else.

Method anatomy

public static <T> List<T> firstN(List<T> xs, int n) throws IllegalArgumentException {
    if (n < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("n = " + n);
    return xs.subList(0, Math.min(n, xs.size()));
}
PartIn the exampleMeaning
access modifierpublicwho may call it: public, protected, package-private (no keyword), private
other modifiersstaticbelongs to the class; there is no this
type parameters<T>makes it a generic method (later phase)
return typeList<T>type of the result, or void
namefirstNa verb phrase in camelCase
parameters(List<T> xs, int n)each one becomes a new local variable in the frame
throws clausethrows IllegalArgumentExceptionexceptions the caller must handle (checked) or should expect
body{ … }the statements; absent for abstract and native methods
Definition · method signature (JLS §8.4.2)

The signature is the method's name plus its parameter types, in order. The return type, the throws clause, modifiers and parameter names are not part of it. Two methods in one class cannot have the same signature; this is the basis of overloading (L27).

Other modifiers you will meet: final (cannot be overridden), abstract (no body), synchronized (takes the object's lock, later phase), native (implemented in C). A throws clause is required for checked exceptions the body does not handle and optional, as documentation, for unchecked ones.

Calling, void and return

A static method is called on the class (Math.max(a, b)), an instance method on a reference (list.size()). The arguments are evaluated left to right before the call, then the JVM pushes a frame holding the parameters and locals (JVMS §2.6). When the method returns, its frame is discarded along with every local variable in it.

A void method may use a bare return; to leave early. A non-void method must end every path with return expr; or a throw; otherwise javac reports "missing return statement". The returned value is copied back to the caller just like an argument.

Java is always pass-by-value: the proof

public class PassByValue {
    static final class Account {
        long balance;
        Account(long balance) { this.balance = balance; }
    }

    static void bump(int n) { n++; }                              // changes its copy
    static void deposit(Account a, long amt) { a.balance += amt; } // mutates the shared object
    static void reset(Account a) { a = new Account(0); }          // rebinds its copy only
    static void swap(Account x, Account y) { Account t = x; x = y; y = t; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int n = 1;
        bump(n);
        Account acct = new Account(100), other = new Account(7);
        deposit(acct, 50);
        reset(acct);
        swap(acct, other);
        System.out.println(n + " " + acct.balance + " " + other.balance);  // 1 150 7
    }
}

Each line of output is a test. bump did not change n: primitives are copied. deposit did change the balance: the copied reference points at the caller's object. reset and swap did nothing visible: they only rebound their own parameter variables. If Java passed references by reference, swap would work, as it does with C++ Account& or C# ref. No Java method can swap two of the caller's variables.

What copying the reference means in memory

So "mutating" and "reassigning" a parameter are different operations: a.balance = 0 follows the reference, a = … overwrites the local copy. Immutable types such as String, Integer and records with immutable components offer no mutators at all, which is why passing them always feels like passing a value. Declaring parameters final (L29) makes accidental reassignment a compile error.

Method length and single responsibility

A good method does one thing at one level of abstraction, and its name says what. Warning signs: a name containing "and", blank-line-separated sections each with a comment heading, more than about four parameters, and boolean flag parameters that make it do two different things. Fixes are mechanical: extract each section into a named method, group related parameters into a record, replace the flag with two methods or an enum (Effective Java Item 51). Check parameters at the top and fail fast (Item 49), and keep methods that change state separate from methods that answer questions.

// before: one method, three jobs, a flag
void process(Order o, boolean sendEmail) { … validate … price … persist … if (sendEmail) … }

// after: each step named; the caller decides about the email
void place(Order o) {
    validate(o);
    Money total = price(o);
    save(o, total);
}

Javadoc for methods

/**
 * Withdraws money from this account.
 *
 * @param amount the amount in cents; must be positive
 * @return the balance after the withdrawal, in cents
 * @throws IllegalArgumentException if {@code amount <= 0}
 * @throws InsufficientFundsException if the balance is below {@code amount}
 */
public long withdraw(long amount) throws InsufficientFundsException { … }

The first sentence becomes the summary in the generated index, so make it a complete description of what the method does. Document every parameter, the return value, every exception and its condition, and any side effect such as "modifies the given list". Generate the HTML with javadoc -d docs or mvn javadoc:javadoc and read it as a caller would (Item 56).

Interview trap
  • "Objects are passed by reference." Wrong: references are passed by value. Prove it with swap.
  • "So a method cannot change my object." Also wrong: it can mutate it through the copied reference. A helper that calls Collections.sort(list) on your argument reorders your list.
  • "The return type is part of the signature." No; int f() and long f() in one class do not compile.
  • "Changing a String parameter changes the caller's string." Strings are immutable; s = s.trim() rebinds the local copy only.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Type and run PassByValue; before running, predict each number and draw the stack and heap after each call.
  2. Write static void appendAll(List<String> target, String... items) and static List<String> withAll(List<String> source, String... items): one mutates, one returns a new list. Document the difference in Javadoc.
  3. Take a 60-line method from an old project (or write one that reads, validates, computes and prints) and split it into methods of at most 15 lines.
  4. Write Javadoc with @param, @return and @throws for three methods and generate the HTML with javadoc.
Answer · exercise 1

Output 1 150 7. acct still points at the original account (now 150) and other still at the account holding 7; the Account(0) made in reset is unreachable once its frame is popped.

Read JLS §8.4 (method declarations), §8.4.2 (signature), §15.12.4 (run-time evaluation of invocation) · JVMS §2.6 (frames) · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Items 49, 51 and 56 · Oracle, “How to Write Doc Comments for the Javadoc Tool”

L27 · DAY 27

Overloading, Varargs & Method Resolution

40m video40m practice

Overloading is resolved entirely by the compiler, from the static types of the arguments, in three phases that try progressively looser conversions. Once you can run those phases in your head, the surprising cases (remove(int), valueOf(null), a long beating an Integer) become predictable.

Overloading rules

Two methods with the same name overload each other when their parameter type lists differ in number, type or order. Because the signature is name plus parameter types (L26), methods that differ only in return type, throws clause or parameter names are duplicates and do not compile. Overloads may have different return types and modifiers; that is just not what distinguishes them. Constructors overload by the same rules.

int  parse(String s)            { … }
int  parse(String s, int radix) { … }   // OK: different arity
long parse(String s)            { … }   // error: already defined (return type does not count)

Compile-time resolution versus runtime dispatch

Definition · overload resolution versus overriding

Which overload to call is decided at compile time from the declared (static) types of the arguments, and written into the bytecode as a fixed method descriptor. Which override of that one method runs is decided at run time from the actual class of the receiver object. Overloading looks at argument types; overriding looks at the receiver.

static String kind(Object o) { return "object"; }
static String kind(String s) { return "string"; }

Object o = "hi";
kind(o);          // "object": the static type of o is Object
kind("hi");       // "string"
o.toString();     // runs String.toString(): dynamic dispatch on the receiver

This is why Effective Java Item 52 warns against overloads with the same number of parameters whose types a caller could confuse: passing a value through a variable of a wider type silently selects a different method.

The three-phase resolution algorithm
