Within the winning phase, the most specific method is chosen: m1 is more specific than m2 if every argument list m1 accepts would also be accepted by m2. An exact match is therefore preferred over a widening one in phase 1, which gives the familiar order exact → widening → boxing → varargs.

static String f(long x)    { return "long"; }
static String f(Integer x) { return "Integer"; }
static String f(int... x)  { return "varargs"; }

f(5);                  // "long": widening (phase 1) beats boxing (phase 2)
f(Integer.valueOf(5)); // "Integer": exact in phase 1
f();                   // "varargs": only phase 3 can match zero arguments

static String g(Integer a, int b) { return "A"; }
static String g(int a, Integer b) { return "B"; }
// g(1, 2);            // error: reference to g is ambiguous (both need one boxing)

static String h(Object o) { return "Object"; }
static String h(String s) { return "String"; }
h(null);               // "String": String is more specific than Object

Varargs: syntax, restrictions and the array underneath

T... xs may appear only once and only as the last parameter. Inside the method xs is a T[]; at each call site the compiler creates the array, so sum(1, 2, 3) compiles to sum(new int[] {1, 2, 3}) and sum() passes an empty array, never null. You may also pass an existing array directly. Each call allocates, which is why List.of and EnumSet.of provide fixed-arity overloads for small counts and fall back to varargs only beyond them (Item 53). To require at least one argument, say so in the signature:

static int min(int first, int... rest) {     // min() does not compile
    int m = first;
    for (int r : rest) m = Math.min(m, r);
    return m;
}

static int count(Object... xs) { return xs == null ? -1 : xs.length; }
count();                   // 0
count(null, null);         // 2: two null elements
count((Object[]) null);    // -1: the array itself is null (javac warns on a bare count(null))

Generic varargs, heap pollution and @SafeVarargs

Definition · heap pollution (JLS §4.12.2)

Heap pollution is a variable of a parameterized type, such as List<String>, referring to an object that is not of that type. Generic varargs make it possible because the compiler must create an array of a non-reifiable type (List<String>[]), whose element type is checked only as List at run time. javac warns "possible heap pollution from parameterized vararg type".

static void dangerous(List<String>... lists) {
    Object[] objs = lists;
    objs[0] = List.of(42);              // no ArrayStoreException: erased type is List
    String s = lists[0].get(0);         // ClassCastException, far from the cause
}

@SafeVarargs                            // a promise, checked by no one
static <T> List<T> flatten(List<? extends T>... lists) {
    List<T> out = new ArrayList<>();
    for (List<? extends T> l : lists) out.addAll(l);
    return out;
}

A generic varargs method is safe when it neither stores anything into the varargs array nor lets a reference to the array escape (returning it, or passing it to code you do not control). Then annotate it with @SafeVarargs, which is allowed on constructors and on static, final and (since Java 9) private methods, the ones that cannot be overridden (Item 32).

What breaks in production
  • List<Integer>.remove(1) removes the element at index 1: remove(int) matches in phase 1, remove(Object) would need boxing. Write list.remove(Integer.valueOf(1)) to remove the value. A Set<Integer> has only remove(Object), so the same line behaves differently there.
  • String.valueOf(null) selects valueOf(char[]), more specific than valueOf(Object), and throws NPE.
  • Refactoring a variable's declared type from String to Object silently switches every overloaded call it is passed to.
  • Overloads that differ only by boxing (int vs Integer) invite ambiguity errors and surprises; give them different names (writeInt, writeLong).

Practice · 40 min

  1. Declare f(long), f(Integer), f(Object) and f(int...). Predict and check f(5), f((short) 5), f(5L), f("x"), f(). Then delete one overload at a time and repeat.
  2. Fill a List<Integer> and a Set<Integer> with -3..2 and call remove(i) on both for i = 0, 1, 2. Explain both results.
  3. Write static double average(double first, double... rest) and a generic @SafeVarargs static <T> Set<T> setOf(T... xs). Explain why the annotation is justified.
  4. Make the ambiguous g(1, 2) call compile in two different ways.
Answer · exercise 2

The set becomes [-3, -2, -1]: remove(Object) removes the values 0, 1, 2. The list becomes [-2, 0, 2]: remove(int) removes by index, and each removal shifts the later elements left (the example from Effective Java Item 52).

Read JLS §15.12.2 (choosing the method signature), §15.12.2.5 (most specific method), §8.4.1 (variable arity parameters), §4.12.2 (heap pollution) · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 52 (use overloading judiciously), Item 53 (use varargs judiciously), Item 32 (combine generics and varargs judiciously) · Javadoc java.lang.SafeVarargs, java.util.List

L28 · DAY 28

Recursion: Stack Frames, Tail Calls & Memoization

45m video55m practice

A recursive call is an ordinary call: it pushes a new frame onto a fixed-size thread stack. Recursion is the clearest way to express self-similar problems (trees, directories, divide and conquer), but in Java its depth is bounded by that stack and its cost by how often it repeats work, so you must know when to memoize and when to switch to a loop.

The call stack, frame by frame

Each thread has its own stack (JVMS §2.5.2). A call pushes a frame holding the method's parameters, local variables, an operand stack and the return address; a return pops it and hands the value to the frame below. A recursive method has many frames of the same method alive at once, each with its own copy of n.

Definition · well-founded recursion

A recursive method needs a base case that returns without recursing, and a recursive case that calls itself on an input strictly closer to a base case. Without both, the calls never stop and the stack overflows.

The classic four

static long factorial(int n) {              // 20! is the largest that fits in a long
    if (n < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("n = " + n);
    if (n <= 1) return 1;                    // base case
    return n * factorial(n - 1);             // recursive case
}

static long fibNaive(int n) {               // exponential: see below
    return n < 2 ? n : fibNaive(n - 1) + fibNaive(n - 2);
}

static long pow(long x, int n) {            // n >= 0; about log2(n) calls
    if (n == 0) return 1;
    long half = pow(x, n / 2);
    return n % 2 == 0 ? half * half : half * half * x;
}

static int gcd(int a, int b) {              // a, b >= 0; Euclid: O(log min(a, b))
    return b == 0 ? a : gcd(b, a % b);
}

pow halves n on each call, so 230 takes about 30 frames instead of a billion multiplications. gcd works because gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, a mod b) and the second argument shrinks at least by half every two steps.

StackOverflowError and -Xss

The stack has a fixed size, set per thread when the thread starts. On 64-bit HotSpot the default is typically 1 MB, which allows somewhere between several thousand and a few tens of thousands of frames depending on frame size and whether the code is interpreted or JIT-compiled. Exceed it and the JVM throws StackOverflowError, a VirtualMachineError: catching it is legal but the program state afterwards is suspect.

java -Xss8m App                      # larger stacks for all new threads, including main
java -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal -version | grep ThreadStackSize   # the default, in KB

You can also request a size for one thread with new Thread(null, task, "deep", 64L << 20); the Javadoc calls the size a hint the platform may ignore. Raising the limit treats the symptom: if depth grows with input size, a large enough input will still overflow.

Recursion on trees and directories, and the explicit stack

Recursion fits data that is itself recursive: a directory contains files and directories, a tree node contains subtrees.

static long sizeOf(Path p) throws IOException {
    if (!Files.isDirectory(p, LinkOption.NOFOLLOW_LINKS)) {
        return Files.size(p);                          // base case: a file
    }
    long total = 0;
    try (DirectoryStream<Path> children = Files.newDirectoryStream(p)) {
        for (Path c : children) total += sizeOf(c);    // one call per child
    }
    return total;
}

record Node(int val, Node left, Node right) {}

static int sumRec(Node n) {
    return n == null ? 0 : n.val() + sumRec(n.left()) + sumRec(n.right());
}

static int sumIter(Node root) {                        // same result, heap-allocated stack
    int sum = 0;
    Deque<Node> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();            // ArrayDeque rejects nulls
    if (root != null) stack.push(root);
    while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
        Node n = stack.pop();
        sum += n.val();
        if (n.right() != null) stack.push(n.right());
        if (n.left() != null) stack.push(n.left());
    }
    return sum;
}

Converting to iteration means doing yourself what the JVM did for you: push the pending work onto an ArrayDeque, and loop while it is non-empty. The deque lives on the heap, so depth is limited by memory rather than by -Xss. That matters for degenerate inputs: a "tree" that is really a 100,000-node linked list overflows sumRec but not sumIter.

ConcernRecursionIteration
clarity on trees, divide and conquerusually clearerbookkeeping obscures the idea
maximum depthbounded by thread stackbounded by heap
per-step costa call and a framea loop jump (plus deque operations)
linear problems (sum a list)pointless riskthe natural choice

Why there is no tail-call optimization

A tail call is a call whose result is returned directly, as in gcd. A language with tail-call elimination reuses the current frame, so tail recursion runs in constant stack space. The JVM specification has no tail-call instruction and HotSpot does not perform the optimization, so every call in Java gets a frame. Reasons commonly given: stack frames are used for stack traces and were historically used for security checks, and the feature was never prioritized. Scala (@tailrec) and Kotlin (tailrec) work around it by compiling self-tail-recursion into a loop. In Java, write the loop yourself:

static int gcdLoop(int a, int b) {
    while (b != 0) { int t = a % b; a = b; b = t; }
    return a;
}

Memoization, mutual recursion and a backtracking preview

fibNaive(n) makes 2·F(n+1) − 1 calls, which grows like 1.618n: fibNaive(40) makes about 331 million calls, because F(38) is computed twice, F(37) three times, and so on. Memoization stores each result the first time it is computed:

static long fibMemo(int n, long[] memo) {
    if (n < 2) return n;
    if (memo[n] != 0) return memo[n];
    return memo[n] = fibMemo(n - 1, memo) + fibMemo(n - 2, memo);
}
// fibMemo(90, new long[91]) == 2880067194370816120 after 179 calls

static boolean isEven(int n) { return n == 0 || isOdd(n - 1); }   // mutual recursion, n >= 0
static boolean isOdd(int n)  { return n != 0 && isEven(n - 1); }

Mutual recursion, where methods call each other, is the shape of recursive-descent parsers (expression calls term calls factor calls expression). Backtracking is recursion over choices: choose, explore, un-choose. Here it enumerates all 2n subsets; later phases use the same skeleton for permutations and N-Queens.

static void subsets(int[] a, int i, Deque<Integer> chosen, List<List<Integer>> out) {
    if (i == a.length) { out.add(List.copyOf(chosen)); return; }
    subsets(a, i + 1, chosen, out);       // leave a[i] out
    chosen.addLast(a[i]);                 // choose
    subsets(a, i + 1, chosen, out);       // explore
    chosen.removeLast();                  // un-choose
}
What breaks in production
  • Depth proportional to input. Recursing over a linked list, a deeply nested JSON document or a long chain of parent IDs works in tests and throws StackOverflowError on the first big customer. Use an explicit stack.
  • Memoizing with HashMap.computeIfAbsent whose mapping function recurses into the same map: since Java 9 this throws ConcurrentModificationException. Use an array, or get then put.
  • Symlink loops in directory recursion: follow links and the walk never ends. Use NOFOLLOW_LINKS or Files.walkFileTree, which detects cycles when following links.
  • Catching StackOverflowError to "retry": the stack was blown in the middle of arbitrary code, possibly holding locks or half-updated state.

Practice · 55 min

  1. Find your machine's maximum depth: recurse with a counter until StackOverflowError, print the count, then repeat with -Xss4m and with an extra long local in the method.
  2. Count the calls fibNaive makes for n = 20, 30, 35 with a static counter, and compare with 2·F(n+1) − 1. Then time fibMemo and an iterative two-variable version for n = 90.
  3. Write sizeOf above, run it on a real folder, and compare with Files.walk(p).filter(Files::isRegularFile) summing sizes.
  4. Convert sumRec's in-order variant (left, node, right) to iteration with an explicit stack. Test it on a 100,000-node left-leaning chain.
  5. Write permutations(String s) with choose/explore/un-choose and check that "abc" gives 6 results.
Answer · exercise 4 (sketch)

Keep a pointer cur = root. Loop while cur != null || !stack.isEmpty(): push and go left while cur != null; then pop, visit the popped node, and set cur to its right child.

Read JVMS §2.5.2 (JVM stacks) and §2.6 (frames) · Javadoc StackOverflowError, Thread(ThreadGroup, Runnable, String, long), Files.walkFileTree · Abelson & Sussman, Structure and Interpretation of Computer Programs, §1.2 (linear and tree recursion, iteration) · Sedgewick & Wayne, Algorithms 4e, §1.1 (recursion)

L29 · DAY 29

Variable Scope, Shadowing & final

30m video30m practice

Scope is a compile-time question (where in the source text can this name be used?) and lifetime is a run-time one (how long does the storage exist?). Keep scopes small, never let a local silently hide a field, and make final your default so that most variables mean one thing for their whole life.

Four scopes, and lifetime versus visibility

Definition · scope and lifetime

The scope of a declaration is the region of program text where its simple name refers to it (JLS §6.3). A local variable's scope runs from its declaration to the end of its block; a parameter's covers the whole method body; a field's covers the whole class body. Lifetime is how long the storage exists: a local lives while its frame does, an instance field as long as its object is reachable, a static field as long as its class is loaded (for application classes, usually the life of the JVM).

The two differ in both directions. A field is visible in every method but has no storage until an object exists. An object created in a method and returned outlives the local variable that referred to it. A lambda that captures a local keeps a copy of its value after the frame is gone.

public class Counter {
    private static int instances;           // class scope: one per class
    private int count;                      // instance scope: one per object

    public void addAll(int[] values) {      // method scope
        for (int v : values) {              // loop scope
            int doubled = v * 2;            // block scope
            count += doubled;
        }
        // System.out.println(doubled);     // error: cannot find symbol
        // int count = 0; would shadow the field, legal but confusing
    }
}

Shadowing: locals, fields and the this. fix

A local variable or parameter with the same name as a field shadows it: inside that scope, the simple name means the local (JLS §6.4.1). The idiom this.name = name in constructors and setters relies on exactly that. Java does not allow a local to shadow another local or parameter of the same method; that is a compile error ("variable x is already defined").

public class Label {
    private String text;

    public void setText(String text) {
        text = text;            // assigns the parameter to itself: the field stays null
    }
    public void setTextFixed(String text) {
        this.text = text;       // this. names the field explicitly
    }
}

The first setter compiles; IDEs and linters flag it as a self-assignment. Declaring the parameter final turns the bug into a compile error.

Shadowing in inner classes and lambdas

public class Outer {
    int x = 1;

    class Inner {
        int x = 2;
        void show(int x) {                      // x == 3 when called with 3
            System.out.println(x);              // 3: the parameter
            System.out.println(this.x);         // 2: Inner's field
            System.out.println(Outer.this.x);   // 1: the enclosing instance's field
        }
    }
}

An inner or anonymous class opens a new scope, so its members can shadow outer names, and this inside it means the inner object. A lambda does not open a new scope for names: a lambda parameter cannot reuse the name of a local in the enclosing method, and this inside a lambda means the enclosing instance.

final on locals, parameters, fields, methods and classes

Applied toMeaningNote
local variableassigned exactly oncethe assignment may be deferred, e.g. in both branches of an if
parametercannot be reassigned in the bodyprevents the setter bug above
instance fieldassigned exactly once, by the end of every constructorenables safe publication across threads (JLS §17.5)
static fieldassigned once, in its initializer or a static blockwith a constant initializer it becomes a constant variable
methodcannot be overriddenprotects invariants that subclasses must not break
classcannot be extendedString, Integer and all records are final

final fixes the variable, not the object: final List<String> names = new ArrayList<>(); can still be filled and cleared, it just cannot be pointed at another list. An immutable object needs final fields and no way to change what they refer to.

Effectively final and lambdas

Definition · effectively final

A local variable or parameter is effectively final if it is never assigned after its initialization (JLS §4.12.4). Lambdas, and local or anonymous classes, may only use enclosing locals that are final or effectively final.

The reason is that a lambda captures the value of the local, a copy stored in the lambda object, because the lambda may run after the method has returned and its frame is gone, possibly on another thread. If the local could change afterwards, the copy and the original would silently disagree. Java forbids the situation instead of defining confusing semantics.

String prefix = "id-";                               // effectively final
Function<Integer, String> tag = n -> prefix + n;     // OK: captures "id-"

int total = 0;
// List.of(1, 2, 3).forEach(n -> total += n);        // error: must be final or effectively final
int sum = List.of(1, 2, 3).stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).sum();  // restructure instead

static final constants, inlining, and immutability by default

A static final field of primitive or String type initialized with a constant expression is a constant variable. javac copies its value into every class that uses it, so the using class no longer references the field at all (JLS §13.4.9).

public class Limits {
    public static final int MAX_USERS = 100;                   // inlined into callers
    public static final String VERSION = "2.1";                // inlined into callers
    public static final int MAX_CONN = Integer.parseInt("50"); // not constant: read at run time
    public static final List<String> ROLES = List.of("admin", "user");  // immutable list
}

Treat immutability as the default: private final fields, records for data carriers, List.copyOf for incoming collections, and setters only where a real requirement needs them. Immutable objects are thread-safe without locks, can be shared and cached freely, and have only one state to reason about (Effective Java Item 17). Scope minimization is the same discipline for locals: declare each where it is first used (Item 57).

What breaks in production
  • Stale inlined constants. A library changes MAX_USERS to 200 and ships a new jar; your code, not recompiled, still uses 100. For values that may change, use a method or a non-constant initializer.
  • Mutable static state. A static counter or cache is shared by every request and every test; it leaks data between tests and races between threads.
  • "Final" collections exposed publicly. public static final List<String> ADMINS = new ArrayList<>() can be modified by anyone. Use List.of.
  • Self-assignment in setters (text = text), invisible until a null shows up far away.

Practice · 30 min

  1. Write Outer/Inner above and add an anonymous Runnable and a lambda inside show that each print this. Explain the difference in output.
  2. Make every parameter and local in one of your earlier classes final. How many compile errors appear, and does each point at a real reassignment?
  3. Compile two classes, one using Limits.MAX_USERS. Change the constant, recompile only Limits, and run. Then inspect the user class with javap -c.
  4. Rewrite a mutable Point class with setters as a record and update its callers.
Answer · exercise 3

The program still prints the old value. javap -c shows bipush 100 (a literal in the user class), not a getstatic on Limits.

Read JLS §6.3 (scope), §6.4 (shadowing and obscuring), §4.12.4 (final, effectively final and constant variables), §13.4.9 (final fields and constants in binary compatibility), §17.5 (final field semantics) · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 17 (minimize mutability) and Item 57 (minimize the scope of local variables)

L30 · DAY 30

Classes, Objects, References & Memory

45m video50m practice

A class describes a kind of object; new creates one on the heap and hands you a reference to it. Variables never contain objects, only references, so assignment shares, == asks "same object?", and an object lives exactly as long as something can still reach it.

Class as blueprint, object as instance

Definition · class and object

A class declares a type: its fields (the state each object carries) and its methods (the behaviour that operates on that state). An object is one instance of a class, with its own copy of every instance field. Method code exists once per class, not once per object; when you call acct.deposit(500), the reference acct is passed to the method as the hidden parameter this.

What new does, and where things live

new BankAccount("Ada", 10_000) performs four steps (JLS §12.5): (1) allocate heap space for an object header plus all instance fields; (2) set every field to its default (0, false, null); (3) run the constructor chain, including field initializers (L31); (4) yield a reference to the new object. HotSpot usually allocates from a thread-local allocation buffer by bumping a pointer, so allocation is cheap; the header is typically 12 to 16 bytes on a 64-bit JVM.

Local variables live in the method's stack frame. A local of a primitive type holds the value itself; a local of a class type holds a reference, typically 4 bytes with compressed pointers. The object it refers to is on the heap (the JIT may optimize away allocations that never escape a method, but the semantics are always as if they were on the heap).

Aliasing, null, and identity versus equality

BankAccount a = new BankAccount("Ada", 10_000);
BankAccount b = a;                  // alias: copies the reference, not the object
b.deposit(500);
System.out.println(a.balanceCents());      // 10500: one object, two names
BankAccount c = new BankAccount("Ada", 10_500);
BankAccount d = null;

System.out.println(a == b);         // true: same object (identity)
System.out.println(a == c);         // false: different objects
System.out.println(a.equals(c));    // false: Object.equals is identity unless overridden
d.deposit(1);                       // NullPointerException

== on references always tests identity (JLS §15.21.3). equals tests whatever the class defines as equality; the version inherited from Object is identity, String compares characters, and records compare their components. Whether a class should override it is a design decision: a bank account is an entity, two accounts with equal balances are still different accounts, so identity is right. A Money amount is a value, and should be a record with value equality. The full equals/hashCode contract comes later in this phase.

null is the reference to no object, and the default for reference fields. Dereferencing it throws NullPointerException; since JDK 15 the message names the culprit, for example Cannot invoke "BankAccount.deposit(long)" because "d" is null (JEP 358; local variable names appear only when the class was compiled with -g, otherwise you see placeholders such as "<local4>").

Garbage collection preview: unreachable means collectable

The JVM frees an object once it is unreachable: no chain of references leads to it from a GC root (locals in live stack frames, static fields, a few JVM-internal roots). After a = null; b = null; account 1 is unreachable and may be reclaimed at any later time. Reachability, not reference counting, decides, so cycles of objects that only point to each other are collected too. There are no destructors: you cannot know when collection happens, finalizers are deprecated for removal (JEP 421), and files or sockets must be closed with try-with-resources. The inverse also holds: an object still referenced from a static map is never collected, which is how Java programs leak memory (Effective Java Item 7).

Designing a first real class: BankAccount

import java.util.Objects;

public class BankAccount {
    private static long nextId = 1;            // one counter shared by all accounts

    private final long id;
    private final String owner;
    private long balanceCents;                 // money as whole cents, never double

    public BankAccount(String owner, long openingCents) {
        this.owner = Objects.requireNonNull(owner, "owner");
        if (openingCents < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("opening balance < 0");
        this.id = nextId++;
        this.balanceCents = openingCents;
    }

    public void deposit(long cents) {
        if (cents <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("deposit must be positive");
        balanceCents = Math.addExact(balanceCents, cents);   // throws on overflow
    }

    public void withdraw(long cents) {
        if (cents <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("withdrawal must be positive");
        if (cents > balanceCents) throw new IllegalStateException("insufficient funds");
        balanceCents -= cents;
    }

    public long id() { return id; }
    public String owner() { return owner; }
    public long balanceCents() { return balanceCents; }

    @Override
    public String toString() {
        return "BankAccount[id=%d, owner=%s, balance=%d.%02d]"
                .formatted(id, owner, balanceCents / 100, balanceCents % 100);
    }
}

The design choices are the lesson: fields are private so every change goes through a method that checks it; id and owner are final because they never change (L29); there is no setBalance, because a balance changes only through deposits and withdrawals; the constructor rejects invalid state so that no invalid account can ever exist; money is an integer count of cents because double cannot represent 0.10 exactly.

UML class diagram basics

A class diagram is for communicating structure, not for generating code: show the members that matter for the discussion, the associations with multiplicities (1, 0..1, 0..*), and generalization (a hollow triangle arrow, coming with inheritance). Sketch one before writing a class with more than a couple of collaborators.

What breaks in production
  • Accidental aliasing. Adding the same mutable object to a list twice, or storing a caller's object and later mutating it, changes data in two places at once.
  • nextId++ from several threads can hand out duplicate IDs; it is a read, an add and a write. Use AtomicLong or let the database assign IDs.
  • Money in double. 0.1 + 0.2 is 0.30000000000000004; balances drift. Use long cents or BigDecimal.
  • Leaks through reachability. A static cache or listener list that is never cleared keeps every object it references alive.

Practice · 50 min

  1. Type in BankAccount and the aliasing snippet. Before running, draw the stack and heap after each line; then run and compare.
  2. Add transferTo(BankAccount target, long cents). Decide what happens when target == this, and make the method leave both balances unchanged if the withdrawal fails.
  3. Write record Money(long cents) with validation in its compact constructor, and show that new Money(5).equals(new Money(5)) is true while two equal BankAccounts are not equal.
  4. Draw a UML diagram for Bank, Customer, BankAccount and Transaction with multiplicities, then write the four class skeletons from it.
  5. Create 1,000,000 accounts in a static list, then clear the list; watch heap usage with jconsole or jcmd <pid> GC.heap_info before and after a System.gc() hint.
Answer · exercise 2 (sketch)

Validate first: reject null, non-positive amounts and target == this. Then call this.withdraw(cents), which throws before any change if funds are short, and only then target.deposit(cents). Making it safe under concurrency needs locking, which comes in the concurrency phase.

Read JLS §4.3.1 (objects), §12.5 (creation of new class instances), §15.21.3 (reference equality) · JVMS §2.5.3 (heap) · JEP 358, “Helpful NullPointerExceptions” · Bloch, Effective Java 3e, Item 7 (eliminate obsolete object references) and Item 8 (avoid finalizers and cleaners) · Fowler, UML Distilled 3e, ch. 3 (class diagrams)

L31 · DAY 31

Constructors, this() & Constructor Chaining

40m video40m practice

A constructor is not a method that happens to run after new; it is one link in a fixed chain that ends at Object(), and the language decides exactly when fields get their values along that chain. Funnel every constructor into one canonical constructor and the rules stop being surprising.

The default constructor, and when it disappears

Definition · default constructor

If a class declares no constructors, the compiler adds one: no parameters, the same access as the class (a public class gets a public one), and a body that is just super();. The moment you declare any constructor, with or without parameters, the compiler adds nothing (JLS §8.8.9).

That second sentence is the source of a whole family of bugs. Add Book(String isbn) to a class that used to have no constructors and every new Book() in the code base stops compiling, every subclass whose constructor relied on the implicit super() stops compiling (L35), and frameworks that instantiate your class reflectively, such as JPA and Jackson, start failing at runtime. If you still need a no-argument constructor, you now have to write it yourself.

Parameterized and overloaded constructors

A parameterized constructor lets a caller supply the state an object needs to be valid on day one. Overloaded constructors follow the overloading rules of L27: they must differ in parameter types, and the compiler picks one at compile time. The discipline that keeps overloaded constructors sane is to choose one canonical constructor that takes every field, validates, and assigns, and to make every other constructor delegate to it with this(...). Validation then lives in exactly one place.

public class Pizza {
    private static int created;                     // class-level (L33)
    private final String size;
    private final boolean extraCheese;
    private final List<String> toppings = new ArrayList<>(); // initializer

    { created++; }                                  // initializer block

    public Pizza(String size) {
        this(size, false);              // delegate: must be the first statement
    }

    public Pizza(String size, boolean extraCheese) {   // canonical constructor
        // implicit super() runs here, then the initializer and the block
        if (!Set.of("S", "M", "L").contains(size))
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("size " + size);
        this.size = size;
        this.extraCheese = extraCheese;
    }

    public Pizza(Pizza other) {                     // copy constructor
        this(other.size, other.extraCheese);
        this.toppings.addAll(other.toppings);       // copy, don't share
    }

    public Pizza addTopping(String t) {             // returning `this`
        toppings.add(t);                            // enables chaining
        return this;
    }
    public static int created() { return created; }
}

this(...) delegation rules

  • First statement. In Java 21, an explicit constructor call, this(...) or super(...), must be the very first statement of the body (JLS §8.8.7.1). Java 25 relaxes this with flexible constructor bodies (JEP 513): statements that don't touch the object under construction, such as argument checks, may come before the call. Code written for a Java 21 baseline must still put the call first.
  • One or the other. A constructor calls either this(...) or super(...), never both. If it calls neither, the compiler inserts super().
  • No cycles. A() { this(1); } plus A(int x) { this(); } is a compile error: recursive constructor invocation.
  • No instance access in the arguments. this(computeDefault()) compiles only if computeDefault is static; the object doesn't exist yet in any useful sense.

this, the current instance

Inside an instance method or constructor, this is a reference to the object the code is running on. You use it in three ways: to disambiguate a field from a parameter that shadows it (this.size = size), to pass the current object somewhere (registry.add(this)), and to return it for fluent chaining (addTopping above). It doesn't exist in static code, because static code has no current instance. Passing this out from a constructor is legal but dangerous; see the box below.

Initialization order inside one class

When new Pizza("L") runs, the JVM allocates the object with every field at its default value (0, false, null), then runs the constructor chain. Field initializers and instance initializer blocks are executed, in textual order, by the constructor that calls super(...), right after that call returns. A constructor that starts with this(...) skips them, which is how they run exactly once (JLS §12.5).

class Order {
    private final String a = log("field a");
    { log("initializer block"); }
    private final String b = log("field b");

    Order()      { this(0); log("Order() body"); }
    Order(int x) { log("Order(int) body"); }

    static String log(String s) { System.out.println(s); return s; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        new Order();
        // field a / initializer block / field b / Order(int) body / Order() body
    }
}

Copy constructors, private constructors, and a builder preview

A copy constructor (Pizza(Pizza other)) is the readable, type-safe way to duplicate an object, and it is what L44 recommends instead of clone(). Decide field by field whether to copy deeply: immutable fields (String, records) can be shared; mutable ones (lists, arrays, dates) must be copied, or both objects will mutate the same list.

A private constructor means "only this class may create instances". Two uses: a utility class that should never be instantiated (L33), and a singleton that hands out one shared instance through a static accessor (Effective Java Items 3–4).

final class IdGenerator {
    private static final IdGenerator INSTANCE = new IdGenerator();
    private long next = 1;

    private IdGenerator() {
        if (INSTANCE != null)                   // blocks a reflective second copy
            throw new IllegalStateException("singleton");
    }
    static IdGenerator instance() { return INSTANCE; }
    synchronized long nextId()    { return next++; }
}

Once a class has five or more parameters, several of them optional, overloaded constructors turn into telescoping constructors: new SearchQuery("java", 20, 0, true, false, "title"), where nobody can tell what true means and two adjacent booleans can be swapped silently. The builder pattern (Effective Java Item 2) replaces this with named steps, SearchQuery.builder("java").pageSize(20).sortBy("title").build(), and still ends in one private canonical constructor that validates. L49 builds one end to end with a static nested class.

What breaks in production
  • The vanished no-arg constructor. Adding a convenience constructor to a JPA entity or a Jackson DTO compiles cleanly, then fails at runtime when the framework tries to instantiate it reflectively. Keep a (possibly protected) no-arg constructor on entities; for DTOs, prefer records (L47), which Jackson binds through the canonical constructor.
  • Leaking this. Registering this as a listener, putting it in a static map, or starting a thread with it inside the constructor publishes an object whose later fields are still default values. Another thread can see final fields before they are assigned. Construct first, register afterwards (often from a static factory).
  • Shallow copy constructors. this.toppings = other.toppings instead of a copy makes the two objects share one list; a change through either shows up in both.
  • Validation in only some constructors. Without a single canonical constructor, one overload eventually forgets a null check.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Run Order. Predict the output first, then move field b above the initializer block and predict again. Then move a constructor above the fields: does the order change? (Only the textual order among initializers matters.)
  2. Write Temperature with constructors (double celsius), (double value, char unit) and (Temperature other), all funnelled into one canonical constructor that rejects values below absolute zero.
  3. In Temperature(double celsius), put an if check before the this(...) call. Compile with --release 21 and read the error; compile with JDK 25 and confirm it is accepted.
  4. Write a test that mutates a copied Pizza's toppings and asserts the original is unchanged. Break the copy constructor on purpose and watch the test fail.
  5. Use reflection (getDeclaredConstructor().setAccessible(true)) to call IdGenerator's private constructor, and confirm the guard throws.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 1–4 · JLS §8.8 (Constructor Declarations), §8.8.7.1, §8.8.9, §12.5 · JEP 513, “Flexible Constructor Bodies”

L32 · DAY 32

Encapsulation, Accessors & Designing for Immutability

40m video45m practice

Encapsulation means the class, not its callers, decides which states are legal. Private fields are the mechanism; immutability is the strongest form, because an object that can never change can never be put into a bad state, by anyone, on any thread.

Why public fields are a design failure

A public field hands every caller write access to your representation. Four things become impossible at once:

  • Invariants. Nothing stops account.balanceCents = -500 or user.email = null.
  • Changing the representation. Switch public double price to a BigDecimal and every client breaks. With an accessor, the field type is a private detail.
  • Behaviour on access. No lazy loading, no logging, no computed values, no locking.
  • Abstraction. Interfaces declare methods, not fields, so a field can't be part of a contract (L39).

Effective Java Item 16 makes the rule precise: in public classes, use accessors. A private nested class, or a package-private class used by one package, may expose fields because the blast radius is small. The JDK's own java.awt.Point and Dimension are the standard cautionary examples of public mutable fields that can never be fixed.

Private fields, accessors, and validating setters

The basic shape is private state plus methods that guard it. A setter is the one place a value enters the object, so it is where you normalise and validate. Better still, replace generic setters with methods that name the operation the business actually performs.

public class Customer {
    private String email;
    private int loyaltyPoints;

    public String getEmail() { return email; }

    public void setEmail(String email) {
        String e = email == null ? "" : email.strip().toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT);
        if (!e.matches("[^@\\s]+@[^@\\s]+"))
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("invalid email: " + email);
        this.email = e;
    }

    public int getLoyaltyPoints() { return loyaltyPoints; }

    public void addPoints(int points) {     // an operation, not setLoyaltyPoints
        if (points <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("points <= 0");
        loyaltyPoints = Math.addExact(loyaltyPoints, points);
    }
}

A read-only class simply has no setters: all state arrives through the constructor (L31), which validates once. Read-only is not yet immutable, because a getter can still hand out a reference to something mutable inside.

Defensive copying in and out

Definition · defensive copy

When a constructor receives a mutable object, store a copy, so the caller's later changes can't reach you. When a getter returns a mutable component, return a copy or an unmodifiable value, so the caller can't change you through it. Copy before validating, so another thread can't change the argument between your check and your copy (Effective Java Item 50).

The five rules for a truly immutable class

Rule set · Effective Java Item 17
  1. No mutators. No setters, no methods that change state.
  2. No extension. Make the class final (or give it only private constructors plus static factories), so a subclass can't add mutable state or override accessors.
  3. All fields final. Documents intent and buys the memory-model guarantee below.
  4. All fields private. Even a public final field pins the representation.
  5. Exclusive access to mutable components. Defensive copies in constructors, getters and readObject; never hand out an internal reference.
public final class Itinerary {
    private final String traveller;
    private final List<String> stops;
    private final Date booked;                     // legacy mutable type

    public Itinerary(String traveller, List<String> stops, Date booked) {
        this.traveller = Objects.requireNonNull(traveller);
        this.stops = List.copyOf(stops);           // copy in (rejects nulls)
        this.booked = new Date(booked.getTime());  // copy, then validate
        if (this.stops.isEmpty()) throw new IllegalArgumentException("no stops");
    }

    public String traveller()   { return traveller; }
    public List<String> stops() { return stops; }  // already unmodifiable
    public Date booked()        { return new Date(booked.getTime()); } // copy out

    public Itinerary withStop(String stop) {       // "change" = new object
        var next = new ArrayList<>(stops);
        next.add(stop);
        return new Itinerary(traveller, next, booked);
    }
}

Note new Date(booked.getTime()) rather than booked.clone(): Date isn't final, so an attacker could pass a subclass whose clone returns something it still controls. With java.time types (L107) the problem disappears, because LocalDate and Instant are immutable and can be stored directly. "Changing" an immutable object means returning a new one, as withStop does; String.replace and BigDecimal.add work the same way.

Immutability and thread safety

An immutable object has one state for its whole life, so there is nothing to race on: it can be shared across threads, cached, and used as a map key with no locking. Java adds a guarantee specifically for final fields (JLS §17.5): once a constructor finishes, any thread that sees a reference to the object sees the correctly initialised values of its final fields, even if the reference was published without synchronisation. The guarantee has one condition, that this did not escape during construction (L31). A class with non-final fields gets no such promise, which is why rule 3 matters even when nothing reassigns the field. L128 covers the memory model properly.

JavaBeans conventions, and when to use records instead

The JavaBeans specification (1997) defines a bean as a class with a public no-argument constructor and properties exposed through getX()/setX(...), with isX() allowed for a primitive boolean. The convention matters wherever a framework discovers properties by name: Jackson maps JSON fields to getters and setters, Spring binds configuration properties and form fields through setters, template engines resolve ${book.title} to getTitle(), and mapping tools such as MapStruct generate code from property names. java.beans.Introspector is the JDK's own implementation of the naming rules.

For a class that is just named, immutable data (a DTO, a value object, an API response), the getters, constructor, equals, hashCode and toString are boilerplate, and a record (L47) generates all of it: record Money(long cents, String currency) {}. Records use cents() rather than getCents(); Jackson has supported records since 2.12, but check older tools before swapping a bean for a record. Keep classic encapsulated classes for objects with identity and a lifecycle, such as JPA entities, which need a no-arg constructor and mutable state.

What breaks in production
  • Unmodifiable view, not copy. Collections.unmodifiableList(items) blocks writes through the view, but changes to items still show through it. Use List.copyOf when you need a snapshot.
  • final arrays. public static final String[] ROLES is a mutable global: anyone can write ROLES[0] = "ADMIN". Expose a List.of(...) or a copy (Item 15).
  • Getters that leak. Returning the internal ArrayList from an entity lets callers bypass every invariant the setters enforce, often by accident in a mapping layer.
  • Generated setters for everything. Lombok @Data or IDE generation produces a setter for each field, including IDs and audit timestamps that should never change after creation.

Practice · 45 min

  1. Take a Product class with public name, price and stock fields. Encapsulate it: validating setters for name and price, and restock(int)/sell(int) instead of setStock.
  2. Attack Itinerary: mutate the list you passed in, call booked().setTime(0), and try to add to stops(). Then remove each defence in turn and show the corresponding attack succeeding.
  3. Audit String, Integer and BigInteger against the five rules. One of them is not final. What does Effective Java say about that, and what must code that accepts it as a parameter do?
  4. Write a bean with getName, setName and isActive, list its properties with Introspector.getBeanInfo(...).getPropertyDescriptors(), then rename isActive to getIsActive and observe the property name change.
  5. Rewrite Itinerary as a record with a compact constructor that does the defensive copy, and compare line counts.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 15, 16, 17, 50 · JLS §17.5 (final field semantics) · JavaBeans Specification 1.01, Ch. 7–8 · Javadoc: List.copyOf, Collections.unmodifiableList, java.beans.Introspector

L33 · DAY 33

static Fields, Methods, Blocks & Utility Classes

40m video40m practice

static means "belongs to the class, not to any object". That makes static members the right home for constants and pure functions, and a dangerous home for mutable state, because a static field is a global variable shared by every instance, every thread and every test in the JVM.

Class-level vs instance-level state

An instance field exists once per object: a thousand Tickets have a thousand number fields. A static field exists once per class, however many objects you create, including zero. In HotSpot it is stored with the class's java.lang.Class object on the heap. Strictly, it is once per class per class loader: the same class loaded by two loaders (common in application servers) has two independent copies, which surprises people who use statics as "global" registries.

Static fields, counters and static blocks

public class Ticket {
    private static int issued;                       // one copy for the class
    private static final int[] POPCOUNT = new int[256];

    static {                                         // runs once, at class init
        for (int i = 1; i < 256; i++) POPCOUNT[i] = POPCOUNT[i >> 1] + (i & 1);
    }

    private final int number;                        // one copy per object

    public Ticket() { number = ++issued; }           // the classic static counter

    public static int issued()  { return issued; }   // no `this` available here
    public int number()         { return number; }
    public static int bits(int b) { return POPCOUNT[b & 0xFF]; }
}

A static initializer block runs once, when the class is initialised: on first new, first static method call, or first access to a non-constant static field (L34 lists the triggers). Use one when a static field needs more than a single expression: a lookup table, a loop, a try/catch around loading a resource. Static field initializers and static blocks run in textual order, top to bottom, which causes a classic bug:

class Config {
    static final Config INSTANCE = new Config();      // runs first...
    static final List<String> DEFAULTS = List.of("a", "b"); // ...this is still null

    private final int size;
    private Config() { size = DEFAULTS.size(); }      // NullPointerException
}

The first use of Config fails with ExceptionInInitializerError wrapping the NPE. Swap the two lines and it works. A compile-time constant such as static final int MAX = 10 or a static final String literal is exempt: the compiler inlines it, so it never appears uninitialised.

Static methods, and calling static members correctly

Definition · static method

A static method has no receiver object. There is no this and no super, so it can't read instance fields or call instance methods without an explicit reference; the compiler reports non-static variable cannot be referenced from a static context. The call target is fixed at compile time, so static methods are never polymorphic (L36 calls this hiding).

That is why main is traditionally static: the launcher can call it before any object exists. (Java 25 also accepts instance main methods, JEP 512, but the static form remains the norm.) Always call a static member through the class name, Ticket.issued(), never through a reference. Calling through a reference compiles, but the compiler uses the reference's declared type and ignores the object entirely:

Thread worker = new Thread(() -> {});
worker.sleep(1_000);            // sleeps the CURRENT thread, not worker
Ticket t = null;
System.out.println(t.issued()); // no NullPointerException: t is never read

Static imports drop the class prefix: import static java.util.Objects.requireNonNull; or import static java.lang.Math.*;. They read well for a few heavily used names (test assertions, Collectors.toList, constants) and badly when a reader can't tell where max came from. Import single names rather than *, and never two statics with the same simple name.

The utility class pattern

public final class Slugs {                // final: no subclasses
    private Slugs() {                      // private: no instances, no subclass ctor
        throw new AssertionError("no instances");
    }

    public static String slug(String s) {
        return s.strip().toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT)
                .replaceAll("[^a-z0-9]+", "-")
                .replaceAll("(^-|-$)", "");
    }
}

A utility class groups stateless functions that don't belong to any one type. The private constructor stops instantiation (and, as a side effect, subclassing); the AssertionError catches a reflective or accidental internal call (Effective Java Item 4). The JDK's reference designs follow exactly this shape: Math, Objects, Collections and Arrays each have a private constructor and nothing but static members, and Objects' constructor throws an AssertionError. Notice what they contain: pure functions of their arguments and immutable constants (Math.PI), never mutable state.

Static vs singleton, and why static state hurts testability

Static utility classSingleton object
Implements an interfacenoyes
Can be passed, injected, mockedno; callers are hard-wired to the classyes, it is an ordinary object
Polymorphismnonethrough its interface
Right forpure functions, constantsa shared service with state or dependencies

Static mutable state is a global variable with a nicer name. It survives from one test to the next in the same JVM, so tests pass alone and fail together; it prevents running tests in parallel; and a class that reads it has a dependency nobody can see in its constructor. The same goes for hidden static calls to the environment: LocalDate.now(), UUID.randomUUID(), System.getenv. Pass a java.time.Clock (or a small interface) into the constructor instead, and a test can supply Clock.fixed(...). This is the motivation for Spring's dependency injection: Spring beans are singletons by scope, ordinary objects created once and passed in, not static members.

What breaks in production
  • Lost updates. ++issued is read-modify-write. Two request threads can read the same value and both write n + 1, issuing duplicate ticket numbers. Use AtomicInteger/AtomicLong (L130), or better, let the database assign IDs.
  • Shared non-thread-safe objects. A static SimpleDateFormat used by many threads corrupts dates intermittently. Use the immutable DateTimeFormatter.
  • Static caches that only grow. A static Map used as a cache with no eviction is a memory leak with a long fuse.
  • Request data in a static field. Storing "the current user" in a static field leaks one user's data into another's request.
  • Failed static initialisation. If a static block throws, the first use gets ExceptionInInitializerError and every later use gets NoClassDefFoundError: Could not initialize class …. Search the logs for the first error; the later ones hide the cause.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Create three Tickets and print Ticket.issued(). Compile t.issued() with javac -Xlint:static and read the warning.
  2. Run the Config bug, read the full stack trace, then fix it by reordering. Explain why changing DEFAULTS to static final int DEFAULTS = 2 would also have "fixed" it.
  3. Increment a static int from two threads, 100,000 times each, and print the result. Repeat with AtomicInteger.
  4. Write a PasswordPolicy utility class. Use it from a test with a single-name static import.
  5. Refactor a Membership class that calls LocalDate.now() to take a Clock, and test expiry with Clock.fixed.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 3, 4, 5 · JLS §8.3.1.1 (static fields), §8.4.3.2 (static methods), §8.7 (static initializers), §7.5.3 (single static import), §12.4.2 (initialization procedure) · Javadoc: Math, Objects, java.time.Clock

L34 · DAY 34

Initializer Blocks & Full Initialization Order

30m video30m practice

Initialization happens in two phases: class initialization, once per class on first use, superclass first; then object initialization, on every new, superclass first again. Know the six steps and you can predict any initialization puzzle, including the bug where a subclass method runs before its own fields exist.

Instance initializer blocks vs constructors

An instance initializer is a bare { ... } block in the class body. The compiler copies all field initializers and instance blocks, in textual order, into every constructor that calls super(...), immediately after that call (JLS §8.6). So a block is just code shared by all constructors. In ordinary classes, delegating to one canonical constructor (L31) says the same thing more clearly. Blocks earn their place in anonymous classes, which can't declare constructors (L49).

The famous misuse is double-brace initialization: new HashMap<>() {{ put("a", 1); }}. The outer braces create an anonymous subclass of HashMap, and the inner ones are its instance initializer. Each use creates a new class file, and when written inside an instance method the object keeps a hidden reference to the enclosing object. Use Map.of("a", 1).

A static initializer, static { ... }, runs once when the class is initialised, together with the static field initializers, in textual order (L33).

The exact order in a class hierarchy

Algorithm · creating a subclass object
  1. If Child is not initialised yet: initialise Parent first (its static fields and blocks, in order), then Child's. This happens once per class.
  2. Allocate the object; every field of Parent and Child holds 0/false/null.
  3. Child() starts and immediately calls super(), which goes up the chain to Object().
  4. Parent's field initializers and instance blocks run, then the rest of the Parent() body.
  5. Child's field initializers and instance blocks run.
  6. The rest of the Child() body runs, and new returns the reference.
class Parent {
    static { System.out.println("1 Parent static block"); }
    private final String p = log("3 Parent field");
    { log("3 Parent instance block"); }

    Parent() {
        log("4 Parent constructor");
        describe();                               // overridable call: the bug
    }
    void describe() {}
    static String log(String s) { System.out.println(s); return s; }
}

class Child extends Parent {
    static { System.out.println("2 Child static block"); }
    private final List<String> tags = new ArrayList<>(List.of("new"));
    { log("5 Child instance block"); }

    Child() { log("6 Child constructor, tags = " + tags); }

    @Override void describe() { log("  Child.describe sees tags = " + tags); }
}

public class InitOrder {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        new Child();          // 1, 2, 3, 3, 4, "tags = null", 5, 6
        new Child();          // 3, 3, 4, "tags = null", 5, 6: statics don't repeat
    }
}

tags is final, and it is still observed as null. Had describe called tags.add(...), you would get a NullPointerException from inside a constructor, a stack trace that looks impossible. Effective Java Item 19 states the rule: constructors must not invoke overridable methods, directly or indirectly. Make such helpers private, final or static.

What triggers class initialization

Definition · initialization triggers (JLS §12.4.1)

A class is initialised immediately before the first of: creating an instance; calling one of its static methods; assigning one of its static fields; reading a static field that is not a constant variable; a reflective call such as Class.forName("X"); or initialising one of its subclasses. Running main initialises the main class.

These do not initialise it: reading a compile-time constant (static final int MAX = 10, inlined by the compiler), creating an array (new X[10]), the literal X.class, Class.forName("X", false, loader), or reading a parent's static field through a subclass name (only the parent is initialised).

Class initialization is also thread-safe by construction: the JVM holds an initialization lock per class, so if two threads trigger it at once, one runs the static initializers and the other waits (JLS §12.4.2). Phase 6 (L154) shows the loading and linking steps that come before this one.

Lazy vs eager initialization, and the holder idiom

Eager initialization (private static final Registry INSTANCE = new Registry();) is simple and thread-safe, and the cost is paid when the class is initialised, which is usually first use anyway. Lazy initialization delays an expensive object until someone needs it. A plain null check is a race; a synchronized getter is correct but takes a lock on every call; double-checked locking works only with a volatile field (L129). For static fields there is a cleaner option that leans on the class-initialization lock:

public final class Registry {
    private Registry() { System.out.println("expensive setup"); }

    private static final class Holder {             // not initialised until used
        static final Registry INSTANCE = new Registry();
    }

    public static Registry get() { return Holder.INSTANCE; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("started");              // printed first
        Registry.get();                             // now: "expensive setup"
        Registry.get();                             // nothing: already done
    }
}

This is the initialization-on-demand holder idiom. Holder is initialised only when get() first touches Holder.INSTANCE; the JVM guarantees that happens exactly once and that every thread sees the finished object; after that, get() is a plain field read with no locking. Effective Java Item 83 recommends it for lazy static fields, and adds the advice that normal (eager) initialization is preferable unless measurement says otherwise.

What breaks in production
  • Overridable calls in constructors. Common in base classes with a protected void init() hook. The subclass's override runs against a half-built object. Call hooks after construction, from a factory.
  • Class-initialization deadlock. If A's static initializer touches B and B's touches A, two threads that start with different classes can each hold one init lock and wait forever. A thread dump shows both threads inside <clinit>. Keep static initializers small and free of cross-class dependencies.
  • Constant inlining hides the bug. If the subclass field were a constant variable such as final String tag = "c", the override would print c, because the compiler inlined the value. Change it to a computed value and the bug appears. Inlining also means changing a public static final constant requires recompiling every class that uses it.

Practice · 30 min

  1. Write down the output of InitOrder before running it, then run it and compare line by line.
  2. Make Parent.describe() private and rerun. Which method runs now, and why does the bug disappear?
  3. Give a class Probe a static block that prints. Which of these print it: Probe.MAX (a constant), Probe.list, new Probe[3], Probe.class, Class.forName("Probe"), Class.forName("Probe", false, loader)?
  4. Run Registry.main and confirm the laziness. Then make INSTANCE an eager field of Registry itself and observe when "expensive setup" prints.

Read JLS §8.6 (instance initializers), §8.7 (static initializers), §12.4 (initialization of classes), §12.5 (creation of instances) · JVMS §5.5 · Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 19, 83

L35 · DAY 35

Inheritance, extends & super

45m video45m practice

extends makes a subclass a special case of its parent: it gets the parent's state and behaviour, and it can be used anywhere the parent is expected. That is a strong promise, and inheritance is right only when the IS-A relationship is true in every situation the parent's contract covers.

IS-A, and single inheritance of classes

"A SavingsAccount is an Account" is the test. If it holds, everything you can do with an Account (deposit, read the balance, describe it) must make sense for a SavingsAccount. If you find yourself saying "is a kind of, except…", inheritance is the wrong tool.

A Java class extends exactly one class. Leave out extends and the parent is java.lang.Object, so every class sits in a single tree rooted there. Java permits multiple inheritance of type through interfaces (L39) but not of state, which avoids the ambiguity of two parents both supplying a field or a constructor.

What is inherited and what isn't

Definition · inherited members (JLS §8.2, §8.4.8)
  • Inherited: public and protected fields and methods; package-private ones too, but only by subclasses in the same package; static members, which you can reach as SavingsAccount.someStatic() although they are hidden, not overridden (L36).
  • Not inherited: private members (they exist in the object, as the figure shows, but the subclass can't access them), constructors, and initializer blocks. Constructors aren't members at all, which is why each subclass has to declare its own.

super(...), super.method() and the implicit super()

public class Account {
    private final String id;
    protected long balanceCents;              // visible to subclasses

    public Account(String id) { this.id = Objects.requireNonNull(id); }

    public String id() { return id; }
    public void deposit(long cents) {
        if (cents <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("amount " + cents);
        balanceCents += cents;
    }
    public String describe() { return id + ": " + balanceCents + "c"; }
}

class SavingsAccount extends Account {
    private final int rateBasisPoints;

    SavingsAccount(String id, int rateBasisPoints) {
        super(id);                            // must come first; picks Account(String)
        this.rateBasisPoints = rateBasisPoints;
    }

    void addInterest() {
        long interest = balanceCents * rateBasisPoints / 10_000;
        if (interest > 0) deposit(interest); // inherited method, reused as is
    }

    @Override public String describe() {
        return super.describe() + " @ " + rateBasisPoints + "bp"; // extend, not copy
    }
}

super(args) chooses which parent constructor initialises the inherited part of the object, and like this(...) it must be the first statement in Java 21 (L31). super.describe() calls the parent's version of a method you have overridden, which is how you extend behaviour instead of duplicating it. It reaches exactly one level up; there is no super.super.

If a constructor starts with neither this(...) nor super(...), the compiler inserts super(). That compiles only if the parent has an accessible no-argument constructor. Account doesn't, so:

class Broken extends Account {
    Broken() { }   // does not compile: implicit super(), but there is no Account()
}

javac reports constructor Account in class Account cannot be applied to given types; required: String; found: no arguments. The same error appears for a class with no constructor at all, because its default constructor also calls super(). The fix is to call super(...) with real arguments, not to add a no-arg constructor to Account that would let an account exist without an id.

Constructor chaining up the hierarchy

Every constructor call climbs to Object() before any constructor body finishes: SavingsAccount(...) → Account(String) → Object(). Then the bodies complete from the top down, so a parent's state is always set up before the child's constructor body runs (L34 gives the exact order). This is why a subclass may rely on inherited fields in its own constructor, and why a parent must never rely on the child's.

Object, the root of everything

MethodDefault behaviourWhere
equals, hashCodeidentity, and an identity-based hashL43
toStringClassName@hexHashL44
getClassthe runtime class; finalL44
cloneprotected; field-by-field copy if CloneableL44
wait, notify, notifyAllmonitor coordination; finalPhase 5
finalizedeprecated for removal; never use itL44

Because everything extends Object, an Object variable can hold any reference, arrays included (every array type extends Object and implements Cloneable and Serializable). Before generics, collections stored Object and callers cast on the way out; L72 shows why that was abandoned.

protected, final classes, and when not to inherit

protected means "this package, plus subclasses anywhere". It is part of your public API in all but name: every subclass ever written may depend on it, so a protected field is as hard to change as a public one. Prefer private fields with protected methods that preserve the invariants. (Across packages there is a further subtlety about which references a subclass may use; see L50.)

A final class can't be extended. String, the wrapper classes, LocalDate and every record are final: their correctness, and in String's case the platform's security, depends on nobody overriding their methods. Make a class final unless you have designed and documented it for extension, the "design and document for inheritance or else prohibit it" rule of Effective Java Item 19.

The JDK contains the cautionary tales. Stack extends Vector, so a stack also inherits add(int, E) and remove(int), which let any caller break LIFO order. Properties extends Hashtable<Object, Object>, so you can put a non-String value that getProperty then ignores. Both should have had a collection rather than been one, which is the composition design of L42.

What breaks in production
  • Proxies vs final. Spring creates class-based (CGLIB) proxies by subclassing your bean, and Hibernate creates lazy-loading proxies by subclassing your entity. A final method can't be overridden, so @Transactional on a final method of a class-proxied bean is simply not applied, and a final entity class can't be proxied for lazy loading.
  • Protected fields. balanceCents is protected, so any subclass can set it to a negative number and bypass deposit's check. One subclass in another team's module is enough.
  • Superclass changes. Adding a method to a widely extended class can collide with a subclass method of the same signature that was written with different intent. L42 shows the general fragile-base-class problem.

Practice · 45 min

  1. Implement Account, SavingsAccount, and a CheckingAccount with an overdraft limit that overrides a withdraw method and calls super where sensible.
  2. Delete super(id) from SavingsAccount, read the compiler error, and explain why adding Account() is the wrong fix.
  3. Walk getClass().getSuperclass() up to null for a SavingsAccount, a String and an int[], printing each class. Which interfaces does the array implement?
  4. Push 1, 2, 3 onto a java.util.Stack, then call add(0, 99) and remove(1). Print the stack and pop until empty. Which inherited methods broke the abstraction?
  5. Make balanceCents private, add a protected method that subclasses can use safely, and confirm addInterest still works.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 18, 19 · JLS §8.1.4 (superclasses), §8.2 (class members), §8.8.7 (constructor body), §4.3.2 (the class Object) · Javadoc: java.lang.Object, java.util.Stack

L36 · DAY 36

Method Overriding, @Override & Hiding

40m video40m practice

Overriding replaces a superclass's implementation for objects of the subclass, and the JVM picks the implementation from the object at run time. Everything else that looks similar (overloading, static methods, fields) is decided by the compiler from declared types, and confusing the two is the root of a surprising number of bugs.

The overriding rules

Definition · overriding (JLS §8.4.8.1, §8.4.8.3)

An instance method in a subclass overrides a superclass instance method when it has the same name and the same parameter types, and the superclass method is accessible from the subclass. The override must then obey:

  • Return type: identical for primitives and void; for references, the same type or a subtype (a covariant return).
  • Access: no weaker. public stays public; protected may become public; package-private may not become private.
  • Checked exceptions: no new or broader ones. It may throw fewer, narrower ones, or none. Unchecked exceptions are unrestricted.

Each rule exists so that code written against the superclass keeps working with a subclass object: a caller that expects a Shape back still gets one, can still call the method, and has already handled every checked exception it might see.

class Shape {
    protected Shape copy() throws IOException { return new Shape(); }
    public String name() { return "shape"; }
    static String kind() { return "Shape.kind"; }
    String label = "shape-field";
}

class Circle extends Shape {
    @Override public Circle copy() { return new Circle(); } // covariant return,
                                                            // wider access, no throws
    @Override public String name() { return "circle"; }
    static String kind() { return "Circle.kind"; }          // hides, does not override
    String label = "circle-field";                          // hides the field
}

public class Dispatch {
    public static void main(String[] args) throws IOException {
        Shape s = new Circle();
        System.out.println(s.name());            // circle       (runtime class)
        System.out.println(s.kind());            // Shape.kind   (declared type)
        System.out.println(s.label);             // shape-field  (declared type)
        System.out.println(((Circle) s).label);  // circle-field
        System.out.println(s.copy().name());     // circle
    }
}

And the overrides the compiler refuses, with javac's wording:

class Base {
    public void a() {}
    void b() throws IOException {}
    final void c() {}
    static void d() {}
}

class Derived extends Base {
    // void a() {}                  attempting to assign weaker access privileges
    // void b() throws Exception {} overridden method does not throw Exception
    // void c() {}                  overridden method is final
    // void d() {}                  overridden method is static
    @Override void b() throws FileNotFoundException {}   // legal: narrower
}

Why @Override is mandatory in practice

@Override asks the compiler to check that the method really overrides something; if it doesn't, compilation fails with method does not override or implement a method from a supertype. Without it, a typo or a wrong parameter type silently creates a new overload. The canonical victim:

class Book {
    final String isbn;
    Book(String isbn) { this.isbn = isbn; }

    public boolean equals(Book other) {        // overload! Object.equals untouched
        return other != null && isbn.equals(other.isbn);
    }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return isbn.hashCode(); }
}
// new HashSet<>(List.of(new Book("1"), new Book("1"))).size()  ==  2

HashSet calls equals(Object), which is still Object's identity check. Put @Override on the method and the bug becomes a compile error. Effective Java Item 40: use @Override on every method you believe overrides a superclass method, and on interface implementations too.

Overriding vs overloading, side by side

OverloadingOverriding
Signaturesame name, different parameter typessame name, same parameter types
Whereany class, including one classsubclass redefines a superclass method
Chosen bycompiler, from argument typesJVM, from the receiver's runtime class
Return typeunconstrainedsame or covariant
Access, exceptionsunconstrainedno weaker access, no broader checked exceptions
Static methodscan be overloadedcan't be overridden, only hidden

What can't be overridden, and what hiding means

  • static methods belong to the class. A static method with the same signature in a subclass hides the parent's: which one runs depends on the declared type in the call, as s.kind() shows. An instance method can't override a static one, and a static method can't hide an instance one.
  • private methods aren't inherited, so a same-named subclass method is unrelated. Calls inside the parent to its private method always go to the parent's version, which is what makes private helpers safe to call from constructors (L34).
  • final methods exist precisely to forbid overriding: the class guarantees that behaviour to every caller.
  • Package-private methods across packages. A subclass in another package can't see the method, so its same-signature method is a new method, not an override. @Override catches it.

Field hiding: declaring a field with the same name as an inherited field creates a second, independent field. Field access never dispatches; s.label reads whichever field the declared type names. It is legal and almost always a mistake, typically a base entity and a subclass both declaring id, each set by different code.

Calling super inside an override

super.name() invokes the parent's implementation, non-virtually, from inside the override. Use it to extend rather than replace: return super.toString() + "[radius=" + r + "]". Frameworks with lifecycle hooks often require it, and forgetting the call skips the parent's setup. Overrides that must always run the parent's logic are a sign the parent should use the Template Method pattern instead (L38), so the subclass can't forget.

Liskov Substitution, the rule behind the rules

Principle · Liskov substitution (Liskov & Wing, 1994)

If S is a subtype of T, any property provable about objects of T must hold for objects of S. In practice: an override may not demand more of its callers (stronger preconditions), may not promise less (weaker postconditions), and must preserve the parent's invariants.

The compiler enforces only the syntactic part (signatures, access, checked exceptions). The behavioural part is on you. Square extends Rectangle breaks it: code that sets width 5 and height 4 expects area 20, and a square can't give it. An override that throws UnsupportedOperationException for an operation the parent supports breaks it too. L37 shows the payoff of substitutable subtypes, and L42 what to do when they aren't.

What breaks in production
  • Overload instead of override. equals(Book), compareTo with the wrong parameter type, or a misspelled framework callback such as onApplicationEvnt: all compile and all are ignored. @Override everywhere.
  • Expecting statics to dispatch. A "polymorphic" static factory or config method called through a base-type reference always runs the base version.
  • Duplicated fields. A subclass redeclaring status or id splits one concept into two fields; getters in the parent and child then disagree.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Predict each line of Dispatch, then run it. Change s.kind() to Shape.kind() and Circle.kind() and explain why that is the honest way to write it.
  2. Uncomment each illegal method in Derived one at a time and record the error. Then make Base.a() protected and find which access levels Derived.a() may now use.
  3. Reproduce the Book bug with a HashSet, add @Override, and fix the signature.
  4. Write Rectangle and Square extends Rectangle with setters, and a test that passes for Rectangle and fails for Square. Redesign so the test can't be written.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Item 40 · JLS §8.4.8 (inheritance, overriding and hiding), §8.3 (field hiding), §9.6.4.4 (@Override) · Liskov & Wing 1994, “A Behavioral Notion of Subtyping”

L37 · DAY 37

Polymorphism & Dynamic Dispatch

45m video45m practice

A variable has two types: the one written in the code, which decides what you may call, and the class of the object it points to, which decides what actually runs. Polymorphism is designing so that callers only ever need the first one, and adding a new kind of object never means editing the callers.

Compile-time type vs runtime type

Definition · static and dynamic type

In PaymentMethod pm = new Card("4242"); the compile-time (static) type of pm is PaymentMethod: the compiler lets you call only methods PaymentMethod declares. The runtime (dynamic) type is Card, the class of the object, which can differ on every execution. For an overridden instance method the JVM runs the version belonging to the runtime type. That is dynamic dispatch, also called virtual method invocation.

abstract class PaymentMethod {                  // abstract: see L38
    abstract long feeCents(long amountCents);
    abstract String authorize(long amountCents);
}

final class Card extends PaymentMethod {
    private final String last4;
    Card(String last4) { this.last4 = last4; }
    String last4() { return last4; }
    long feeCents(long a)    { return 30 + a * 29 / 1000; }      // 30c + 2.9%
    String authorize(long a) { return "card *" + last4 + " ok " + a; }
}

final class BankTransfer extends PaymentMethod {
    long feeCents(long a)    { return 0; }
