    String authorize(long a) { return "transfer queued " + a; }
    String reference()       { return "TRF-" + Long.toHexString(System.nanoTime()); }
}

final class Wallet extends PaymentMethod {
    long feeCents(long a)    { return a / 100; }                 // 1%
    String authorize(long a) { return "wallet charged " + a; }
}

public class Checkout {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<PaymentMethod> methods =                  // a polymorphic collection
            List.of(new Card("4242"), new BankTransfer(), new Wallet());
        for (PaymentMethod pm : methods)                // one loop, three behaviours
            System.out.println(pm.authorize(10_000) + ", fee " + pm.feeCents(10_000));
    }
}
// card *4242 ok 10000, fee 320
// transfer queued 10000, fee 0
// wallet charged 10000, fee 100

Virtual invocation and the vtable mental model

javac compiles pm.feeCents(x) to invokevirtual PaymentMethod.feeCents(J)J: it records the method's signature and the class it was found in, not an implementation. At run time the JVM needs the implementation for the object's actual class. HotSpot answers this with a per-class virtual method table: each class copies its parent's table, overwrites the slots it overrides, and appends slots for new methods. A method keeps the same slot index throughout the hierarchy, so dispatch is three steps: read the object's class pointer, load the entry at the fixed index, jump.

Interface calls (invokeinterface) use a similar per-interface table that needs a short search. In practice the JIT removes most of this cost: if a call site has only ever seen one or two receiver classes, HotSpot compiles a direct, inlined call guarded by a cheap class check, and falls back to the table only at megamorphic sites. Phase 6 covers this (L160). Polymorphism is not a performance problem to design around.

Programming to the supertype

Declare variables, parameters, return types and fields with the most general type that offers what the code needs: List<PaymentMethod> rather than ArrayList<Card>, charge(PaymentMethod pm) rather than charge(Card card). The code then works unchanged for subclasses that don't exist yet, and tests can pass in a fake. Effective Java Item 64 states it as "refer to objects by their interfaces".

Upcasting, downcasting and pattern-matching instanceof

PaymentMethod pm = new BankTransfer();   // upcast: implicit, always safe
BankTransfer bt = (BankTransfer) pm;     // downcast: explicit, checked at run time
// Wallet w = (Wallet) pm;               // compiles, throws ClassCastException
// String s = (String) pm;               // compile error: types can never match

if (pm instanceof BankTransfer t) {      // test, cast and bind in one step
    System.out.println(t.reference());
}
if (pm instanceof Card c && c.last4().startsWith("4")) { /* c in scope here */ }
if (!(pm instanceof Wallet w)) return;   // flow scoping: w is in scope below
System.out.println(w.feeCents(500));
  • Upcasting (subclass to superclass) needs no syntax and can't fail: every Card is a PaymentMethod.
  • Downcasting is a promise to the compiler, verified by the JVM. If the object isn't an instance of the target type, the cast throws ClassCastException. If the compiler can prove no object could ever satisfy the cast, as with two unrelated classes, it refuses to compile it.
  • x instanceof T is false for null, so it never throws. Since Java 16 (JEP 394), x instanceof T t also casts and binds t. The binding is in scope only where the compiler can prove the test succeeded: after &&, inside the if, or after an early return on the negated test. It is not in scope after ||.

Preventing ClassCastException: prefer a polymorphic method to a cast at all; if you must narrow, use pattern matching so the test and the cast can't drift apart; use generics instead of collections of Object (L72); and for closed hierarchies, use sealed types with exhaustive switch (L48).

Replacing if-else type checks with polymorphism

static long fee(PaymentMethod pm, long a) {      // the shape to avoid
    if (pm instanceof Card) return 30 + a * 29 / 1000;
    else if (pm instanceof Wallet) return a / 100;
    else if (pm instanceof BankTransfer) return 0;
    throw new IllegalArgumentException("unknown method " + pm);
}

Every function like this is a list of all subclasses, written out by hand. Add Crypto and you must find and edit every such list; miss one and the bug shows up at run time, possibly only in production for the first customer who pays that way. With feeCents as a method on the hierarchy, adding Crypto means writing one class, and the compiler forces you to implement every abstract method. This is the Open/Closed Principle: open to new types, closed to edits in existing callers. (When the set of types is fixed and the operations keep growing, the trade-off flips; L48's sealed types and pattern-matching switch handle that case, still checked by the compiler.)

What breaks in production
  • Lost generic types after deserialization. Read JSON into a raw List or Map and you get LinkedHashMaps, not your DTOs. The cast happens later, far from the cause: class java.util.LinkedHashMap cannot be cast to class com.acme.BookDto. Give the mapper the full type (L77 explains why it is otherwise lost).
  • instanceof chains that drift. A new subclass is added, one chain gets updated, another falls through to a default branch that quietly does the wrong thing, for instance charging no fee.
  • Downcasting a framework proxy. Spring may hand you a JDK proxy that implements your interface but is not your class, so (MyServiceImpl) bean throws. Inject and call the interface.

Practice · 45 min

  1. Implement the PaymentMethod hierarchy and Checkout. Add a Crypto method with a flat 1.5% fee. Count the files you touched, then count how many you would have touched with three fee-style functions.
  2. Trigger a ClassCastException deliberately and read its message. Then write the unrelated-class cast that javac rejects, and explain how the compiler knows.
  3. Rewrite three instanceof-then-cast pairs with pattern matching. Try to use a binding variable after || and read the error.
  4. Write Map<String, Long> feesByType(List<PaymentMethod>) that sums fees per getClass().getSimpleName(), with no casts at all.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Item 64 · JLS §15.12.4 (run-time evaluation of method invocation), §5.5 (casting contexts), §15.20.2 (instanceof), §6.3.1 (scope of pattern variables) · JVMS §6.5 (invokevirtual) · JEP 394, “Pattern Matching for instanceof”

L38 · DAY 38

Abstract Classes & Abstract Methods

40m video40m practice

An abstract class is a deliberately unfinished class: it fixes the state, the invariants and the overall algorithm, and leaves named blanks that each subclass must fill in. Its best use is the Template Method pattern, where the base class owns the order of the steps and subclasses supply only the steps.

The abstract keyword

Definition · abstract class and method

An abstract method has a signature and no body: protected abstract String formatRow(List<String> row);. A class that declares or inherits an abstract method without implementing it must itself be declared abstract. An abstract class can't be instantiated with new, but it can have constructors, fields, static members and any number of concrete methods. It may even have no abstract methods at all.

Forbidden combinations: abstract with final (it could never be completed), with private (no subclass could see it to implement it), and with static (static methods aren't dispatched, L36). javac reports illegal combination of modifiers.

Why can't you instantiate one? Because some of its methods have no code. If new Report(...) were allowed, a call to report.formatRow(row) would find an empty vtable slot (L37). The compiler prevents that state from existing: Report is abstract; cannot be instantiated. What you can do is instantiate a subclass, including an anonymous one written inline, new Report(...) { ... }, which must supply every missing body.

Constructors, fields and concrete methods

An abstract class is a full class with blanks, not just a list of signatures. Its constructor runs as part of every subclass's construction (L35), so it is the right place to validate and store state that all subclasses share. Make that constructor protected: only subclasses can call it anyway, and the modifier documents the fact.

The compiler also forces subclasses to implement the blanks. A concrete subclass that misses one gets CsvReport is not abstract and does not override abstract method formatRow(List<String>) in Report. That is the point: the base class turns "remember to implement X" from a code-review comment into a compile error. A subclass that wants to leave a blank for its own subclasses declares itself abstract too.

Partial implementation and the Template Method pattern

Pattern · Template Method (Gamma et al.)
  1. Write the algorithm once, in the base class, as a public final method: the template.
  2. Express each varying step as a protected method the template calls: abstract if every subclass must supply it, concrete with a sensible default if most can share one.
  3. Add optional hooks: protected methods with empty bodies, called at extension points, that subclasses may override.
  4. Subclasses override steps and hooks only. They never override, or even call, the template itself.
public abstract class Report {
    private final String title;
    private final Clock clock;

    protected Report(String title, Clock clock) {
        this.title = Objects.requireNonNull(title);
        this.clock = Objects.requireNonNull(clock);
    }

    public final String render() {                     // the template
        var out = new StringBuilder(generateHeader()).append('\n');
        for (List<String> row : rows()) out.append(formatRow(row)).append('\n');
        return out.append(generateFooter()).toString();
    }

    protected String generateHeader() {                // step with a default
        return title + " (" + LocalDate.now(clock) + ")";
    }
    protected abstract List<List<String>> rows();      // required steps
    protected abstract String formatRow(List<String> row);
    protected String generateFooter() { return ""; }   // optional hook
}
class CsvReport extends Report {
    private final List<List<String>> data;
    CsvReport(String title, Clock clock, List<List<String>> data) {
        super(title, clock);
        this.data = List.copyOf(data);
    }
    @Override protected List<List<String>> rows() { return data; }
    @Override protected String formatRow(List<String> r) { return String.join(",", r); }
}

class MarkdownReport extends CsvReport {               // reuse rows(), change format
    MarkdownReport(String title, Clock clock, List<List<String>> data) {
        super(title, clock, data);
    }
    @Override protected String generateHeader() { return "# " + super.generateHeader(); }
    @Override protected String formatRow(List<String> r) {
        return "| " + String.join(" | ", r) + " |";
    }
    @Override protected String generateFooter() { return "_" + rows().size() + " rows_"; }
}

public class Reports {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var clock = Clock.fixed(Instant.parse("2026-01-05T10:00:00Z"), ZoneOffset.UTC);
        var data = List.of(List.of("Dune", "3"), List.of("Emma", "1"));
        System.out.print(new CsvReport("Loans", clock, data).render());
        System.out.println();
        System.out.print(new MarkdownReport("Loans", clock, data).render());
    }
}
Loans (2026-01-05)
Dune,3
Emma,1

# Loans (2026-01-05)
| Dune | 3 |
| Emma | 1 |
_2 rows_

Protected abstract hooks

Steps and hooks are protected because their only legitimate caller is the template. Public steps would let clients call formatRow without a header, or rows() twice, and every subclass would then need to defend against out-of-order calls. Note that L36's access rule lets an override widen a protected step to public; don't. The JDK uses the same shape: java.io.InputStream has one abstract method, read(), and builds read(byte[], int, int), skip and transferTo on top of it; AbstractList needs only get and size to provide an entire read-only List; servlet frameworks dispatch from a template service method to doGet/doPost hooks.

Abstract or concrete? Design decisions

ChooseWhen
Abstract base classThe base concept has no sensible instances ("a report in no format"), subclasses share state and invariants enforced in one constructor, and the algorithm's order must be fixed.
Concrete base classThe base is itself a useful default and subclasses are genuine refinements. Then design it for extension (Effective Java Item 19) or make it final.
Interface (+ skeletal abstract class)Unrelated classes should share a type, or a class needs several such types. Publish the interface; offer an AbstractX helper for implementors (Item 20). See L39.
Strategy object or lambdaOnly one step varies. Pass it in (new Report(title, clock, formatter)) instead of subclassing; this is composition (L42) and the style of Spring's callback templates such as JdbcTemplate.
What breaks in production
  • A non-final template. If render() isn't final, some subclass will override it and skip a step, typically the one that does auditing, escaping or a permission check.
  • Calling abstract steps from the constructor. Report's constructor calling rows() would run CsvReport.rows() before data is assigned and return null (L34).
  • Deep abstract hierarchies. AbstractBaseCrudServiceImpl three levels up, with hooks overridden at different levels, forces readers to bounce up and down the hierarchy to follow one call. Keep it to one abstract level, or switch to composition.
  • Adding an abstract method to a published base class breaks every existing subclass at compile time. Add a concrete method with a default instead.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Run Reports and check the output above. Write new Report("x", clock) and read the compiler error, then make it compile as an anonymous subclass.
  2. Add HtmlReport producing a <table>, overriding only what it must. Which hook did you need that doesn't exist yet? Add it to Report without breaking the other two subclasses.
  3. Remove final from render(), write a subclass that overrides it and drops the header, and explain in one sentence what final was protecting.
  4. Open the source of java.io.InputStream in your IDE. List which methods are abstract, which are built on read(), and which a fast subclass such as FileInputStream overrides for performance.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 19, 20 · Gamma, Helm, Johnson & Vlissides, Design Patterns, “Template Method” · JLS §8.1.1.1 (abstract classes), §8.4.3.1 (abstract methods) · Javadoc: java.io.InputStream, java.util.AbstractList

L39 · DAY 39

Interfaces: Contracts & Multiple Inheritance of Type

45m video45m practice

An interface is a type with no implementation commitments: it says what an object can do, never how or with what state. Because a class can implement any number of them, interfaces are how Java gets multiple inheritance of type, and how backend code keeps business logic independent of databases, networks and frameworks.

Declaring an interface

Definition · interface members (JLS Ch. 9)
  • Abstract methods are implicitly public abstract; writing either modifier is redundant.
  • Fields are implicitly public static final: constants only. There is no instance state.
  • No constructors and no instance initializers. You can't new an interface, only a class that implements it (or an anonymous class or lambda, L41).
  • Since Java 8 and 9 an interface may also contain default, static and private methods with bodies; L40 covers them.
record Book(String isbn, String title) {}            // records: L47

interface BookRepository {                           // the contract
    Optional<Book> findByIsbn(String isbn);          // public abstract, implicitly
    void save(Book book);
}

final class InMemoryBookRepository implements BookRepository, Iterable<Book> {
    private final Map<String, Book> books = new LinkedHashMap<>();

    @Override public Optional<Book> findByIsbn(String isbn) {
        return Optional.ofNullable(books.get(isbn));
    }
    @Override public void save(Book b)          { books.put(b.isbn(), b); }
    @Override public Iterator<Book> iterator()  { return books.values().iterator(); }
}

final class LoanService {
    private final BookRepository books;              // depends on the contract only
    LoanService(BookRepository books) { this.books = books; }

    String lend(String isbn) {
        Optional<Book> book = books.findByIsbn(isbn);
        return book.isPresent() ? "lent " + book.get().title() : "no such book";
    }
}

The implementing methods must be declared public. Leave the modifier off and the method is package-private, which is weaker than the interface's implicit public: attempting to assign weaker access privileges; was public (the rule from L36).

implements, and multiple inheritance of type

InMemoryBookRepository is simultaneously a BookRepository (so LoanService accepts it) and an Iterable<Book> (so for (Book b : repo) works). A class has one superclass but any number of interfaces, and an interface may extend several others: Deque<E> extends Queue<E>, which extends Collection<E>. This is safe in a way that multiple inheritance of classes isn't, because interfaces carry no fields and no constructors. If two interfaces declare the same abstract method, one implementation satisfies both. If they declare the same method with incompatible return types, no class can implement both, and the compiler says so. Conflicting default bodies are L40's diamond problem.

Interfaces as contracts, and dependency inversion

An interface is a contract in two directions. Callers may rely only on what it states, so they keep working with any implementation; implementors must honour everything it states, including what its Javadoc promises about nulls, ordering, exceptions and thread safety. The method signatures are the part the compiler checks; the documentation is the rest of the contract.

Principle · dependency inversion (Robert C. Martin)

High-level policy should not depend on low-level detail; both should depend on abstractions. And the abstraction belongs to the policy: BookRepository is declared next to LoanService, in terms the domain understands, and the JDBC or JPA implementation lives in an infrastructure package and depends inward on it.

This is interface-based programming: LoanService receives its repository through the constructor and never names a concrete class. A test can run it in microseconds against the in-memory implementation, and swapping JDBC for JPA (Phase 10) touches no business code.

Constants in interfaces: the anti-pattern

interface Limits { int MAX_LOANS = 5; }       // public static final, implicitly

class Member implements Limits {              // "implements" just to skip the prefix
    boolean canBorrow(int current) { return current < MAX_LOANS; }
}

This is the constant interface anti-pattern (Effective Java Item 22). implements is a statement about type, so Member now claims to be a Limits; the constant becomes part of Member's public API and of every subclass's, permanently. The JDK has a few historical examples, such as java.io.ObjectStreamConstants. Put constants in the class or enum they belong to, or in a non-instantiable utility class (L33), and use a static import if the prefix bothers you. Remember too that such constants are inlined into callers at compile time (L34).

Abstract class or interface?

InterfaceAbstract class
How many per classany numberexactly one superclass
Instance state, constructorsnone (constants only)yes
Member accesspublic, plus private helpers (Java 9+)any
Retrofit onto an existing classeasy: add implementshard: the class may already have a superclass
Adding a method laterdefault method (L40)concrete method
Best fortypes, capabilities, boundariesshared state plus a fixed algorithm (L38)

The usual answer is both: publish the interface as the type, and offer an abstract skeletal implementation for convenience, as List and AbstractList do (Effective Java Item 20).

Interfaces you will use constantly

InterfaceSingle methodWhat implementing it unlocks
Comparable<T>int compareTo(T o)natural ordering: Collections.sort, TreeMap keys (L45)
Runnablevoid run()a unit of work for a Thread or executor (Phase 5)
Iterable<T>Iterator<T> iterator()the enhanced for loop (L23)
AutoCloseablevoid close()try-with-resources (L52)

Each shows the interface idea at its smallest: a single capability, written once, that any class can opt into and that the language or library then knows how to use.

What breaks in production
  • Leaky contracts. Optional<Book> findByIsbn(String isbn) throws SQLException ties every caller to JDBC, and returning ArrayList instead of List pins the implementation. The contract should speak the domain's language only.
  • Fat interfaces. A twenty-method BookService forces every implementation, including test fakes, to stub methods it doesn't care about, and some end up throwing UnsupportedOperationException. Split by client need (interface segregation).
  • Adding an abstract method to a published interface breaks every implementor, including ones in other teams' code. This exact problem is why Java 8 added default methods.
  • Ceremony. An IFooService/FooServiceImpl pair for every class, with one implementation each, adds files without adding decoupling. Extract an interface at real boundaries (storage, network, clock, payment provider) or when a second implementation exists.

Practice · 45 min

  1. Build BookRepository, InMemoryBookRepository and LoanService, and write a test of lend against the in-memory implementation.
  2. Make the repository AutoCloseable as well (print "closed"), and use it in both a for-each loop and a try-with-resources block.
  3. Remove public from save in the implementation and read the error.
  4. Declare interface A { String id(); } and interface B { String id(); } and implement both with one method. Then change B.id() to return long and explain the error.
  5. Replace the Limits constant interface with a better home and check that Member's public API no longer contains MAX_LOANS.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 20, 22, 64 · JLS Ch. 9 (§9.3 field declarations, §9.4 method declarations), §8.1.5 (superinterfaces) · Robert C. Martin, Agile Software Development: Principles, Patterns, and Practices, on the Dependency-Inversion and Interface-Segregation principles

L40 · DAY 40

default, static & private Interface Methods

40m video40m practice

Default methods let an interface gain new methods without breaking the classes that already implement it. They are a tool for evolving APIs, not a way to turn interfaces into classes: they still have no state, a class's own method always wins over them, and two conflicting defaults must be resolved by hand.

Why Java 8 needed default methods

Before Java 8, adding a method to an interface broke every implementation, because each class suddenly lacked a body for it (L39). That made the collections interfaces effectively frozen in 1998. Lambdas and streams needed Iterable.forEach, Collection.stream, Collection.removeIf, List.sort and Map.computeIfAbsent, on interfaces implemented by thousands of classes outside the JDK.

Definition · default method

An interface method declared with the default modifier and a body. Any implementing class that doesn't declare the method itself inherits that body. Existing implementations compile and run unchanged and gain the method for free; adding one is source- and binary-compatible for them (JLS §13.5.6, with the conflict caveat below).

The same mechanism let Java 21 add getFirst() and reversed() to lists and deques through the new SequencedCollection interface (JEP 431).

Writing defaults, statics and private helpers

interface Notifier {
    void send(String to, String msg);                    // the one abstract method

    default void sendAll(List<String> recipients, String msg) {   // default
        for (String to : recipients) send(to, normalise(msg));
    }

    static Notifier console() {                          // static factory
        return new Notifier() {                          // anonymous class, L49
            @Override public void send(String to, String msg) {
                System.out.println(to + " <- " + msg);
            }
        };
    }

    private String normalise(String msg) {               // private (Java 9+)
        return clip(msg.strip(), 40);
    }
    private static String clip(String s, int max) {      // private static
        return s.length() <= max ? s : s.substring(0, max) + "...";
    }
}
  • Default methods are implicitly public. They can call the interface's other methods, abstract ones included, because by the time a default runs there is a concrete object implementing all of them. They can't touch instance fields, since an interface has none.
  • Static methods belong to the interface and are called through its name: Notifier.console(), List.of(...), Comparator.comparing(...), Map.entry(k, v). Unlike static methods of a superclass, they are not inherited by implementing classes: ArrayList.of(1) fails with cannot find symbol. They are the natural home for factories, replacing companion utility classes such as Collections.
  • Private methods (Java 9) share code between defaults without adding it to the public API. A private static one can also serve static methods.

A default method may not override a method of Object: default boolean equals(Object o) is rejected with default method equals in interface E overrides a member of java.lang.Object. Every class inherits equals from Object, and the class-wins rule below would make such a default unreachable anyway.

Overriding a default method

An implementing class overrides a default like any other method, usually because it can do the job better:

final class SmsGateway implements Notifier {
    @Override public void send(String to, String msg) {
        System.out.println("SMS " + to + ": " + msg);
    }
    @Override public void sendAll(List<String> recipients, String msg) {
        System.out.println("one batch call for " + recipients.size()); // cheaper
    }
}

The JDK does this constantly. List.sort's default copies the list into an array, sorts it with Arrays.sort, and writes the elements back through a ListIterator. That works for any list, but ArrayList already has an array, so it overrides sort to sort its backing array in place. Comparator.reversed() is a default returning Collections.reverseOrder(this); Comparator.comparing and naturalOrder are static factories on the same interface. Iterable.forEach's default is just an enhanced for loop calling the action.

The diamond problem and how Java resolves it

interface Auditable { default String describe() { return "audited"; } }
interface Versioned { default String describe() { return "v1"; } }

final class Document implements Auditable, Versioned {
    @Override public String describe() {         // mandatory: defaults conflict
        return Auditable.super.describe() + ", " + Versioned.super.describe();
    }
}

class Base { public String describe() { return "base"; } }
class Memo extends Base implements Auditable {}  // describe() is "base": rule 1

Without the override, javac reports types Auditable and Versioned are incompatible; class Document inherits unrelated defaults for describe() from types Auditable and Versioned. Auditable.super.describe() is the syntax for "call that interface's default on this object"; it works only for interfaces the class implements directly. Rule 1 means a superclass method, even an abstract one, beats any default, which keeps pre-Java-8 class hierarchies behaving exactly as before.

Design note · what defaults are for

Use defaults to evolve a published interface, or for convenience methods expressible purely in terms of the abstract ones (sendAll via send, reversed via compare). Don't use them to smuggle in implementation that needs state; that is a job for an abstract skeletal class or for composition. Effective Java Item 21 is blunt: design interfaces for posterity, because a default can't always be written so that it works for every existing implementation.

What breaks in production
  • A default that breaks an existing implementation's invariants. Item 21's example: Java 8's default Collection.removeIf knows nothing about locking, so a third-party synchronized collection wrapper that didn't override it suddenly had an unsynchronized mutating method. The JDK's own Collections.synchronizedCollection had to override removeIf to stay correct.
  • Class wins, silently. If an implementing class already has a method with the signature a library later adds as a default, the class's version is used, with whatever contract it had. The compiler sees a perfectly valid override, written years before the default existed.
  • New conflicts in old binaries. If a library update adds a default that conflicts with another interface's default, classes compiled against the old version are not recompiled and fail only when the method is called, with IncompatibleClassChangeError.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Implement Notifier and SmsGateway; call sendAll on both and on Notifier.console().
  2. Add a default void sendUrgent(String to, String msg) that prefixes "URGENT". Confirm SmsGateway compiles untouched. Then make it abstract instead and count what breaks.
  3. Reproduce the Auditable/Versioned error, resolve it three ways (pick one, pick the other, combine), and demonstrate rule 1 with Memo.
  4. Read the source of List.sort, ArrayList.sort and Comparator.reversed. Write one sentence on why ArrayList overrides sort, and one on why no comparator ever needs to override reversed.
  5. Try ArrayList.of(1) and default boolean equals(Object o) and explain both errors.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 20, 21 · JLS §9.4 (method declarations), §9.4.1.3 (inheriting methods with override-equivalent signatures), §8.4.8.4, §15.12.1 (TypeName.super) · Javadoc: Iterable.forEach, List.sort, Comparator · JEP 431, “Sequenced Collections”

L41 · DAY 41

Marker & Functional Interfaces

35m video35m practice

Two special shapes of interface: one with no methods at all, which exists only so code can ask "is this object one of these?", and one with exactly one abstract method, which turns a single piece of behaviour into a value you can pass around. The second is the foundation for every lambda in Phase 3.

Marker interfaces and who checks them

Definition · marker interface

An interface with no methods, implemented to declare a property of a class. The marker adds no behaviour; some library or JVM code tests for it with instanceof (or the reflective equivalent) and changes what it does.

MarkerChecked byWithout it
java.io.SerializableObjectOutputStream.writeObjectNotSerializableException naming the class
java.lang.Cloneablethe native Object.clone()CloneNotSupportedException
java.util.RandomAccessCollections algorithms such as binarySearchiterator-based instead of index-based access
 
record Loan(String isbn) {}                     // not Serializable

try (var out = new ObjectOutputStream(OutputStream.nullOutputStream())) {
    out.writeObject(new Loan("978-0"));
} catch (NotSerializableException e) {
    System.out.println("refused: " + e.getMessage()); // the class name
}

RandomAccess is the instructive one. ArrayList implements it and LinkedList doesn't, so Collections.binarySearch uses list.get(mid) on the first and walks a ListIterator on the second (for large lists), avoiding an accidental O(n²) search. The marker turns a performance property into a type that algorithms can test.

Annotations as the modern replacement, mostly

Annotations (L114) can mark any program element, not just classes: methods, fields, parameters. They can carry data (@Table(name = "books")) and are what frameworks use today: @Entity, @Component, @Test. Effective Java Item 41 keeps one advantage for marker interfaces: they define a type, so misuse can be caught at compile time. A method declared save(Serializable s) rejects non-serializable arguments before the program runs, whereas an annotation can only be checked reflectively at run time or by an annotation processor. The rule of thumb: if you would ever write a method that accepts only marked objects, use a marker interface; otherwise use an annotation.

Functional interfaces: exactly one abstract method

Definition · functional interface (JLS §9.8)

An interface with exactly one abstract method, its single abstract method (SAM). Methods that do not count: default methods, static methods, private methods, and abstract redeclarations of Object's public methods such as boolean equals(Object), since every implementation inherits those from Object anyway. That is why Comparator, which redeclares equals and has dozens of default and static methods, is still functional.

@FunctionalInterface and writing your own

@FunctionalInterface
interface PriceRule {
    long apply(long cents);                          // the SAM

    default PriceRule then(PriceRule next) {         // doesn't count
        PriceRule first = this;
        return new PriceRule() {
            @Override public long apply(long c) { return next.apply(first.apply(c)); }
        };
    }

    static PriceRule identity() {                    // doesn't count
        return new PriceRule() {
            @Override public long apply(long c) { return c; }
        };
    }

    boolean equals(Object o);                        // doesn't count: Object's
}

@FunctionalInterface is optional; any interface with one abstract method is functional. The annotation adds a compile-time check: add a second abstract method and javac stops with Unexpected @FunctionalInterface annotation … multiple non-overriding abstract methods found in interface PriceRule. Put it on every interface you intend callers to implement with lambdas, so that a well-meaning change can't break all of them (Effective Java Item 44).

Implementing it today: anonymous classes

PriceRule tenPercentOff = new PriceRule() {
    @Override public long apply(long cents) { return cents * 90 / 100; }
};
PriceRule fiveOff = new PriceRule() {
    @Override public long apply(long cents) { return Math.max(0, cents - 500); }
};
System.out.println(tenPercentOff.then(fiveOff).apply(10_000));   // 8500

// From L81 the same two rules are one line each:
// PriceRule tenPercentOff = cents -> cents * 90 / 100;
// PriceRule fiveOff = cents -> Math.max(0, cents - 500);

An anonymous class declares and instantiates an unnamed implementation in one expression (L49 covers its rules). Five lines of ceremony around one line of logic is exactly what lambdas remove in L81. The important idea arrives now, though: tenPercentOff is an object whose whole purpose is one behaviour, and then builds new behaviour by combining two of them. That is functional composition, and Function.andThen (L83) is the same method in the JDK.

A preview of java.util.function

InterfaceAbstract methodShape
Supplier<T>T get()nothing in, a value out
Consumer<T>void accept(T t)a value in, nothing out
Function<T, R>R apply(T t)transform one value into another
Predicate<T>boolean test(T t)yes/no question about a value
UnaryOperator<T>, BinaryOperator<T>T apply(T), T apply(T, T)same type in and out
LongUnaryOperator, IntPredicate, …applyAsLong, test, …primitive versions, no boxing (L94)

Older JDK interfaces became functional retroactively: Runnable, Callable<V>, Comparator<T>. PriceRule is structurally a LongUnaryOperator. Item 44 says to prefer the standard interface unless yours earns its keep: a domain name that documents intent, a stronger contract, or useful default methods such as then.

What breaks in production
  • Serializable is a commitment, and a risk. Adding it freezes the class's private fields as a public wire format, and deserializing untrusted bytes has been the root of many remote-code-execution vulnerabilities. Effective Java Item 85: prefer JSON or another explicit format. L102 covers it.
  • Breaking a functional interface. Adding a second abstract method to an un-annotated public functional interface compiles in your module and breaks every caller that implemented it with a lambda.
  • this in anonymous classes. Inside an anonymous class, this is the anonymous object, not the enclosing one, which is why then saves first = this before creating it. Lambdas change this rule (L81), so mechanical conversions between the two can change behaviour.

Practice · 35 min

  1. Write PriceRule and three anonymous implementations (percentage off, fixed amount off, a floor price). Compose all three with then and test the result.
  2. Add long undo(long cents) to PriceRule. Read the @FunctionalInterface error, then remove the annotation: what do your anonymous classes now have to do?
  3. Serialize a record to a ByteArrayOutputStream without and then with implements Serializable; print the byte count.
  4. Name the standard functional interface for each shape: ISBN string to Book; nothing to UUID; Book to boolean; long to long; two BigDecimals to one.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 41, 44, 85 · JLS §9.8 (functional interfaces), §9.6.4.9 (@FunctionalInterface) · Javadoc: package java.util.function, java.io.Serializable, java.util.RandomAccess

L42 · DAY 42

Composition Over Inheritance & Delegation

40m video45m practice

Extending a class you don't control couples you to how it is implemented, not just to what it promises, and the coupling breaks without warning when that implementation changes. Holding an instance and forwarding to it gives you the same reuse while depending only on the public contract.

Inheritance breaks encapsulation: the InstrumentedHashSet

Effective Java Item 18's example: a set that counts how many elements were ever added.

class InstrumentedHashSet<E> extends HashSet<E> {
    private int addCount = 0;

    @Override public boolean add(E e) {
        addCount++;
        return super.add(e);
    }
    @Override public boolean addAll(Collection<? extends E> c) {   // ? extends: L76
        addCount += c.size();
        return super.addAll(c);
    }
    int addCount() { return addCount; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var s = new InstrumentedHashSet<String>();
        s.addAll(List.of("Dune", "Emma", "Ulysses"));
        System.out.println(s.addCount());           // 6, not 3
    }
}

HashSet inherits addAll from AbstractCollection, which loops and calls add for each element. That add is a virtual call (L37), so it lands in the override, and every element is counted twice. The subclass's correctness depends on a self-use detail of the parent, which is documented in AbstractCollection's Javadoc but is not part of the Set contract at all. Deleting the addAll override "fixes" it, until a future JDK changes that detail.

The fragile base class problem

Definition · fragile base class

A superclass is fragile when changes that look safe in isolation (changing which methods call which, adding a method) break subclasses the author has never seen. Three typical ways: self-use changes, as above; new methods that bypass a subclass's checks, such as a new insert method in the parent that skips the subclass's validating add; and signature collisions, when the parent adds a method a subclass already has with a different meaning, which turns an unrelated method into an accidental override, or a compile error if the return types differ.

HAS-A, composition and delegation

IS-A is inheritance: the new class is a kind of the old one. HAS-A is composition: the new class holds an instance of the old one in a private field and calls it. A Library has a list of books; it is not a list. When a method of the wrapper simply calls the same method on the held object, that is forwarding or delegation. The wrapper depends on the held object's public API only, so implementation changes inside it can't leak out.

class ForwardingSet<E> implements Set<E> {      // reusable forwarding class
    private final Set<E> s;
    ForwardingSet(Set<E> s) { this.s = Objects.requireNonNull(s); }

    public int size()                  { return s.size(); }
    public boolean isEmpty()           { return s.isEmpty(); }
    public boolean contains(Object o)  { return s.contains(o); }
    public Iterator<E> iterator()      { return s.iterator(); }
    public Object[] toArray()          { return s.toArray(); }
    public <T> T[] toArray(T[] a)      { return s.toArray(a); }
    public boolean add(E e)            { return s.add(e); }
    public boolean remove(Object o)    { return s.remove(o); }
    public boolean containsAll(Collection<?> c)      { return s.containsAll(c); }
    public boolean addAll(Collection<? extends E> c) { return s.addAll(c); }
    public boolean retainAll(Collection<?> c)        { return s.retainAll(c); }
    public boolean removeAll(Collection<?> c)        { return s.removeAll(c); }
    public void clear()                { s.clear(); }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) { return s.equals(o); }
    @Override public int hashCode()    { return s.hashCode(); }
    @Override public String toString() { return s.toString(); }
}

class InstrumentedSet<E> extends ForwardingSet<E> {   // the decorator
    private int addCount;
    InstrumentedSet(Set<E> s) { super(s); }

    @Override public boolean add(E e) { addCount++; return super.add(e); }
    @Override public boolean addAll(Collection<? extends E> c) {
        addCount += c.size();
        return super.addAll(c);
    }
    int addCount() { return addCount; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var s = new InstrumentedSet<String>(new TreeSet<>());   // any Set works
        s.addAll(List.of("Dune", "Emma", "Ulysses"));
        System.out.println(s.addCount() + " " + s);    // 3 [Dune, Emma, Ulysses]
    }
}

InstrumentedSet still uses extends, but of ForwardingSet, a class written for exactly this purpose and under our control. The wrapped set's internal calls go to its own add, never back to the wrapper. A bonus: it now instruments any Set, a TreeSet as easily as a HashSet, which the subclass could never do.

The Decorator pattern and wrapper classes

InstrumentedSet is a decorator: it implements the same interface as the object it wraps, forwards to it, and adds behaviour around the calls. Decorators stack, because each one is itself a valid instance of the interface. java.io is built this way, as in new BufferedInputStream(new FileInputStream(path)), and so are Collections.unmodifiableList(list) and Collections.synchronizedMap(map). Effective Java calls them wrapper classes (unrelated to L54's Integer and friends). In backend code, decorators add retries, metrics or caching around a repository or HTTP client without touching it; Spring's AOP proxies (Phase 12) are generated decorators.

When inheritance is genuinely right

  • A true subtype. Every instance of B is an A in every situation A's contract covers (L36's substitution principle).
  • Designed for extension. The parent documents its self-use with @implSpec, as AbstractCollection does, offers deliberate protected hooks (L38), and never calls overridable methods from constructors (L34).
  • Same ownership. Parent and child live in one package or module, maintained by the same people, so self-use changes are made knowingly.

Effective Java Item 19 states the rule: design and document for inheritance, or else prohibit it. Prohibit by making the class final, or by making constructors private and offering static factories. Most classes in a backend code base should be final.

Refactoring a hierarchy into composition

A library starts with Member, adds StudentMember and PremiumMember that override maxLoans() and lateFeePerDay(), and then a premium student needs PremiumStudentMember. Every new dimension multiplies the classes, and a student who upgrades needs a different object, because an object can never change its class. Extract the varying behaviour into a value the member has:

record LoanPolicy(int maxLoans, long lateFeePerDayCents) {
    static final LoanPolicy STANDARD = new LoanPolicy(5, 25);
    static final LoanPolicy STUDENT  = new LoanPolicy(8, 10);
    LoanPolicy premium() {
        return new LoanPolicy(maxLoans * 2, lateFeePerDayCents / 2);
    }
}

final class Member {                             // final: no subclasses
    private final String name;
    private LoanPolicy policy;                       // HAS-A, swappable at run time

    Member(String name, LoanPolicy policy) {
        this.name = name;
        this.policy = Objects.requireNonNull(policy);
    }
    boolean canBorrow(int currentLoans) { return currentLoans < policy.maxLoans(); }
    long lateFee(int daysLate) { return daysLate * policy.lateFeePerDayCents(); }
    void upgrade() { policy = policy.premium(); }    // same object, new behaviour
}

Four classes became one class plus a value; "premium student" is LoanPolicy.STUDENT.premium(). This is the Strategy pattern, the composition counterpart of L38's Template Method.

What breaks in production
  • Subclassing library classes you don't own. Extending HashMap, ArrayList or a framework's base client to add a check works until an upgrade changes self-use or adds a method that bypasses your override.
  • Stale decorators. A forwarding class written before Java 8 doesn't forward removeIf or forEach; their default implementations run against the wrapper instead of the delegate, which may be slower or may skip the delegate's own locking.
  • The SELF problem. If the wrapped object passes this to a callback registry, it hands out itself, not the wrapper, and callbacks bypass the decoration (Item 18).

Practice · 45 min

  1. Run InstrumentedHashSet, then open AbstractCollection.addAll in your IDE and find the sentence in its Javadoc that documents the self-use.
  2. Build ForwardingSet and InstrumentedSet; wrap a HashSet, a TreeSet and an unmodifiable Set.of(). What happens on add for the last one, and whose behaviour is that?
  3. Write LoggingNotifier and RetryingNotifier decorators for L40's Notifier and stack them in both orders. Does the order change what gets logged?
  4. Refactor a three-subclass Member hierarchy into LoanPolicy, then add an "overdue-suspended" policy without adding a class.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 18, 19 · Gamma et al., Design Patterns, “Decorator” and “Strategy” · Mikhajlov & Sekerinski 1998, “A Study of the Fragile Base Class Problem” · Javadoc: java.util.AbstractCollection

L43 · DAY 43

Object.equals & hashCode: The Contract

50m video50m practice

equals and hashCode are not methods you call so much as promises that every collection relies on. Break the equals contract and sets, maps and contains give answers that depend on argument order; break the link between the two methods and a HashMap can't find a key you just put in.

Identity by default

Object.equals is this == obj: two references are equal only if they point to the same object. That is right for objects with identity, such as a thread, a connection, or a service, and wrong for values such as an ISBN, a money amount or a date range, where two separately created objects with the same content should be interchangeable. A class whose instances are values overrides equals, and then must override hashCode too.

The five-part contract

Definition · the equality contract (Javadoc)

For non-null references x, y, z:

  1. Reflexive: x.equals(x) is true.
  2. Symmetric: x.equals(y) is true exactly when y.equals(x) is.
  3. Transitive: if x.equals(y) and y.equals(z), then x.equals(z).
  4. Consistent: repeated calls give the same answer as long as the fields used don't change.
  5. Non-null: x.equals(null) is false, and never throws.

Writing a correct equals

final class Edition {
    private final String isbn;
    private final int year;
    private final double price;
    private final String[] authors;

    Edition(String isbn, int year, double price, String... authors) {
        this.isbn = Objects.requireNonNull(isbn);
        this.year = year;
        this.price = price;
        this.authors = authors.clone();
    }

    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        if (this == o) return true;                       // 1. identity shortcut
        if (!(o instanceof Edition other)) return false;  // 2. type (and null) check
        return year == other.year                         // 3. cheap fields first
            && Double.compare(price, other.price) == 0    //    doubles via compare
            && isbn.equals(other.isbn)                     //    objects via equals
            && Arrays.equals(authors, other.authors);      //    arrays via Arrays
    }

    @Override public int hashCode() {                     // same fields as equals
        int h = isbn.hashCode();
        h = 31 * h + Integer.hashCode(year);
        h = 31 * h + Double.hashCode(price);
        h = 31 * h + Arrays.hashCode(authors);
        return h;
    }
}
  • The == shortcut is an optimisation and also guarantees reflexivity cheaply.
  • The parameter type must be Object. equals(Edition) is an overload that collections never call (L36); @Override catches it.
  • instanceof returns false for null, so step 2 handles rule 5 for free, and the pattern binding (L37) avoids a separate cast.
  • float/double fields go through Float.compare/Double.compare. With ==, NaN is not equal to itself, which breaks reflexivity, and 0.0 == -0.0 is true although the two have different Double.hashCode values, which breaks the hash contract.
  • Array fields need Arrays.equals (or Arrays.deepEquals for nested arrays); an array's own equals is identity.
  • Nullable object fields use Objects.equals(a, b), which is null-safe.

getClass() or instanceof?

if (o == null || getClass() != o.getClass()) return false; makes instances of different classes never equal, even a subclass that adds nothing. That violates Liskov substitution (L36), and it breaks with frameworks that subclass your class at run time: a Hibernate lazy-loading proxy is a generated subclass, so it is never equal to the real entity. instanceof lets a subclass instance equal a superclass instance, which is correct as long as the subclass doesn't add state that takes part in equality. When it does, neither style works, as the next section shows. The clean answer is to make value classes final, which records (L47) do automatically.

How inheritance and interop break symmetry and transitivity

final class CaseInsensitiveString {
    private final String s;
    CaseInsensitiveString(String s) { this.s = Objects.requireNonNull(s); }

    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        if (o instanceof CaseInsensitiveString c) return s.equalsIgnoreCase(c.s);
        if (o instanceof String str) return s.equalsIgnoreCase(str); // one-way!
        return false;
    }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return s.toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT).hashCode(); }
}
// cis.equals("Polish") == true, but "Polish".equals(cis) == false

String knows nothing about this class, so the interop is one-directional, and whether list.contains("Polish") finds cis depends on which side the list implementation calls equals on (Effective Java Item 10). Delete the String branch. Inheritance produces the transitivity version: let ColorPoint extends Point compare colour with other ColorPoints but ignore it against plain Points. Then red(1,2) equals plain (1,2), and plain (1,2) equals blue(1,2), yet red is not blue. Item 10's conclusion: there is no way to extend an instantiable class and add a value component while preserving the contract. Use composition (L42): ColorPoint has a Point.

The hashCode contract, and how HashMap uses it

Contract · hash codes (Javadoc)
  1. Repeated calls return the same value, as long as the fields used by equals don't change.
  2. If x.equals(y), then x.hashCode() == y.hashCode().
  3. Unequal objects may share a hash code, but distinct codes make hash tables faster.

Writing it. Combine the hash of each field used in equals: start from the first, then h = 31 * h + fieldHash for each of the rest (Item 11). 31 is odd, so multiplying by it is reversible even when it overflows and loses no bits (an even multiplier would shift information out), and 31 * h equals (h << 5) - h, which is cheap. The ordering makes ("ab", "c") and ("a", "bc") hash differently. Objects.hash(isbn, year, price) does the same in one line, at the cost of a varargs array and boxing on every call, which is fine everywhere except hot paths.

What breaks in production
  • Mutable keys. Put a Book in a HashSet, then change its ISBN: it now sits in the bucket for the old hash, contains returns false, and remove can't delete it, yet iteration still shows it.
  • equals without hashCode. Duplicate rows in a "deduplicated" set, and a cache keyed by such objects that never hits and grows forever.
  • Arrays inside Objects.hash. Objects.hash(isbn, authors) hashes the array by identity. Use Arrays.hashCode(authors) as the argument.
  • Lombok on entities. @EqualsAndHashCode (and @Data) uses all non-static, non-transient fields: on a JPA entity that pulls in lazy collections, can recurse forever across a bidirectional association (StackOverflowError), and uses an ID that is null until the entity is saved, so the hash changes after persist. On subclasses it defaults to callSuper = false and ignores the parent's fields, a choice Lombok itself warns about. For entities, compare by ID when it is set and use a constant hashCode (Phase 10).
  • Stale generated code. IDE-generated equals is correct on the day it is generated; add a field next month and forget to regenerate, and equality silently ignores it. Records remove the problem.

Practice · 50 min

  1. Implement Edition and write one test per contract rule, plus a test that equal editions have equal hash codes. Include a NaN price and a -0.0 price.
  2. Delete hashCode. Show a HashSet holding two equal editions, and a HashMap returning null for an equal key.
  3. Reproduce the mutable-key bug: add, mutate, then print contains, remove and the set itself.
  4. Write Point and ColorPoint extends Point and demonstrate the transitivity failure with three objects. Then redesign with composition.
  5. Generate equals/hashCode for Edition with your IDE, once with the getClass template and once with instanceof. Compare with the hand-written version, then rewrite it as a record and compare line counts.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 10, 11 · Javadoc: Object.equals, Object.hashCode, Objects.hash, Arrays.equals, Double.compare · Project Lombok documentation, @EqualsAndHashCode

L44 · DAY 44

toString, clone, getClass & the Objects Utility

35m video35m practice

The rest of Object's methods sort into one to always override (toString), one to use as is (getClass), and two to avoid (clone and finalize), each of which has a better modern replacement. java.util.Objects fills in the null-safe helpers that Object itself lacks.

Overriding toString, without leaking secrets

Object.toString returns getClass().getName() + "@" + Integer.toHexString(hashCode()), as in Member@1b6d3586, which tells you nothing in a log line or a debugger. Override it to show the fields a human needs to identify the object (Effective Java Item 12). Its audience is developers reading logs, so it must also be safe to log: no passwords, tokens, card numbers, or personal data beyond what your logging policy allows.

record Credentials(String username, String password) {
    @Override public String toString() {            // the generated one would
        return "Credentials[username=" + username + ", password=***]";  // print it
    }
}

final class Member {
    private final long id;
    private final String name;
    private final List<String> loans = new ArrayList<>();
    Member(long id, String name) { this.id = id; this.name = name; }

    @Override public String toString() {             // identify, don't dump
        return "Member[id=" + id + ", name=" + name + ", loans=" + loans.size() + "]";
    }
}

Note the record: a record's generated toString prints every component, so a record holding a secret needs an explicit override. Summarise collections (loans.size()) rather than printing them; a member with 3,000 loans, or an entity whose collection loads lazily from the database, should not be dumped into one log line.

getClass() and the runtime type

getClass() is final and returns the Class object of the runtime type (L37): pm.getClass() is Card.class even when pm is declared as PaymentMethod. Compare with the class literal PaymentMethod.class, which is fixed at compile time, and with instanceof, which also accepts subclasses. Useful methods on the result: getName() (com.acme.Card, or Outer$Inner for nested classes), getSimpleName() (empty for anonymous classes), and the reflection API of L116. Expect surprises with generated subclasses: a Hibernate lazy proxy's getClass() is the proxy's class, not your entity's.

clone(), Cloneable, shallow and deep copies

Definition · cloning

A protected native method. If the object's class implements the marker Cloneable (L41), it allocates a new object of the same class without running any constructor and copies every field bit for bit; otherwise it throws the checked CloneNotSupportedException. Copying references bit for bit makes it a shallow copy: the clone and the original share every object their fields point to.

class Shelf implements Cloneable {
    private List<String> books = new ArrayList<>();   // can't be final: see below
    void add(String b)   { books.add(b); }
    List<String> books() { return books; }

    @Override public Shelf clone() {                  // public, covariant return
        try {
            Shelf copy = (Shelf) super.clone();       // shallow: same list
            copy.books = new ArrayList<>(books);      // make it deep by hand
            return copy;
        } catch (CloneNotSupportedException e) {
            throw new AssertionError(e);              // can't happen: Cloneable
        }
    }
}

Why clone is broken (Effective Java Item 13): Cloneable has no clone method, so the interface doesn't let you call it, and implementing it instead changes the behaviour of a protected method in a superclass; the copy skips constructors and their validation; deep copying requires non-final fields, which defeats immutability (L32); every class in the hierarchy must cooperate by calling super.clone(); and callers must deal with a checked exception that can never happen. The one place to use it is arrays: int[] copy = original.clone() is correct and idiomatic.

Copy constructors and static factories instead

final class Library {
    private final List<String> books;                  // final works now
    Library(List<String> books) { this.books = new ArrayList<>(books); }

    Library(Library other) { this(other.books); }       // copy constructor
    static Library copyOf(Library other) { return new Library(other); } // factory
}

Both run a constructor, so validation runs too; fields can be final; there is no exception and no marker; and the parameter can be an interface, making it a conversion constructor: new ArrayList<>(anyCollection), new TreeSet<>(hashSet), List.copyOf(...). That is how the collections framework itself does copying.

finalize() is deprecated; use Cleaner only as a safety net

finalize() ran at some unpredictable time after the object became unreachable, or never, on a single finalizer thread; it could resurrect the object; it slowed allocation and collection; and it enabled finalizer attacks on half-constructed objects. It was deprecated in Java 9 and deprecated for removal in Java 18 (JEP 421). Release resources deterministically with close() and try-with-resources (L52). java.lang.ref.Cleaner (Java 9) is the supported safety net for the case where a caller forgets:

final class NativeBuffer implements AutoCloseable {
    private static final Cleaner CLEANER = Cleaner.create();

    private record Release(long address) implements Runnable {  // no reference
        @Override public void run() {                            // to NativeBuffer!
            System.out.println("freeing " + address);
        }
    }

    private final Cleaner.Cleanable cleanable;
    NativeBuffer(long address) {
        cleanable = CLEANER.register(this, new Release(address));
    }

    @Override public void close() { cleanable.clean(); }        // runs at most once
}

The cleanup action must not refer to the object being watched: if Release were an inner class or a lambda capturing this, the object could never become unreachable and the action would never run.

java.util.Objects, and two annotations

String title = null;
Objects.requireNonNull("Dune", "title");           // returns "Dune"
Objects.equals(title, null);                       // true: null-safe
Objects.hashCode(title);                           // 0 for null
Objects.toString(title, "untitled");               // "untitled"
Objects.isNull(title);                             // true; nonNull for filters
Objects.requireNonNullElse(title, "untitled");     // "untitled"
Objects.requireNonNullElseGet(title, () -> "x");   // lazily computed default
Objects.checkIndex(2, 3);                          // 2; throws if out of range
// Objects.requireNonNull(title, "title") -> NullPointerException: title

Use requireNonNull at the top of constructors and public methods: the failure then names the parameter at the point of the mistake instead of surfacing as an anonymous NPE ten calls later.

@Deprecated(since = "2.3", forRemoval = true) marks an API that callers should stop using; javac warns at every use, with a stronger "removal" warning when forRemoval is set. Pair it with a Javadoc @deprecated tag that names the replacement. @SuppressWarnings("unchecked"), "deprecation" or "removal" silences a warning you have verified is safe; put it on the smallest possible scope, ideally one local variable declaration, with a comment explaining why (Effective Java Item 27).

What breaks in production
  • Secrets in logs. A record or a Lombok @ToString class holding a token, logged at INFO once per request, puts every customer's token in your log aggregator.
  • Recursive or expensive toString. Two JPA entities that print each other overflow the stack; one that prints a lazy collection fires a query or throws LazyInitializationException from inside a log statement.
  • Leaks behind a Cleaner. Relying on GC timing for file handles or connections exhausts them under load, long before memory pressure triggers a collection.

Practice · 35 min

  1. Print a Credentials record with and without the override, then add a toString to a class of yours that is safe to log at INFO.
  2. Clone a Shelf, add to the clone, and print both. Remove the deep-copy line and repeat.
  3. Replace Shelf.clone with a copy constructor and a copyOf factory, and make books final.
  4. Create a NativeBuffer in try-with-resources and one that you never close; drop the reference and call System.gc() a few times. When does each "freeing" line appear, and is the second one guaranteed?
  5. Deprecate a method with forRemoval = true, compile a caller with -Xlint:all, then suppress the warning on the narrowest scope javac allows.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 8, 12, 13, 27 · JEP 421, “Deprecate Finalization for Removal” · Javadoc: Object.clone, java.lang.ref.Cleaner, java.util.Objects, java.lang.Deprecated

L45 · DAY 45

Comparable & Comparator

45m video50m practice

A class gets one natural ordering, built in through Comparable; every other ordering is a separate Comparator object that you build, combine and pass to sorts and sorted collections. Both must obey a contract as strict as equals, and violating it produces lost elements or a sort that throws.

Natural ordering with compareTo

record Book(String title, String author, int year) implements Comparable<Book> {
    @Override public int compareTo(Book o) {          // natural order: title, ...
        int c = title.compareTo(o.title);
        if (c == 0) c = author.compareTo(o.author);   // ...then author...
        if (c == 0) c = Integer.compare(year, o.year); // ...then year
        return c;                                     // < 0, 0 or > 0
    }
}

x.compareTo(y) returns a negative number if x comes first, zero if they tie, and a positive number if x comes second; only the sign matters. Implementing Comparable makes a class sortable by Collections.sort, usable as a TreeMap key, and searchable by Arrays.binarySearch, without any extra arguments. String, the wrapper types, BigDecimal, LocalDate and enums (by declaration order) all have natural orderings. Compare primitive fields with Integer.compare, Long.compare and Double.compare, never with < and > chains, and never by subtraction (see below).

The contract, and consistency with equals

Definition · the ordering contract (Javadoc)
  1. Antisymmetric: sgn(x.compareTo(y)) == -sgn(y.compareTo(x)), and if one throws, so does the other.
  2. Transitive: x > y and y > z imply x > z.
  3. Ties are interchangeable: if x.compareTo(y) == 0, then x and y compare the same way against every z.
  4. Strongly recommended: (x.compareTo(y) == 0) == x.equals(y), called consistent with equals.

Unlike equals, compareTo(null) should throw NullPointerException.

Rule 4 is "recommended", but sorted collections treat it as law. TreeSet and TreeMap decide uniqueness with compareTo (or their comparator) and never call equals. BigDecimal is the famous inconsistent class: new BigDecimal("1.0") and new BigDecimal("1.00") are not equals (the scales differ) but compareTo returns 0. Put both into a HashSet and you get 2 elements; into a TreeSet, 1. The Book above compares exactly the fields the record's equals uses, so it is consistent.

Sorting: Collections.sort, List.sort, Arrays.sort

Collections.sort(list) and Collections.sort(list, cmp) just call list.sort(cmp) (L40 showed its default and ArrayList's override). Arrays.sort(Object[]) and List.sort use TimSort: a stable merge sort, O(n log n) in the worst case and close to O(n) on input that is already mostly sorted. Stability means ties keep their existing order, which is what makes "sort by year, then sort by author" give authors with years in order. Arrays.sort(int[]) and the other primitive overloads use dual-pivot quicksort, which is not stable; for primitives nobody can tell.

Comparator: other orderings, built from parts

A Comparator<T> is a separate object with int compare(T a, T b), a functional interface (L41). Use it for orderings other than the natural one, for classes you can't modify, and for classes with no natural order. Before Java 8 you wrote one as an anonymous class; since then you build them from key extractors. The Book::author syntax is a method reference, covered properly in L82: read it as "the function that takes a book and returns its author".

List<Book> books = new ArrayList<>(List.of(
        new Book("Emma", "Austen", 1815), new Book("Dune", "Herbert", 1965),
        new Book("Persuasion", "Austen", 1817), new Book("Ulysses", "Joyce", 1922)));

Comparator<Book> byYear = new Comparator<>() {         // the anonymous-class way
    @Override public int compare(Book a, Book b) {
        return Integer.compare(a.year(), b.year());
    }
};

Collections.sort(books);                                 // natural: Dune, Emma, ...
books.sort(byYear);                                      // Emma 1815 first
books.sort(Comparator.comparing(Book::author)            // author A-Z, then
        .thenComparing(Comparator.comparingInt(Book::year).reversed())); // newest
System.out.println(books);  // Persuasion, Emma, Dune, Ulysses

List<String> names = new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList("emma", null, "Dune", "anna"));
names.sort(Comparator.nullsLast(String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER));
System.out.println(names);  // [anna, Dune, emma, null]
MethodBuilds
comparing(keyFn), comparing(keyFn, keyCmp)order by an extracted key, naturally or with a given comparator
comparingInt, comparingLong, comparingDoublethe same for primitive keys, without boxing
thenComparing(...), thenComparingInt(...)a tie-breaker consulted only when the earlier keys tie
reversed()the whole comparator so far, backwards
nullsFirst(cmp), nullsLast(cmp)a null-tolerant wrapper; plain comparators throw on null
naturalOrder(), reverseOrder()the Comparable order, and its reverse

Chaining multi-field sorts

Effective Java Item 14 suggests building compareTo itself from these pieces, held in a private static final Comparator<Book>: concise, hard to get wrong, and only slightly slower than the hand-written chain. One trap belongs to Phase 3: Comparator.comparing(b -> b.title()).reversed() does not compile, because the lambda's parameter type can't be inferred through the chained call; a method reference or an explicit (Book b) -> fixes it.

The subtraction-overflow bug

Comparator<Integer> broken = new Comparator<>() {
    @Override public int compare(Integer a, Integer b) { return a - b; }  // overflows
};
System.out.println(broken.compare(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1)); // 2147483647: "MIN > 1"

a - b looks like a neat way to get the sign, and it is correct only while the difference fits in an int. Integer.MIN_VALUE - 1 wraps to Integer.MAX_VALUE (L9), so the comparator claims the most negative number is the largest. Now transitivity fails, and TimSort, which checks for this as it merges, may throw IllegalArgumentException: Comparison method violates its general contract!: sorting 100,000 random ints with broken does exactly that. The same bug hides in o1.hashCode() - o2.hashCode() tie-breakers. Use Integer.compare(a, b).

Preview: TreeSet and TreeMap

Sorted collections (L61, L65) keep elements ordered by the natural ordering or by a comparator given to the constructor, and use that same comparison for lookups and uniqueness. So new TreeSet<Book>(Comparator.comparing(Book::author)) holding our four books has size 3: Emma and Persuasion compare as equal, and one of them is silently not added. A comparator used for a sorted collection must therefore distinguish every pair of elements you consider different.

What breaks in production
  • "Comparison method violates its general contract!" appears in production, not in tests, because it needs particular data: a subtraction comparator, a comparator reading a field that changes during the sort, or inconsistent null handling.
  • Records silently merged. A TreeMap<Customer, ...> ordered by last name loses every customer who shares one. Add tie-breakers down to a unique key.
  • Locale-blind string order. String.compareTo compares UTF-16 code units: "Zoe" sorts before "anna", and "Émile" after "Zoe". For user-facing sorting use String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER at minimum, or java.text.Collator for a locale (L110).

Practice · 50 min

  1. Implement Book.compareTo by hand, then again as a static comparator chain. Write a test that checks consistency with equals on a few hundred random books.
  2. Sort the books by: author, then newest first; title ignoring case; year descending, then title. Then append .reversed() to the end of the first chain and explain the new order.
  3. Add a nullable Integer rating and sort with unrated books last, using comparing(Book::rating, nullsLast(naturalOrder())).
  4. Sort 100,000 random ints with the subtraction comparator until it throws, then fix it. Separately, show HashSet and TreeSet disagreeing about 1.0 and 1.00.
  5. Build a TreeSet with an author-only comparator, observe the lost book, and add tie-breakers until nothing is lost.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Item 14 · Javadoc: java.lang.Comparable, java.util.Comparator, Collections.sort, Arrays.sort, java.util.TreeSet

L46 · DAY 46

Enums: Far More Than Constants

45m video45m practice

A Java enum is a class with a fixed, compiler-known set of instances. Because each constant is a full object, it can carry fields and its own behaviour, implement interfaces, and act as a strategy, and because the set is closed, the compiler can check that a switch handles every case.

What enum declares

enum LoanStatus { ACTIVE, OVERDUE, RETURNED, LOST }

The compiler turns that line into, roughly:

final class LoanStatus extends java.lang.Enum<LoanStatus> {
    public static final LoanStatus ACTIVE   = new LoanStatus("ACTIVE", 0);
    public static final LoanStatus OVERDUE  = new LoanStatus("OVERDUE", 1);
    ...                                         // one object per constant
    private static final LoanStatus[] $VALUES = { ACTIVE, OVERDUE, RETURNED, LOST };
    private LoanStatus(String name, int ordinal) { super(name, ordinal); }
    public static LoanStatus[] values()          { return $VALUES.clone(); }
    public static LoanStatus valueOf(String name) { ... }
}
Definition · enum class (JLS §8.9)

Every enum implicitly extends java.lang.Enum<E>, so it can't extend anything else, though it can implement interfaces. Its constants are public static final instances created once, during class initialization (L34). Its constructors are implicitly private; nothing outside can create another instance, not even reflection, which fails with Cannot reflectively create enum objects. Enum supplies name(), ordinal(), compareTo (declaration order) and final, identity-based equals/hashCode, so comparing constants with == is correct and null-safe.

  • values() returns a new copy of the constants array each call, in declaration order. Cache it if you call it in a hot loop.
  • valueOf("OVERDUE") looks a constant up by exact, case-sensitive name and throws IllegalArgumentException: No enum constant LoanStatus.overdue otherwise.
  • name() is the identifier as declared; toString() returns the same by default but may be overridden for display. Use name() for persistence.
  • ordinal() is the zero-based position. It changes when anyone reorders or inserts a constant, so never persist it or compute with it (Effective Java Item 35). If a constant needs a number, give it a field.

Fields, constructors, methods, and per-constant behaviour

enum MembershipTier {
    BASIC(3, 25), STUDENT(6, 10), PREMIUM(10, 0);   // constructor arguments

    private final int maxLoans;
    private final long lateFeePerDayCents;

    MembershipTier(int maxLoans, long lateFeePerDayCents) {    // implicitly private
        this.maxLoans = maxLoans;
        this.lateFeePerDayCents = lateFeePerDayCents;
    }
    int maxLoans()              { return maxLoans; }
    long lateFee(int daysLate)  { return daysLate * lateFeePerDayCents; }
}

enum FineRule {                                     // constant-specific bodies
    FLAT    { long fine(int days) { return days > 0 ? 100 : 0; } },
    PER_DAY { long fine(int days) { return 25L * days; } },
    CAPPED  { long fine(int days) { return Math.min(25L * days, 1_000); } };

    abstract long fine(int daysLate);               // every constant must implement
}

Data that differs per constant goes in fields; behaviour that differs goes in constant-specific method bodies: declare an abstract method on the enum and implement it in a body after each constant. The compiler forces every constant, including ones added next year, to provide it, which an if/switch elsewhere in the code base can't guarantee. Each body compiles to an anonymous subclass (FineRule$1), so FineRule.FLAT.getClass() is not FineRule.class; use getDeclaringClass() when you need the enum type.

Enums implementing interfaces: the strategy pattern

record Book(String title, int year) {}

enum BookOrder implements Comparator<Book> {
    BY_TITLE {
        public int compare(Book a, Book b) { return a.title().compareTo(b.title()); }
    },
    BY_YEAR {
        public int compare(Book a, Book b) { return Integer.compare(a.year(), b.year()); }
    }
}

// GET /books?sort=BY_YEAR
static List<Book> sorted(List<Book> books, String sortParam) {
    var copy = new ArrayList<>(books);
    copy.sort(BookOrder.valueOf(sortParam));        // the strategy, chosen by name
    return copy;
}

Each constant is a Comparator, so it can be passed anywhere one is expected, and the set of allowed orderings is closed and discoverable through values(). This is Effective Java Item 34's strategy enum. Because an interface is the real contract, other code can add its own implementations alongside the enum's: Item 38's "emulate extensible enums with interfaces".

switch over enums

static String describe(LoanStatus s) {
    return switch (s) {                 // exhaustive: no default branch
        case ACTIVE   -> "on loan";
        case OVERDUE  -> "late, fines accruing";
        case RETURNED -> "back on the shelf";
        case LOST     -> "replacement fee due";
    };
}

A switch expression must cover every possible value (L21). Over an enum, listing all constants is enough, and leaving out default is a feature: add CLAIMED_RETURNED and this method stops compiling (the switch expression does not cover all possible input values) until someone decides what it means. A default branch would have hidden the new case. If an old compiled copy of describe meets the new constant at run time, it throws MatchException.

EnumSet and EnumMap

EnumSet<LoanStatus> open = EnumSet.of(LoanStatus.ACTIVE, LoanStatus.OVERDUE);
EnumSet<LoanStatus> closed = EnumSet.complementOf(open);   // RETURNED, LOST

Map<LoanStatus, Integer> counts = new EnumMap<>(LoanStatus.class);
for (LoanStatus s : List.of(LoanStatus.ACTIVE, LoanStatus.LOST, LoanStatus.ACTIVE))
    counts.put(s, counts.getOrDefault(s, 0) + 1);
System.out.println(open + " " + closed + " " + counts);
// [ACTIVE, OVERDUE] [RETURNED, LOST] {ACTIVE=2, LOST=1}

With up to 64 constants an EnumSet is a single long bit vector, so add, contains and set operations are a few bit instructions. It replaces "bit field" int flags with type safety (Item 36). EnumMap replaces arrays indexed by ordinal() (Item 37). Use both whenever the key or element type is an enum.

Enum singletons, and a JPA preview

enum Registry { INSTANCE; ... } is the singleton Effective Java Item 3 recommends: the JVM guarantees one instance, serialization preserves it (enums serialize by name), and reflection can't create a second. The limitations are that it can't extend a class and it is still global state (L33).

In JPA (L226) an enum field is mapped with @Enumerated, and the default is EnumType.ORDINAL: the column stores 0, 1, 2. Insert a constant in the middle of the enum and every existing row silently changes meaning. Always write @Enumerated(EnumType.STRING), and treat constant names as part of your schema from then on.

What breaks in production
  • Persisted ordinals, through the JPA default or hand-written code: a harmless-looking reorder corrupts historical data with no error anywhere.
  • valueOf on user input. ?sort=year throws IllegalArgumentException, which surfaces as a 500. Look the name up defensively and answer 400 for unknown values.
  • Renaming a constant breaks stored STRING values, JSON contracts with clients, and message payloads in queues. A display name belongs in a field, not in the constant's identifier.
  • New constants versus old clients. A service that adds LOST can break consumers that deserialize into an enum without it; JSON libraries reject unknown values by default.

Practice · 45 min

  1. Write LoanStatus and describe. Add CLAIMED_RETURNED and read the compiler error; then add a default branch instead and note what you lost.
