  2. Implement MembershipTier and FineRule. Print FineRule.FLAT.getClass() and getDeclaringClass().
  3. Write sorted(books, sortParam) so that an unknown or lower-case parameter falls back to BY_TITLE instead of throwing.
  4. Count loans per status with an EnumMap and with a HashMap; compare the iteration order.
  5. Try to call an enum's constructor reflectively and read the exception.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 3, 34–38 · JLS §8.9 (enum classes), §15.28 (switch expressions) · Javadoc: java.lang.Enum, java.util.EnumSet, java.util.EnumMap · Jakarta Persistence specification, @Enumerated

L47 · DAY 47

Records: Concise Immutable Data Carriers

40m video45m practice

A record is a class whose state is its declaration: list the components, and the compiler writes the fields, constructor, accessors, equals, hashCode and toString, correctly and consistently. In return you give up inheritance and hidden state, which is exactly the trade a value object wants.

What one line generates

record Money(long cents, String currency) {}
Definition · record class (JLS §8.10)

A record class is implicitly final and extends java.lang.Record, so it can't extend another class or be extended. For each component it has a private final field and a public accessor. It may declare constructors, methods, static fields and static methods, and nested types, and it may implement interfaces; it may not declare other instance fields or instance initializers. Accessors, equals, hashCode and toString can be written explicitly to replace the generated ones.

Compact constructors: validate and normalise

public record Money(long cents, String currency) {
    public static final Money ZERO_EUR = new Money(0, "EUR");   // statics allowed

    public Money {                                  // compact canonical constructor
        Objects.requireNonNull(currency, "currency");
        currency = currency.toUpperCase(Locale.ROOT);   // reassign the parameter
        if (cents < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("negative: " + cents);
    }                                       // fields assigned here, implicitly

    public Money(long cents) { this(cents, "EUR"); }    // extra constructors delegate

    public static Money parse(String s) {           // static factory: "12.50 eur"
        String[] p = s.strip().split("\\s+");
        long cents = new BigDecimal(p[0]).movePointRight(2).longValueExact();
        return new Money(cents, p[1]);
    }

    public Money plus(Money o) {                    // "changing" returns a new value
        if (!currency.equals(o.currency))
            throw new IllegalArgumentException(currency + " vs " + o.currency);
        return new Money(Math.addExact(cents, o.cents), currency);
    }
}
// Money.parse("12.50 eur").plus(new Money(250))  ->  Money[cents=1500, currency=EUR]

A compact constructor omits the parameter list; its parameters are the components. It runs your checks first and then assigns every field from the (possibly reassigned) parameters. Writing this.cents = 0 inside it is a compile error, since the field assignment is the compiler's job. Every other constructor must begin with this(...), so the canonical constructor, and therefore your validation, runs for every instance ever created. That includes instances created by Java deserialization, which for records always goes through the canonical constructor.

Interfaces, nesting, and local records

Records implement interfaces like any class: record Book(String title, int year) implements Comparable<Book> (L45). Nested records are implicitly static, so they never capture an enclosing instance. Local records, declared inside a method, are also implicitly static: they can't use the method's local variables, but they are perfect as named tuples for intermediate results:

static List<String> topBorrowers(Map<String, Integer> loansByMember) {
    record Entry(String member, int loans) {}       // local, named, typed tuple
    var entries = new ArrayList<Entry>();
    for (var e : loansByMember.entrySet())
        entries.add(new Entry(e.getKey(), e.getValue()));
    entries.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(Entry::loans).reversed());
    var top = new ArrayList<String>();
    for (Entry e : entries.subList(0, Math.min(3, entries.size())))
        top.add(e.member());
    return top;
}

Records as DTOs, value objects and API responses

Records fit everywhere data crosses a boundary: request and response bodies (Jackson binds JSON through the canonical constructor since 2.12, and Spring MVC accepts records as @RequestBody types), Spring Boot @ConfigurationProperties, query projections, event payloads, and domain value objects such as Money, Isbn or DateRange, whose identity is their value. They do not fit JPA entities, which need a no-argument constructor, mutable state and subclassing for proxies (L35); keep entities as classes and map them to record DTOs at the edge.

Record patterns in instanceof and switch

record Book(String isbn, String title) {}
record Member(long id, String name) {}
record Loan(Book book, Member member, LocalDate due) {}

static String reminder(Object event, LocalDate today) {
    return switch (event) {
        case Loan(Book(var isbn, var title), Member m, LocalDate due)
                when due.isBefore(today) -> m.name() + ": '" + title + "' is overdue";
        case Loan(Book b, Member m, LocalDate due) -> m.name() + ": due " + due;
        case Money(long cents, String cur) -> "fine of " + cents + " " + cur;
        default -> "nothing to send";
    };
}
// if (event instanceof Money(long c, String cur) && c > 0) { ... }

A record pattern (Java 21, JEP 440) tests the type and deconstructs the components in one step, and patterns nest: Loan(Book(var isbn, var title), ...) reaches two levels down. Patterns match the components positionally, through the accessors. Combined with when guards and the sealed hierarchies of L48, this replaces most visitor-pattern code (L119 has the full syntax).

Limitations

  • No inheritance. A record can't extend a class, and nothing can extend a record. Share behaviour through interfaces with default methods (L40).
  • Shallow immutability. The fields are final, but a List component can still be mutated through the accessor. Copy in the compact constructor: books = List.copyOf(books);.
  • Arrays compare by identity. An int[] component makes equals use the array's identity, so two records with equal contents are unequal. Prefer List, or override equals, hashCode and toString.
  • Everything is public. Every component has a public accessor and appears in toString. There is no private state.
What breaks in production
  • Component order is an API. Reordering record Range(int from, int to) to (int to, int from) still compiles everywhere: every new Range(a, b) and every positional pattern Range(var x, var y) silently swaps meaning.
  • Adding a component changes the canonical constructor's signature, breaking every caller, every pattern, and possibly old JSON payloads without the field. Add a delegating constructor for the old shape while callers migrate.
  • Secrets in toString. The generated toString prints every component, so a token in a record goes straight into logs (L44).

Practice · 45 min

  1. Write Money with tests for normalisation ("eur" to "EUR"), validation, parse and plus. Try this.cents = 0 in the compact constructor and read the error.
  2. Write record Shelf(String name, List<String> books), show that shelf.books().add(...) mutates it, then fix it with List.copyOf.
  3. Give a record an int[] component and show two records with equal contents being unequal. Fix it two ways.
  4. Implement reminder; add a pattern that handles a returned loan. Then swap two String components in Book and see what still compiles.
  5. Convert a JavaBean DTO from L32 into a record and compare the line counts and the behaviour of equals.

Read JLS §8.10 (record classes), §14.30.1 (record patterns) · JEP 395, “Records” · JEP 440, “Record Patterns” · Javadoc: java.lang.Record · Brian Goetz, “Data Classes and Sealed Types for Java” (OpenJDK design note, 2019)

L48 · DAY 48

Sealed Classes & Interfaces

40m video40m practice

A sealed type lists every one of its direct subtypes. That closed list lets the compiler prove a switch handles every case, so you can model "a payment result is exactly one of these three things, each with its own data" and have every missing case reported as a compile error rather than a production incident.

sealed, permits, and the rule for subtypes

sealed interface PaymentResult permits Approved, Declined, Pending {}

record Approved(String authCode) implements PaymentResult {}  // records: final
record Declined(String reason) implements PaymentResult {}
non-sealed class Pending implements PaymentResult {}          // reopened below
class PendingReview extends Pending {}                        // allowed
Definition · sealed class or interface (JEP 409, Java 17)

A sealed class or interface may be directly extended or implemented only by the types named in its permits clause. Each permitted subtype must directly extend it and must declare exactly one of:

  • final: no further subtypes (records and enums are implicitly final);
  • sealed: closed again, with its own permits list;
  • non-sealed: open again; anything may extend it.

Forget the modifier and javac says sealed, non-sealed or final modifiers expected; implement the interface from an unlisted class and it says class is not allowed to extend sealed class: PaymentResult (as it is not listed in its 'permits' clause).

Where the subtypes may live. In a named module, permitted subtypes must be in the same module; in the unnamed module (an ordinary classpath application), in the same package. If they are all declared in the same source file, for example as nested types, the permits clause may be omitted and the compiler infers it. At run time, Class.isSealed() and getPermittedSubclasses() expose the list.

Why sealing enables exhaustive switch

static String receipt(PaymentResult r) {
    return switch (r) {                         // no default: the compiler checks
        case Approved a -> "paid, code " + a.authCode();
        case Declined d -> "declined: " + d.reason();
        case Pending p  -> "awaiting confirmation";
    };
}

Over a non-sealed interface, a switch expression needs a default, because tomorrow someone can write a fourth implementation. Over a sealed one, three cases provably cover everything, so no default is needed, and you should not write one. Add record Refunded(...) to the permits list and every such switch in the code base stops compiling until it handles the new case (JLS §14.11.1.1). Code compiled against the old hierarchy that meets a Refunded at run time throws MatchException, as with enums (L46).

Algebraic data types: sealed interfaces plus records

Definition · algebraic data type

A type built from two operations. A product type holds several values at once ("an Approved has an authCode and a timestamp"), which is a record. A sum type is exactly one of several alternatives ("a result is Approved or Declined or Pending"), which is a sealed interface. Records give the products, sealing gives the sums, and record patterns (L47) take them apart.

The classic example is a Result<T> that makes failure part of the return type instead of an exception the caller may forget to catch:

sealed interface Result<T> {                   // permits inferred: same file
    record Ok<T>(T value) implements Result<T> {}
    record Err<T>(String error) implements Result<T> {}

    static <T> Result<T> ok(T value)      { return new Ok<>(value); }
    static <T> Result<T> err(String error) { return new Err<>(error); }
}

class Copies {
    static Result<Integer> parse(String s) {
        try {
            int n = Integer.parseInt(s.strip());
            return n > 0 ? Result.ok(n) : Result.err("must be positive: " + n);
        } catch (NumberFormatException e) {
            return Result.err("not a number: '" + s + "'");
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        for (String input : List.of("3", "-1", "lots")) {
            String msg = switch (parse(input)) {
                case Result.Ok<Integer>(var n)  -> "adding " + n + " copies";
                case Result.Err<Integer>(var e) -> "rejected, " + e;
            };
            System.out.println(msg);
        }
    }
}

Use it where failure is an expected outcome the caller must handle (validation, parsing, a payment provider's answer). Unexpected failures, such as a lost database connection, remain exceptions (L53).

Modelling a domain state machine

sealed interface LoanState {
    record Requested(LocalDate on) implements LoanState {}
    record Active(LocalDate due) implements LoanState {}
    record Overdue(LocalDate due, long fineCents) implements LoanState {}
    record Returned(LocalDate on, long finePaidCents) implements LoanState {}
}

static LoanState nextDay(LoanState s, LocalDate today) {
    return switch (s) {
        case LoanState.Active a when today.isAfter(a.due())
                -> new LoanState.Overdue(a.due(), 25);
        case LoanState.Overdue o
                -> new LoanState.Overdue(o.due(), o.fineCents() + 25);
        case LoanState.Requested r -> r;
        case LoanState.Active a    -> a;
        case LoanState.Returned r  -> r;
    };
}

Compare the usual alternative: one Loan class with a status enum plus nullable due, fineCents and returnedOn fields, where nothing stops "returned, with no return date" or "active, with a fine". Here each state carries exactly the data that is meaningful in it, so those illegal combinations can't even be constructed. "Make illegal states unrepresentable" is the design goal; sealed types are how Java expresses it.

Sealed hierarchies vs enums vs open polymorphism

EnumSealed + recordsOpen polymorphism
Alternativesfixed singletonsfixed types, many instancesopen-ended
Data per alternativesame fields for alldifferent fields per typeanything
Exhaustive switchyesyesno, needs default
Adding an alternativeedit the enum; compiler flags switchesadd to permits; compiler flags switchesone new class, nothing else
Adding an operationone switch or methodone switchedit every class
Use forstatuses, categoriesresults, events, states with dataplug-ins, providers, strategies

The last two columns are the two sides of the "expression problem": open polymorphism (L37) makes new types cheap, sealed types make new operations cheap. Choose by which one you expect to change more often.

What breaks in production
  • A default branch over a sealed type silently swallows the variant someone adds next quarter. Leave it out and let the compiler find every switch.
  • Sealing a type shared across services or teams. Adding a permitted subtype is a breaking change for every consumer that switches over it; consumers deployed with the old jar fail at run time with MatchException. Seal freely inside a module, deliberately across boundaries.
  • JSON polymorphism. A sealed hierarchy serialised to JSON needs an explicit discriminator (a "type" field) to be deserialised back into the right record; configure it, or the client receives fields it can't map.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Write PaymentResult and receipt. Remove non-sealed from Pending and read the error; add Refunded to permits and fix the switch the compiler points to.
  2. Run Copies.main, then add an Err case for an empty string and a third variant Warn<T>(T value, String warning), letting the compiler guide every change.
  3. Implement LoanState transitions activate, nextDay and returnBook, with a test for each, and try to write "active with a fine".
  4. Print PaymentResult.class.getPermittedSubclasses().
  5. For HTTP methods, payment-provider integrations and CSV parse outcomes, pick enum, sealed or open polymorphism and justify each in one line.

Read JEP 409, “Sealed Classes” · JEP 441, “Pattern Matching for switch” · JLS §8.1.1.2 (sealed, non-sealed and final classes), §9.1.1.4 (sealed interfaces), §14.11.1.1 (exhaustive switch blocks) · Javadoc: Class.getPermittedSubclasses · Brian Goetz, “Data Classes and Sealed Types for Java” (2019)

L49 · DAY 49

Nested Classes: static, Inner, Local & Anonymous

45m video45m practice

A class declared inside another is either static, an ordinary class that happens to live in another's namespace, or not, in which case every instance carries a hidden reference to an enclosing object. Choosing static unless you need that reference is the one rule that prevents most nested-class bugs.

The four kinds

KindDeclaredEnclosing instance?Typical use
Static nestedclass body, with staticnobuilders, helper types, nested records and enums
Innerclass body, no staticyes, alwaysviews and iterators over the outer object
Localinside a method or blockyes, in instance methodsa named helper used in one method
Anonymousin an expression, new T() { ... }yes, in instance methodsone-off implementations

All four compile to separate class files: Outer$Nested, Outer$Inner, Outer$1Local, and Outer$1, Outer$2 for anonymous classes in order of appearance. (Lambdas produce no class file at all; their classes are spun up at run time.) Nested classes and their outer class can read each other's private members; since Java 11 the JVM supports this directly as nestmates (JEP 181).

Static nested classes, and the builder end to end

A static nested class has no link to any instance of the outer class; it is a top-level class with a qualified name and private access to its host. The canonical use is the builder that L31 previewed (Effective Java Item 2):

public final class SearchQuery {
    private final String text;
    private final int pageSize, page;
    private final String sortBy;
    private final boolean availableOnly;

    private SearchQuery(Builder b) {             // only the builder may call this
        text = b.text; pageSize = b.pageSize; page = b.page;
        sortBy = b.sortBy; availableOnly = b.availableOnly;
    }
    public static Builder builder(String text) { return new Builder(text); }

    public static final class Builder {          // static: no SearchQuery needed
        private final String text;               // required
        private int pageSize = 20, page = 0;     // optional, with defaults
        private String sortBy = "relevance";
        private boolean availableOnly;

        private Builder(String text) { this.text = Objects.requireNonNull(text); }
        public Builder pageSize(int n)  { pageSize = n; return this; }
        public Builder page(int n)      { page = n; return this; }
        public Builder sortBy(String s) { sortBy = Objects.requireNonNull(s);
                                          return this; }
        public Builder availableOnly()  { availableOnly = true; return this; }

        public SearchQuery build() {             // validate the whole combination
            if (pageSize < 1 || pageSize > 100)
                throw new IllegalArgumentException("pageSize " + pageSize);
            if (page < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("page " + page);
            return new SearchQuery(this);
        }
    }

    @Override public String toString() {
        return "SearchQuery[" + text + ", size=" + pageSize + ", page=" + page
             + ", sort=" + sortBy + ", available=" + availableOnly + "]";
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var q = SearchQuery.builder("dune").pageSize(50).availableOnly().build();
        System.out.println(q);
    }   // SearchQuery[dune, size=50, page=0, sort=relevance, available=true]
}

Every step is named, optional parameters keep their defaults, cross-field rules are checked once in build(), and the product is immutable. The builder must be static: it exists before any SearchQuery does, so it can't belong to one.

Inner classes, Outer.this, and outer.new Inner()

class Library {
    private final String name;
    private final List<String> books = new ArrayList<>();
    Library(String name) { this.name = name; }

    class Shelf {                                  // inner: tied to one Library
        private final String name;                 // shadows Library.name
        Shelf(String name) { this.name = name; }

        String describe() {                        // reads the outer object's state
            return name + " in " + Library.this.name + ", " + books.size() + " books";
        }
    }
    Shelf shelf(String label) { return new Shelf(label); }   // inside: plain new
}

// from outside, an inner instance needs an outer one:
// Library lib = new Library("Central");
// Library.Shelf a1 = lib.new Shelf("A1");   // or lib.shelf("A1")
Definition · enclosing instance (JLS §8.1.3)

Every instance of an inner class is bound to an instance of its outer class. The compiler stores it in a hidden final field (this$0 in the bytecode) and passes it to every inner constructor. Unqualified names resolve to the inner class first, so name is the shelf's; Library.this names the outer object explicitly. From outside, lib.new Shelf("A1") makes the binding visible: no Library, no Shelf.

JDK collections use inner classes for exactly this case: an ArrayList's iterator is an inner class reading its list's array.

The memory-leak risk

The danger is any inner or anonymous object that outlives its outer object: a listener on a long-lived event bus, a task on a scheduled executor, an entry in a cache. Each keeps its whole enclosing object reachable, and serializing it serializes the outer object too. Effective Java Item 24: if a member class does not need its enclosing instance, declare it static.

Local and anonymous classes, and captured variables

static List<Runnable> reminders(List<String> members, int daysLeft) {
    String suffix = daysLeft == 1 ? " day" : " days";   // effectively final
    var tasks = new ArrayList<Runnable>();
    for (String m : members) {
        tasks.add(new Runnable() {                         // anonymous: Outer$1
            @Override public void run() {
                System.out.println(m + ": due in " + daysLeft + suffix);
            }
        });
    }
    // suffix = "d";   // compile error: local variables referenced from an inner
    //                 // class must be final or effectively final
    return tasks;
}

A local class is a named class declared inside a block, visible only there; an anonymous class declares and instantiates an unnamed subclass or implementation in one expression. Neither can declare a constructor (an anonymous class can pass arguments to its superclass's constructor and use an instance initializer, L34). Both may use the enclosing method's local variables and parameters, but only if they are effectively final, never reassigned after initialization. The reason is mechanical: the object copies each captured value into a synthetic field when it is created and may run long after the method has returned and its stack frame is gone. If the variable could still change, the copy and the variable would silently disagree, so Java forbids the change.

When a lambda replaces an anonymous class, and when it can't

When the target is a functional interface (L41), a lambda is shorter and clearer, and Effective Java Item 42 says to prefer it: tasks.add(() -> System.out.println(m + ": due in " + daysLeft + suffix)). Lambdas cover the same capture rules and, unlike anonymous classes declared in instance methods, don't capture this unless they use it. Anonymous classes remain necessary when:

  • The target is an abstract class, such as java.util.TimerTask, or Jackson's new TypeReference<List<Book>>() {}, which subclasses to capture a generic type that erasure would otherwise lose (L77).
  • The interface has more than one abstract method.
  • The object needs state (its own fields) or must refer to itself: inside a lambda this means the enclosing instance, so a lambda can't use this to, say, unregister itself as a listener.
What breaks in production
  • Leaks through listeners and tasks. An anonymous Runnable scheduled every minute from inside a request-scoped or per-tenant object keeps that object, and everything it references, alive forever.
  • Unstable class names. Outer$3 becomes Outer$4 when someone adds an anonymous class above it, which breaks serialized data and confuses heap dumps and logs. Name classes that matter.
  • Builders without validation. A build() that just copies fields lets half-configured objects into the system. Validate there, once, for every combination.

Practice · 45 min

  1. Implement SearchQuery and its builder. Test the defaults, each validation rule, and that two builds from one builder produce independent objects.
  2. Create a Shelf from outside with lib.new Shelf("A1"). Remove the Library. qualifier from describe and explain the new output.
  3. Compile a class with two anonymous classes, a local class and a lambda, and list the generated .class files.
  4. Store 1,000 inner Shelfs, each from a Library holding 10,000 strings, in a static list; print used heap after System.gc(). Make Shelf static, pass in only what it needs, and repeat.
  5. Convert the anonymous classes from L41 and L45 to lambdas. Find one in your own code that can't be converted and say why.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 2, 24, 42 · JLS §8.1.3 (inner classes and enclosing instances), §14.3 (local classes), §15.9.5 (anonymous classes), §15.8.4 (qualified this) · JEP 181, “Nest-Based Access Control”

L50 · DAY 50

Packages, Imports & Access Modifiers

40m video40m practice

Packages are Java's unit of encapsulation above the class: a namespace, a directory, and an access boundary. Used well, with package-private as the default and a small public surface per feature, they let a large backend code base keep its internals internal without any framework.

Package declarations and directory structure

The first statement of a source file, package com.acme.library.catalog;, puts every type in the file into that package. The fully qualified name of Book is then com.acme.library.catalog.Book, and by convention (enforced by Maven, Gradle and the compiler's source lookup) the file lives in a matching directory under a source root. Compiled classes land in the same structure under the output directory, and the classpath points at the root of that structure, never at a package directory (L7).

src/main/java/
└── com/acme/library/
    ├── LibraryApp.java            package com.acme.library;
    ├── catalog/
    │   ├── Book.java              package com.acme.library.catalog;
    │   └── CatalogService.java
    └── loans/
        └── LoanService.java       package com.acme.library.loans;

$ javac -d out $(find src/main/java -name "*.java")
$ java -cp out com.acme.library.LibraryApp

Reverse-domain naming makes names globally unique without a registry: a company that owns acme.com uses com.acme.*. Package names are all lower case; replace hyphens with underscores or drop them (com.acme.order_service or com.acme.orderservice), and never start one with java or javax. Classes with no package declaration live in the unnamed package, fine for a JShell experiment and wrong for anything else, because no named package can import from it.

import, wildcards, static imports and fully qualified names

package com.acme.library.catalog;

public final class CatalogService {                 // the feature's public API
    private final Map<String, Book> books = new HashMap<>();

    public void add(Book b) { books.put(IsbnRules.normalise(b.isbn()), b); }
    public Optional<Book> find(String isbn) {
        return Optional.ofNullable(books.get(IsbnRules.normalise(isbn)));
    }
    public record Book(String isbn, String title) {}
}

final class IsbnRules {                             // package-private: invisible
    static String normalise(String isbn) {          // outside ...catalog
        return isbn.replace("-", "").strip();
    }
}
package com.acme.library.loans;

import com.acme.library.catalog.CatalogService;      // single-type import
import com.acme.library.catalog.CatalogService.Book; // nested type, imported
import java.time.*;                                  // on-demand (wildcard)
import static java.util.Objects.requireNonNull;      // static import (L33)

public final class LoanService {
    private final CatalogService catalog;
    private final java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicLong ids = // fully qualified:
            new java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicLong();       // no import needed
    private final Clock clock;

    public LoanService(CatalogService catalog, Clock clock) {
        this.catalog = requireNonNull(catalog);
        this.clock = requireNonNull(clock);
    }
    public String lend(String isbn) {
        Book b = catalog.find(isbn).orElseThrow();
        // IsbnRules.normalise(isbn);  error: IsbnRules is not public in
        //                             com.acme.library.catalog
        return "loan " + ids.incrementAndGet() + ": " + b.title()
             + " due " + LocalDate.now(clock).plusWeeks(3);
    }
}

An import is purely a compile-time shorthand: it loads nothing, costs nothing at run time, and doesn't exist in the bytecode, where every name is fully qualified. java.lang is imported implicitly. A wildcard imports the public types of one package, not its subpackages: import java.util.* does not bring in java.util.concurrent.

Name collisions are resolved by precedence. A single-type import beats an on-demand one, so import java.util.*; plus import java.sql.Date; makes Date mean java.sql.Date. Two wildcards that both supply Date are fine until you use the name, at which point javac reports reference to Date is ambiguous. When you genuinely need both, import one and write the other fully qualified. This is the main practical argument for single-type imports, which is also what IDEs generate by default.

The four access levels

ModifierSame classSame packageSubclass, other packageEverywhere
privateyesnonono
(none) package-privateyesyesnono
protectedyesyesyes, with the rule belowno
publicyesyesyesyes (if the module exports the package)

protected across packages: the subtle rule

package com.acme.base;

public class Account {
    protected long balance;
}
package com.acme.savings;

import com.acme.base.Account;

public class SavingsAccount extends Account {
    void transfer(SavingsAccount other, Account any) {
        this.balance -= 100;      // OK: our own inherited field
        other.balance += 100;     // OK: through a SavingsAccount reference
        // any.balance += 100;    // error: balance has protected access in Account
    }
}
Rule · protected access (JLS §6.6.2.1)

Outside the declaring package, code in a subclass S may access a protected instance member only through references of type S or a subtype of S. The reasoning: protected access exists so that S can implement itself, not so that it can reach into an arbitrary Account, which might be another team's CheckingAccount. Inside com.acme.base, protected members are simply package-accessible.

Package-by-layer vs package-by-feature

by layer                              by feature
com.acme.library.controller           com.acme.library.catalog
    BookController, LoanController        BookController, CatalogService,
com.acme.library.service                  BookRepository, Book
    CatalogService, LoanService       com.acme.library.loans
com.acme.library.repository               LoanController, LoanService,
    BookRepository, LoanRepository        LoanRepository, Loan
com.acme.library.model                com.acme.library.members
    Book, Loan, Member                    ...

Package-by-layer looks tidy, but every class must be public because each call crosses a package, so package-private does nothing and any controller can reach any repository. One feature change touches four packages. Package-by-feature keeps everything a feature needs in one package, so most classes can be package-private and only the feature's entry points are public. Features then become natural candidates for modules or separate services later.

An encapsulation strategy that works for a real backend: package by feature; make package-private the default (change your IDE's class template); make public only what another feature must call, typically a service and its DTOs; keep entities, repositories and helpers package-private where your frameworks allow it; and put tests in the same package under src/test/java, where they can see package-private members without widening anything. Tools such as ArchUnit (Phase 13) turn rules like "loans may not touch catalog internals" into failing tests.

A teaser: module-info.java

module com.acme.library {
    requires java.sql;
    exports com.acme.library.catalog;     // only exported packages are visible
}                                         // outside the module, public or not

Since Java 9, a module (L118) can hide whole packages: a public class in a package the module doesn't export is inaccessible to other modules, and reflective access into it is denied unless explicitly opened. It is the strong encapsulation that packages alone can't give you, because any jar on the classpath can otherwise call any public class.

What breaks in production
  • Everything public. In a package-by-layer code base, "internal" helpers are called from other features within months, and the dependency graph becomes a ball of mud that is hard to split into services.
  • Split packages. Two jars that both contain classes in com.acme.util work on the classpath, with whichever jar comes first winning for duplicate class names, and fail outright as modules. Keep each package in exactly one artifact.
  • Wildcard imports and upgrades. A library upgrade adds a class named like one of yours to a package you import with *, and previously compiling code becomes ambiguous.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Lay out catalog and loans as above, compile with javac -d out, and run from the command line. Then try to call IsbnRules from loans and read the error.
  2. Import both java.util.* and java.sql.*, use Date, read the ambiguity error, and fix it two different ways.
  3. Reproduce the protected-access rule with Account and SavingsAccount in different packages. Move SavingsAccount into com.acme.base and explain why the error disappears.
  4. Take a small package-by-layer project (or the library you have been building) and reorganise it by feature. Count how many classes can become package-private.

Read JLS §7.4 (package declarations), §7.5 (import declarations), §6.6 (access control), §6.6.2.1 (protected access) · Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Item 15 · dev.java tutorial: “Packages”

L51 · DAY 51

The Exception Hierarchy: Checked vs Unchecked

45m video40m practice

Everything that can be thrown descends from Throwable, and where a class sits in that tree decides two things: whether the compiler forces callers to deal with it, and whether anyone should try to recover from it at all. Reading the resulting stack traces quickly is one of the most valuable debugging skills in backend work.

Throwable, Error, Exception, RuntimeException

Definition · the three branches (JLS §11.1.1)
  • Error: a serious problem in the JVM or its environment that a reasonable application should not try to catch, such as running out of memory, overflowing the stack, or failing to load a class. Unchecked.
  • Exception (except the runtime branch): a condition a well-written program may anticipate and recover from, such as a missing file, a refused connection, or an interrupted wait. Checked: the compiler enforces handling.
  • RuntimeException: usually a programming error, a broken precondition or a bug, such as a null where an object was required, a bad argument, or an index out of range. Unchecked.

Checked vs unchecked: compile-time enforcement

For a checked exception, the compiler requires every method that can throw it either to catch it or to declare it with throws (JLS §11.2). Calling Files.readString(path) without either is unreported exception IOException; must be caught or declared to be thrown. For unchecked exceptions (RuntimeException, Error and their subclasses), the compiler says nothing; they propagate silently until something catches them or the thread dies.

The design debate. Effective Java Item 70 gives the classic rule: use checked exceptions for conditions the caller can reasonably recover from, and runtime exceptions for programming errors. Item 71 adds: avoid checked exceptions the caller can't actually do anything about. The critics' points are real: checked exceptions leak implementation details into signatures (a repository that throws SQLException ties every caller to JDBC), they don't compose with lambdas and streams because Function.apply declares nothing, and they encourage empty catch blocks written only to satisfy the compiler. Modern Java backends lean unchecked: Spring translates SQLException into its unchecked DataAccessException hierarchy, and newer JDK APIs such as java.time throw unchecked exceptions. Kotlin and C# have no checked exceptions at all. L53 turns this into a decision guide for your own exceptions.

Error types you should never catch

After an OutOfMemoryError, any thread may have died halfway through updating shared state, and even logging may fail because it needs memory. StackOverflowError almost always means unbounded recursion (L28), for instance two objects whose toString methods call each other (L44). Catching either and carrying on leaves a process in an unknown state. The production stance is to let the JVM die and have the platform restart it: run with -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError to capture evidence and -XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError so a container doesn't limp on. This is also why catch (Throwable t) in application code is almost always wrong: it catches these too.

Reading a stack trace, top to bottom

import java.util.Map;

public class Greeter {
    record Member(String name) {}
    static final Map<String, Member> MEMBERS = Map.of("m1", new Member("Ada"));

    static String greet(String id) {
        Member m = MEMBERS.get(id);
        return "Hello " + m.name();                  // NPE when id is unknown
    }

    static String handle(String id) {
        try {
            return greet(id);
        } catch (NullPointerException e) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("cannot greet member " + id, e);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println(handle("m1"));
        System.out.println(handle("m2"));
    }
}
Hello Ada
Exception in thread "main" java.lang.IllegalStateException: cannot greet member m2
	at Greeter.handle(Greeter.java:16)
	at Greeter.main(Greeter.java:22)
Caused by: java.lang.NullPointerException: Cannot invoke "Greeter$Member.name()" because "m" is null
	at Greeter.greet(Greeter.java:9)
	at Greeter.handle(Greeter.java:14)
	... 1 more
Method · reading a trace
  1. First line: the thread, the exception class, and its message. This is the outermost exception, the last one thrown.
  2. The at lines: the call stack when it was created, innermost first. The top frame is where it was thrown (Greeter.java:16); each line below is the caller of the one above.
  3. Caused by: the exception that was wrapped, with its own stack. Chains can be several levels deep; the last Caused by: is the root cause, and usually the one to read first.
  4. ... 1 more: the remaining frames are identical to the enclosing trace's last frames, so they are omitted.
  5. Skim past framework frames (org.springframework…, jdk.internal…) to the first frame in your package: that is usually the line to open.

The message Cannot invoke "Greeter$Member.name()" because "m" is null comes from helpful NullPointerExceptions (JEP 358, on by default since Java 15). It names the null expression exactly; local variable names appear only if the class was compiled with debug information (javac -g, which Maven and Gradle do by default), otherwise you see "<local1>".

The Throwable API

  • getMessage(): the detail message, possibly null. toString() is the class name plus the message.
  • getCause(): the wrapped exception, or null. Walk it in a loop to reach the root cause.
  • getStackTrace(): an array of StackTraceElements (class, method, file, line), innermost first, captured when the exception was constructed, not when it was thrown.
  • printStackTrace(): writes the trace to System.err. In a server that output bypasses the logging framework: no timestamp, level, thread or request ID, lines from concurrent requests interleave, and log shippers that expect structured JSON may not collect it. Use log.error("payment failed for order {}", orderId, e); passing the exception as the last argument makes SLF4J-style loggers print the full trace with every Caused by:.
What breaks in production
  • Logging only the message. log.error(e.getMessage()) drops the stack and the cause chain, and logs the literal null for exceptions without a message, the typical NPE before Java 14.
  • Wrapping without the cause. throw new IllegalStateException("failed") inside a catch discards the root cause; there is no Caused by: to read.
  • Catching too broadly. catch (Exception e) also catches every bug (NullPointerException, ClassCastException) and, if you aren't careful, InterruptedException, which breaks cancellation (Phase 5).
  • Stack traces in API responses. They reveal class names, library versions and internal structure to attackers. Return an error ID and log the trace under it.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Run Greeter. Using only the trace, find the file and line where the null was dereferenced. Then compile without -g and compare the NPE message.
  2. Remove , e from the IllegalStateException constructor and compare the trace. What can you no longer tell?
  3. For ten exceptions from the figure, write a method that walks getSuperclass() and prints "checked", "unchecked" or "error".
  4. Write rootCause(Throwable t) and summary(Throwable t), which produces a single line such as IllegalStateException: cannot greet member m2 <- NullPointerException: .... Guard against cycles in the cause chain.
  5. Trigger a StackOverflowError with unbounded recursion, count the depth reached, and try different -Xss values. Note in one sentence why this is an experiment, not a pattern.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 69–72 · JLS Ch. 11 (§11.1.1 kinds of exceptions, §11.2 compile-time checking) · JEP 358, “Helpful NullPointerExceptions” · Javadoc: java.lang.Throwable, StackTraceElement

L52 · DAY 52

try / catch / finally, Multi-Catch & try-with-resources

50m video50m practice

catch decides who handles a failure, finally decides what happens regardless, and try-with-resources makes the most common finally (closing something) automatic and correct. Most exception-handling bugs are an exception lost on the way out: swallowed by finally, masked by a failing close(), or caught by the wrong clause.

try/catch mechanics and catch ordering

When an exception is thrown, the JVM checks the current method's exception table for a handler that covers the throwing instruction and whose type matches (an instanceof test). The first matching catch, in source order, wins. If none matches, the frame is discarded and the search repeats in the caller, up the stack, until a handler is found or the thread terminates (L51). Because the first match wins, more specific types must come first; javac rejects the reverse order as unreachable code: exception NumberFormatException has already been caught.

static int parsePositive(String s) {
    try {
        int n = Integer.parseInt(s);
        if (n <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("not positive: " + n);
        return n;
    } catch (NumberFormatException e) {     // subclass of IllegalArgumentException:
        System.out.println("not a number: " + s);   // must come first
        return -1;
    } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {
        System.out.println(e.getMessage());
        return -1;
    } finally {
        System.out.println("finally for " + s);     // runs on every path,
    }                                               // even after a return
}
// parsePositive("5")   -> prints "finally for 5", returns 5
// parsePositive("-3")  -> "not positive: -3", "finally for -3", returns -1
// parsePositive("abc") -> "not a number: abc", "finally for abc", returns -1

finally: guaranteed, with exceptions

Definition · finally (JLS §14.20.2)

The finally block runs after the try block and any catch that ran, however they finished: normally, with return, break or continue, or by throwing. If the finally block itself completes abruptly (it returns or throws), that outcome replaces whatever the try or catch was doing.

When finally does not run: System.exit or Runtime.halt called inside the try (shutdown hooks run on exit, finally blocks don't); the process being killed (kill -9, the container's OOM killer, a node failure); a JVM crash; the try block never finishing because of an infinite loop or a deadlock; and daemon threads that are still inside a try when the JVM exits. So finally is for in-process cleanup, never for guaranteeing that something durable happens; that needs transactions or idempotent retries (Phase 16).

static int swallow() {
    try {
        throw new IllegalStateException("payment failed");
    } finally {
        return 42;              // the exception is discarded: the caller sees 42
    }
}

static int savedValue() {
    int x = 1;
    try {
        return x;               // the value 1 is evaluated and saved here
    } finally {
        x = 2;                  // too late: savedValue() returns 1
    }
}

The return-in-finally trap: swallow() returns 42 and the failure vanishes without a trace. A throw in finally, including one from a close() call there, does the same by replacing the original exception. javac warns with -Xlint:finally (finally clause cannot complete normally); treat that warning as an error.

Multi-catch, and nested try blocks

static Properties loadConfig(Path path) {
    try (Reader in = Files.newBufferedReader(path)) {
        var props = new Properties();
        props.load(in);
        int port = Integer.parseInt(props.getProperty("port", "8080"));
        if (port < 1 || port > 65_535)
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("port " + port);
        return props;
    } catch (IOException | IllegalArgumentException e) {   // one handler, two types
        // e = null;   error: multi-catch parameter e may not be assigned
        throw new IllegalStateException("config unusable: " + path, e);
    }
}

A multi-catch handles several unrelated exception types with one block. Its parameter is implicitly final, because its static type is the closest common supertype (here Exception), and assigning, say, a new IOException to it would be ill-typed for one of the alternatives. The alternatives may not be subclasses of one another (Alternatives in a multi-catch statement cannot be related by subclassing); the subclass would be redundant. Note that NumberFormatException from parseInt is caught too, as a subclass of IllegalArgumentException.

Nested try blocks let an inner block handle a local, recoverable failure (one malformed line in a file) while an outer block handles the failure of the whole operation (the file can't be opened). The inner finally runs before control reaches any outer catch. If you nest more than two levels, extract a method instead.

try-with-resources, close order and suppressed exceptions

Definition · try-with-resources (JLS §14.20.3)

Any object implementing AutoCloseable (method void close() throws Exception) may be declared in the parentheses after try. Closeable is the older I/O subinterface whose close() throws only IOException and must be idempotent. Each resource is closed automatically when the block exits, by any route, in reverse order of declaration, before any catch or finally clause of the same statement runs. If the body threw and a close() also throws, the close exception is attached to the body's exception with addSuppressed instead of replacing it. Since Java 9, an existing effectively final variable can be used directly: try (reader) { ... }.

public class Resources {
    record Res(String name, boolean failOnClose) implements AutoCloseable {
        Res { System.out.println("open " + name); }
        @Override public void close() {
            System.out.println("close " + name);
            if (failOnClose) throw new IllegalStateException("close failed: " + name);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try (var db = new Res("db", false); var file = new Res("file", true)) {
            throw new RuntimeException("work failed");
        } catch (RuntimeException e) {
            System.out.println("caught: " + e.getMessage());
            for (Throwable s : e.getSuppressed())
                System.out.println("  suppressed: " + s.getMessage());
        }
    }
}
open db
open file
close file
close db
caught: work failed
  suppressed: close failed: file

Rewriting legacy finally-close code

static String firstLineLegacy(String file) throws IOException {
    BufferedReader r = null;
    try {
        r = new BufferedReader(new FileReader(file));
        return r.readLine();
    } finally {
        if (r != null) r.close();      // if readLine AND close throw,
    }                                  // close's exception hides the real one
}

static String firstLine(Path file) throws IOException {   // Java 7+
    try (BufferedReader r = Files.newBufferedReader(file)) {
        return r.readLine();
    }
}

The legacy version is longer, needs a null check, and still loses the original exception when both readLine and close fail. With two or three resources (a JDBC connection, statement and result set) the hand-written version needs nested try/finally blocks and is almost always subtly wrong. Effective Java Item 9: prefer try-with-resources to try-finally, always.

What breaks in production
  • Leaked connections. A JDBC Connection not closed on an error path never returns to the pool; after enough errors every request waits for a connection until the pool's timeout and fails. TWR on every connection, statement and result set.
  • Wrapped resources that leak. In try (var in = new BufferedInputStream(new FileInputStream(f))), if the wrapper's constructor throws, the inner stream was never registered as a resource and stays open. Declare each layer as its own resource.
  • Cleanup that throws. A finally { conn.rollback(); } that throws replaces the exception that explains why you were rolling back. Log or suppress secondary failures; let the primary one propagate.

Practice · 50 min

  1. Run parsePositive on "5", "-3" and "abc" and check the output above. Swap the two catch clauses and read the error.
  2. Compile swallow with -Xlint:finally. Then write a caller that logs the exception it expects and observe that nothing is logged.
  3. Vary Resources: make both resources fail on close; make only the body succeed; open a third resource. Predict the output each time before running it.
  4. Rewrite a given three-resource legacy method (open connection, prepare statement, read result) into a single TWR statement. Write a test where the statement's close() fails and assert the primary exception and its suppressed list.
  5. Call System.exit(0) inside a try with a finally that prints, then register a shutdown hook with Runtime.getRuntime().addShutdownHook. Which one prints?

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Item 9 · JLS §14.20 (the try statement), §14.20.2 (try-finally), §14.20.3 (try-with-resources) · Javadoc: AutoCloseable, java.io.Closeable, Throwable.addSuppressed

L53 · DAY 53

throw, throws, Custom Exceptions & Exception Design

45m video45m practice

Exceptions are part of an API, so they need design as much as method signatures do: a small hierarchy in your domain's vocabulary, precise failure information, causes that are never dropped, and exactly one place that logs and responds. Get those right and a production failure explains itself.

throw vs throws, and propagating checked exceptions

throw expr; is a statement: it throws the Throwable object now (throw null throws a NullPointerException). throws X is part of a method's signature: it declares the checked exceptions the method may let escape, which every caller must then catch or declare in turn (L51). Declaring is the right choice when this method can't do anything useful about the failure but a caller can:

static String loadCover(Path dir, String isbn) throws IOException {
    return Files.readString(dir.resolve(isbn + ".txt"));    // propagate, don't hide
}

Declare the specific types (throws IOException), never throws Exception, which forces every caller to catch everything. Effective Java Item 74: document every exception with a Javadoc @throws tag, but declare only the checked ones.

Overriding and narrowing (L36): an override or interface implementation may throw the same checked exceptions, narrower ones, or none, but never new or broader ones. So an implementation of BookDao.query(...) throws SQLException may declare nothing, and code holding the implementation's type directly then needs no catch.

Custom exceptions and their four constructors

public class CatalogUnavailableException extends Exception {     // checked
    public CatalogUnavailableException() { super(); }
    public CatalogUnavailableException(String message) { super(message); }
    public CatalogUnavailableException(String message, Throwable cause) {
        super(message, cause);
    }
    public CatalogUnavailableException(Throwable cause) { super(cause); } // message
}                                                                // = cause.toString()

These four mirror Exception's own public constructors, so a general-purpose exception can be created, and chained, in every way a caller expects. Throwable also has a protected fifth constructor, (message, cause, enableSuppression, writableStackTrace), covered under performance below. A domain exception should carry more than a string: the IDs and values that explain the failure, as fields with accessors (Item 75, "include failure-capture information"), and never secrets.

Checked or unchecked for your domain?

SituationUse
Caller passed a bad valueIllegalArgumentException (or NullPointerException via requireNonNull)
Call is invalid in the object's current stateIllegalStateException
Operation not supported by this implementationUnsupportedOperationException
Business rule outcome reported to clients: not found, limit reached, conflictcustom unchecked exception in a domain hierarchy, or a Result type (L48) when it is an ordinary branch of the flow
Infrastructure failure: database down, timeouttranslate into an unchecked infrastructure exception, keeping the cause
A library API whose callers can and must recover (retry, fall back)checked, sparingly (Item 71)

Fail fast with the standard exceptions (Item 72) at the top of public methods: validate arguments and state before doing any work, so the failure surfaces at the line that caused it, with a message naming the bad value. The rule for choosing between the two: if no argument value could have worked in the current state, it is IllegalStateException; otherwise IllegalArgumentException.

private volatile boolean closedForInventory;

void lend(long memberId, String isbn, int days) {
    Objects.requireNonNull(isbn, "isbn");
    if (days < 1 || days > 60)
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("days must be 1..60, was " + days);
    if (closedForInventory)
        throw new IllegalStateException("library closed for inventory");
    // ... the actual work
}

Designing an exception hierarchy for a backend service

abstract class LibraryException extends RuntimeException {   // one file each
    private final String code;                                  // in a real project
    protected LibraryException(String code, String message, Throwable cause) {
        super(message, cause);
        this.code = code;
    }
    public String code() { return code; }               // stable, machine-readable
}

class NotFoundException extends LibraryException {
    NotFoundException(String what, Object id) {
        super("NOT_FOUND", what + " " + id + " not found", null);
    }
}

class LoanLimitExceededException extends LibraryException {
    private final long memberId;
    private final int limit;
    LoanLimitExceededException(long memberId, int limit) {
        super("LOAN_LIMIT", "member " + memberId + " at limit " + limit, null);
        this.memberId = memberId;
        this.limit = limit;
    }
    long memberId() { return memberId; }
    int limit()     { return limit; }
}

class StorageException extends LibraryException {             // infrastructure
    StorageException(String message, Throwable cause) {
        super("STORAGE", message, cause);
    }
}

Translation, chaining, and never losing the cause

import java.sql.SQLException;

interface BookDao { Book query(String isbn) throws SQLException; }
record Book(String isbn, String title) {}

final class BookRepository {
    private final BookDao dao;
    BookRepository(BookDao dao) { this.dao = dao; }

    Book byIsbn(String isbn) {
        try {
            Book b = dao.query(isbn);
            if (b == null) throw new NotFoundException("book", isbn);
            return b;
        } catch (SQLException e) {                    // low-level and checked
            throw new StorageException("lookup failed: " + isbn, e);  // keep e!
        }
    }
}
Definition · translation and chaining

Exception translation (Item 73): a layer catches lower-level exceptions and throws ones in its own vocabulary, so callers of BookRepository never see JDBC types. Chaining passes the low-level exception as the new one's cause (constructor or initCause), so the trace still ends in Caused by: java.sql.SQLException .... Translating without the cause turns a five-minute diagnosis into an afternoon.

  • Never swallow. catch (Exception e) {} converts a failure into silent wrong behaviour (Item 77). If ignoring really is correct, name the variable ignored and write a comment saying why.
  • Log or rethrow, not both. Logging at every layer and rethrowing prints the same trace three or four times per failure, which hides the signal and inflates log bills. Wrap with context as it travels up; log once, where it is finally handled.

The performance cost of exceptions

Creating a Throwable calls fillInStackTrace(), which walks the thread's stack, so the cost grows with stack depth, and framework stacks are deep. In a rough laptop measurement, creating one took about 3 µs at a depth of 10 frames and over 20 µs at 200, and over ten times less with the stack trace disabled. The capture, not the throw, is the cost. Three consequences:

  • Don't use exceptions for ordinary control flow in hot paths (Item 69). "Not in cache" is a return value, not an exception.
  • For high-volume expected exceptions, the protected constructor super(message, null, false, false) (or overriding fillInStackTrace to return this) skips the capture. Use it rarely: you lose the trace.
  • HotSpot does this automatically for implicit exceptions such as NullPointerException: after a site has thrown many times (thousands, in a quick test), compiled code may throw a preallocated instance with no stack trace. For bare java.lang.NullPointerException lines in production logs, find the first occurrence, or run with -XX:-OmitStackTraceInFastThrow.
What breaks in production
  • Duplicate logs from log-and-rethrow make one failure look like four and drown real alerts.
  • Lost root causes from throw new XException(e.getMessage()): the message survives, the stack and the cause don't.
  • Leaky messages. An exception message containing an email address or token travels into logs, error trackers and sometimes API responses. Put IDs in messages, not personal data.

Practice · 45 min

  1. Build the LibraryException hierarchy, make it sealed (L48), and write an exhaustive switch mapping each subclass to an HTTP status code.
  2. Test BookRepository with a fake BookDao that throws SQLException: assert the thrown type, the message, and that getCause() is the original.
  3. Take a three-layer call chain that logs and rethrows at every layer; fix it so the failure is logged exactly once with full context.
  4. Measure exception creation at stack depths 10 and 200, with and without writableStackTrace. Treat the numbers as rough (L159 explains why naive timing lies).
  5. Throw an implicit NPE from the same line 100,000 times and record when the stack trace disappears; rerun with -XX:-OmitStackTraceInFastThrow.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 69–77 · JLS §14.18 (the throw statement), §8.4.6 (method throws), §11.2 · Javadoc: Throwable constructors and fillInStackTrace

L54 · DAY 54

Wrapper Classes, Integer Cache & Parsing

40m video40m practice

Each primitive has a wrapper class so that numbers can live where only objects are allowed: in collections, generics and nullable fields. Autoboxing hides the conversion, but not its consequences: identity comparisons that work only for small numbers, nulls that explode when unboxed, and objects where you expected bare numbers.

The eight wrappers

PrimitiveWrapperBoxing cacheUseful statics
byte, shortByte, Short-128 to 127parseByte, toUnsignedInt
intInteger-128 to 127 (upper bound tunable)parseInt, compare, sum, bitCount, toBinaryString
longLong-128 to 127parseLong, compare, numberOfTrailingZeros
float, doubleFloat, DoublenoneparseDouble, compare, isNaN, isFinite
charCharacter0 to 127isDigit, isLetter, getNumericValue (L11)
booleanBooleanTRUE, FALSEparseBoolean, logicalXor

All eight are final and immutable; the six numeric ones extend Number, which offers intValue(), longValue(), doubleValue() and so on. Each defines MIN_VALUE and MAX_VALUE, with two traps: Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE) is still -2147483648, because the positive counterpart doesn't fit in an int (L9), and Double.MIN_VALUE (4.9E-324) is the smallest positive double, not the most negative one, which is -Double.MAX_VALUE.

What autoboxing compiles to

Integer boxed = 42;        // javac emits: Integer.valueOf(42)
int raw = boxed;           // javac emits: boxed.intValue()
boxed++;                   // boxed = Integer.valueOf(boxed.intValue() + 1)
Integer missing = null;
int n = missing;           // missing.intValue()  -> NullPointerException

Boxing is a call to valueOf and unboxing a call to xxxValue(), inserted silently by the compiler (JLS §5.1.7–5.1.8). That explains every behaviour below: boxed++ allocates or fetches a new object because wrappers are immutable, and unboxing null is a method call on null.

The Integer cache and the == trap

Definition · the boxing cache

Integer.valueOf, which autoboxing calls, is documented to always return a cached instance for values from -128 to 127, held in a preallocated array; Short, Byte and Long do the same, Character caches 0 to 127, and Boolean has just TRUE and FALSE (JLS §5.1.7 requires this sharing for boxed constants). The upper bound for Integer can be raised with -XX:AutoBoxCacheMax=N. Other values produce a new object on each boxing.

Integer a = 127, b = 127, c = 128, d = 128;
System.out.println(a == b);              // true:  same cached object
System.out.println(c == d);              // false: two different objects
System.out.println(c.equals(d));         // true:  compare values with equals
System.out.println(c.intValue() == d);   // true:  a primitive side unboxes d
Long big = 127L;
System.out.println(big.equals(127));     // false: 127 boxes to an Integer, not a Long
System.out.println(big.equals(127L));    // true

Code that compares wrappers with == passes every test that uses small IDs and fails in production as soon as an ID exceeds 127. Compare with equals or Objects.equals, or unbox explicitly. equals also checks the type, hence big.equals(127) being false: a classic with Map<Long, ...> looked up with an int key, which always misses.

valueOf vs new Integer. The wrapper constructors are deprecated (since Java 9) and marked for removal (since Java 16, JEP 390, which also labels the wrappers value-based classes whose identity you must not rely on). They always allocated a new object and bypassed the cache. Use Integer.valueOf, or let autoboxing call it.

Parsing: parseInt, valueOf, radix, and errors

jshell> Integer.parseInt("42")              // primitive int
$1 ==> 42
jshell> Integer.valueOf("42")               // Integer, via the cache
$2 ==> 42
jshell> Integer.parseInt("-ff", 16)         // radix 2..36
$3 ==> -255
jshell> Integer.parseInt("+42")
$4 ==> 42
jshell> Integer.parseUnsignedInt("4294967295")
$5 ==> -1                                   // the same 32 bits as 0xFFFFFFFF
jshell> Integer.toString(255, 2) + " " + Integer.toHexString(255)
$6 ==> "11111111 ff"
jshell> Long.parseLong("777", 8)
$7 ==> 511
jshell> Integer.parseInt(" 42")
|  Exception java.lang.NumberFormatException: For input string: " 42"
jshell> Integer.parseInt("2147483648")
|  Exception java.lang.NumberFormatException: For input string: "2147483648"

parseInt returns an int; valueOf returns an Integer. Both accept an optional sign and digits in the given radix, and nothing else: no whitespace, no thousands separators, no decimal point, and nothing outside the type's range. All failures, including null and the empty string, throw NumberFormatException, which is unchecked (it extends IllegalArgumentException), so the compiler never reminds you. Every parse of external input needs a decision about what happens on failure:

static OptionalInt parseCopies(String raw) {
    if (raw == null || raw.isBlank()) return OptionalInt.empty();
    try {
        return OptionalInt.of(Integer.parseInt(raw.strip()));
    } catch (NumberFormatException e) {      // "3 copies", "1,000", "9999999999"
        return OptionalInt.empty();
    }
}

Boolean.parseBoolean quirks. It returns true only for the string "true", ignoring case. Everything else, including "yes", "1", "on", " true" with a leading space, and null, returns false, and it never throws. A misspelled configuration flag therefore silently means false. Validate boolean input against an explicit set of accepted values when the difference matters.

Wrappers in collections, and the boxing cost

Collections and generics hold only references (L72), so List<Integer> is the price of putting numbers in a collection. It is usually fine; it is not fine in hot loops. Effective Java Item 6's example declares the accumulator as Long sum = 0L and adds about two billion longs to it: every sum += i unboxes, adds and boxes a new Long, which is several times slower than with a primitive long. Item 61: prefer primitive types to boxed primitives. Use int[], primitive streams (L94) or Integer.sum-style statics where volume matters.

Two boxing bugs live in collections. list.remove(1) on a List<Integer> calls remove(int index), not remove(Object), because overload resolution prefers no boxing (L27); write list.remove(Integer.valueOf(1)) to remove the value. And int count = counts.get(key) on a Map<String, Integer> throws NullPointerException for a missing key; use getOrDefault(key, 0).

What breaks in production
  • == on wrappers. if (order.getUserId() == currentUserId) with two Longs works for users 1 to 127 and fails for user 128. An authorisation check written this way is a security bug.
  • Unboxing in a ternary. Integer x = flag ? map.get(k) : 0; has type int (one branch is primitive), so a null from map.get is unboxed and throws a NullPointerException on a line that seems to contain no dereference.
  • Nullable wrapper fields. A JPA entity or DTO with Integer quantity receives null from the database or JSON, and the first arithmetic on it fails. Decide whether the field may be absent; if not, use a primitive or validate at the boundary.

Practice · 40 min

  1. Run the == snippet, then run it again with -XX:AutoBoxCacheMax=1000 and explain the change.
  2. Write parseCopies and test it on "3", " 3 ", "", null, "1,000", "3.0", "-0", "2147483648" and "+7".
  3. Write parseFlag(String) that accepts true/false/yes/no/1/0 in any case and throws IllegalArgumentException for anything else. Compare with Boolean.parseBoolean on the same inputs.
  4. Sum 100 million numbers with a Long accumulator and with a long, timing both with System.nanoTime (results are rough; L159 explains why).
  5. On a List<Integer> of 10, 20, 30, call remove(1) and remove(Integer.valueOf(10)) and explain both results using L27's resolution rules.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 6, 61 · JLS §5.1.7 (boxing conversion), §5.1.8 (unboxing conversion) · JEP 390, “Warnings for Value-Based Classes” · Javadoc: Integer, Long, Boolean, Number

L55 · DAY 55

java.lang Toolbox + Phase 1 Capstone

55m video120m practicemilestone project

A handful of JDK classes cover most everyday needs, each with one trap worth knowing. Then the phase ends by putting fifty lessons to work in one program: a console Library Management System in plain Java, no frameworks.

Math: rounding, powers and exact arithmetic

System.out.println(Math.round(2.5) + " " + Math.round(-2.5)); // 3 -2
System.out.println(Math.rint(2.5) + " " + Math.floorDiv(-7, 2)); // 2.0 -4
System.out.println(Math.pow(2, 10) + " " + Math.sqrt(2.0));    // 1024.0 1.414...
try {
    Math.multiplyExact(1_000_000, 1_000_000);        // * would wrap silently
} catch (ArithmeticException e) {
    System.out.println(e.getMessage());              // integer overflow
}

Math.round is floor(x + 0.5), so -2.5 rounds to -2; rint rounds halves to even; floorDiv/floorMod round towards negative infinity (-7 / 2 is -3). None is for money: use BigDecimal (L10). pow, sqrt, min/max and Math.random() (a double in [0, 1)) do what they say. The *Exact methods (addExact, toIntExact, absExact, ...) throw instead of overflowing.

Random numbers and UUIDs

GeneratorPropertiesUse for
Random48-bit linear congruential, seedable, thread-safe but contendedreproducible tests: new Random(42)
ThreadLocalRandomper thread, no contention, not seedableconcurrent server code: ThreadLocalRandom.current().nextInt(1, 7)
SecureRandomcryptographically strong, OS-seeded, sloweranything an attacker must not guess
 
byte[] bytes = new byte[32];
new SecureRandom().nextBytes(bytes);
String resetToken = HexFormat.of().formatHex(bytes);        // Java 17+

UUID id = UUID.randomUUID();                                // version 4
byte[] name = "isbn:9780441013593".getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8);
UUID same = UUID.nameUUIDFromBytes(name);                   // version 3
System.out.println(id.version() + " " + same.version());    // 4 3

A UUID is a 128-bit identifier unique without coordination. randomUUID() is version 4 (122 random bits from a SecureRandom); nameUUIDFromBytes is version 3, an MD5 of the input, so a name always maps to the same UUID. Random keys scatter database index inserts, so time-ordered version 7 (RFC 9562) is popular for primary keys; as of Java 25 the JDK has no v7 factory.

System, Runtime and a first look at Optional

Definition · two clocks

System.currentTimeMillis() is wall-clock time since the epoch: right for timestamps, but it can jump backwards or forwards when the clock is adjusted. System.nanoTime() is monotonic with an arbitrary origin: meaningless as a date, correct for measuring durations and timeouts.

System.getenv("DB_URL") reads environment variables (how containers pass configuration); getProperty reads JVM properties, including -Dkey=value; lineSeparator() is the OS newline; arraycopy is a native copy that handles overlap. Runtime.getRuntime().availableProcessors() is container-aware since JDK 10; maxMemory() is the heap limit and totalMemory() - freeMemory() the heap in use. Optional<Book> findBook(Isbn) puts possible absence in the type and makes the caller choose orElse, orElseThrow or isEmpty(); return it, don't store it, never call get() blind (Item 55, L96).

Capstone: the Library Management System

Requirements: books with several copies; members whose tier limits their loans; lending with a 21-day due date and returns with a per-day late fine; search by title or author; and a console menu that never crashes on bad input.

Plan · build order
  1. Value objects that reject bad input: Isbn, Money.
  2. Enums for fixed data (Tier) and derived state (LoanStatus).
  3. Loan, the only mutable object, guarding its own transitions.
  4. The Library service: injected Clock, an exception per rule.
  5. The console last: a thin shell that parses, calls and reports. Bad ISBNs, non-numeric IDs (NumberFormatException, L54) and double returns all land in its multi-catch (L52).
record Isbn(String value) implements Comparable<Isbn> {
    Isbn {
        value = value.replace("-", "").strip();
        if (!value.matches("\\d{13}"))
            throw new IllegalArgumentException("bad ISBN: " + value);
    }
    public int compareTo(Isbn o) { return value.compareTo(o.value); }
}
record Money(long cents) {
    Money { if (cents < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("negative: " + cents); }
    Money times(long n) { return new Money(Math.multiplyExact(cents, n)); }
    public String toString() { return "%d.%02d".formatted(cents / 100, cents % 100); }
}
enum Tier {
    STANDARD(3, 25), PREMIUM(8, 10);                 // max loans, fee per day late
    final int maxLoans;
    final long feePerDay;
    Tier(int max, long fee) { maxLoans = max; feePerDay = fee; }
}
record Book(Isbn isbn, String title, String author, int year)
        implements Comparable<Book> {
    static final Comparator<Book> ORDER =
            Comparator.comparing(Book::title).thenComparing(Book::isbn);
    public int compareTo(Book o) { return ORDER.compare(this, o); }
}
record Member(long id, String name, Tier tier) {}

class LibraryException extends RuntimeException {
    LibraryException(String message) { super(message); }
}
class NotFoundException extends LibraryException {
    NotFoundException(String what) { super(what + " not found"); }
}
class LoanLimitException extends LibraryException {
    LoanLimitException(Member m) { super(m.name() + " is at the loan limit"); }
}
class NoCopyException extends LibraryException {
    NoCopyException(Book b) { super("no copy of " + b.title() + " left"); }
}
enum LoanStatus { ACTIVE, OVERDUE, RETURNED }

final class Loan {                                   // identity + lifecycle
    final long id, memberId;
    final Isbn isbn;
    final LocalDate due;
    private LocalDate returnedOn;                    // null while on loan

    Loan(long id, long memberId, Isbn isbn, LocalDate due) {
        this.id = id; this.memberId = memberId; this.isbn = isbn; this.due = due;
    }
    LoanStatus status(LocalDate today) {
        if (returnedOn != null) return LoanStatus.RETURNED;
        return today.isAfter(due) ? LoanStatus.OVERDUE : LoanStatus.ACTIVE;
    }
    long returnOn(LocalDate day) {                   // returns days late
        if (returnedOn != null)
            throw new IllegalStateException("loan " + id + " returned");
        returnedOn = day;
        return Math.max(0, ChronoUnit.DAYS.between(due, day));
    }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) { return o instanceof Loan l && l.id == id; }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return Long.hashCode(id); }
    @Override public String toString() { return "loan " + id + ", due " + due; }
}

final class Library {
    private final Map<Isbn, Book> books = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<Isbn, Integer> free = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<Long, Member> members = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<Long, Loan> loans = new LinkedHashMap<>();
    private final Clock clock;                       // Clock.fixed(...) in tests
    private long nextId = 1;

    Library(Clock clock) { this.clock = Objects.requireNonNull(clock); }

    void addBook(Book b, int copies) {
        books.put(b.isbn(), b);
        free.merge(b.isbn(), copies, Integer::sum);
    }
    void addMember(Member m) { members.put(m.id(), m); }
    Optional<Book> findBook(Isbn isbn) { return Optional.ofNullable(books.get(isbn)); }

    Loan lend(long memberId, Isbn isbn) {
        Member m = members.get(memberId);
        if (m == null) throw new NotFoundException("member " + memberId);
        Book b = findBook(isbn)
                .orElseThrow(() -> new NotFoundException("book " + isbn.value()));
        long open = 0;
        for (Loan l : loans.values())
            if (l.memberId == memberId
                    && l.status(today()) != LoanStatus.RETURNED) open++;
        if (open >= m.tier().maxLoans) throw new LoanLimitException(m);
        if (free.get(isbn) == 0) throw new NoCopyException(b);
        free.merge(isbn, -1, Integer::sum);
        var loan = new Loan(nextId++, memberId, isbn, today().plusDays(21));
        loans.put(loan.id, loan);
        return loan;
    }

    Money giveBack(long loanId) {                    // returns the fine due
        Loan loan = loans.get(loanId);
        if (loan == null) throw new NotFoundException("loan " + loanId);
        long daysLate = loan.returnOn(today());
        free.merge(loan.isbn, 1, Integer::sum);
        return new Money(members.get(loan.memberId).tier().feePerDay).times(daysLate);
    }

    private LocalDate today() { return LocalDate.now(clock); }
}
public class LibraryApp {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        var lib = new Library(Clock.systemDefaultZone());
        var dune = new Book(new Isbn("978-0441013593"), "Dune", "Frank Herbert", 1965);
        lib.addBook(dune, 2);
        lib.addMember(new Member(1, "Ada", Tier.STANDARD));
        var in = new Scanner(System.in);
        while (true) {
            System.out.print("1) lend  2) return  0) quit > ");
            if (!in.hasNextLine()) return;                 // end of input
            try {
                switch (in.nextLine().strip()) {
                    case "1" -> System.out.println(lib.lend(
                            Long.parseLong(ask(in, "member")), new Isbn(ask(in, "isbn"))));
                    case "2" -> System.out.println("fine: "
                            + lib.giveBack(Long.parseLong(ask(in, "loan"))));
                    case "0" -> { return; }
                    default  -> System.out.println("unknown option");
                }
            } catch (LibraryException | IllegalArgumentException
                     | IllegalStateException e) {
                System.out.println("! " + e.getMessage());  // report, keep going
            }
        }
    }
    static String ask(Scanner in, String prompt) {
        System.out.print(prompt + ": ");
        if (!in.hasNextLine()) throw new IllegalStateException("input closed");
        return in.nextLine();
    }
}
What breaks in production
  • Durations from currentTimeMillis go negative when NTP adjusts the clock, and timeouts built on them misfire. Use nanoTime, or Instant from an injected Clock.
  • Predictable tokens. Reset codes from Random or Math.random() can be predicted from a few observed outputs. Use SecureRandom.
  • State that only grows. Every loan kept in a map forever is fine in a console and a leak in a server; Phase 9 moves it to a database.

Practice · 120 min

  • Split the reference code into packages catalog, members, loans; make everything package-private that can be.
  • Menu options: add books and copies, register members, a member's loans with LoanStatus, all overdue loans.
  • Search by title or author, case-insensitive, in natural order, plus a newest-first Comparator; a Genre enum filter.
  • Fines capped at a replacement price; members owing over 10.00 can't borrow (a new exception subclass).
  • Tests with Clock.fixed/offset: loan limit, no copies, unknown member or ISBN, 10 days late, double return, Loan equality in a HashSet.
  • No input (non-numeric, blank, bad ISBN, end of input) ever prints a stack trace.
  • A public loan reference from UUID.randomUUID().
  • Stretch: save and load state as CSV with java.nio.file.Files.
  • Commit with a README: what is a record, an enum or a class, and why.

Read Bloch, Effective Java (3rd ed.), Items 55, 59, 60 · Javadoc: Math, ThreadLocalRandom, SecureRandom, UUID, System, Runtime, Optional · RFC 9562, “Universally Unique IDentifiers (UUIDs)”
